Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from typing import List, Dict
from qiskit.circuit.random import random_circuit
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler, SamplerOptions
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def run_multiple_sampler() -> List[Dict[str, int]]:
    """
    Generate two random quantum circuits, each with 2 qubits and a depth of 2,
    using seed values of 0 and 1 respectively. Run the circuits using the Sampler
    on the Aer simulator with the seed set as 42 and return the counts for both circuits.
    """
    num_qubits = 2
    depth = 2
    seeds = [0, 1]

    # Generate two random circuits with specified seeds
    circuits = []
    for seed in seeds:
        qc = random_circuit(num_qubits, depth, max_operands=2, seed=seed)
        # Add measurement on all qubits
        qc.measure_all()
        circuits.append(qc)

    # Initialize AerSimulator with seed_simulator=42 for reproducibility
    simulator = AerSimulator(seed_simulator=42)

    # Initialize Sampler with the AerSimulator backend and seed=42
    sampler = Sampler(
        backend=simulator,
        options=SamplerOptions(seed=42)
    )

    counts_list = []
    for circuit in circuits:
        # Run the sampler on the circuit
        result = sampler.run(circuit).result()

        # The result quasi-probabilities are in result.quasi_dists
        # Convert quasi-probabilities to counts by scaling with shots
        # Default shots is 1024 in Sampler, but we can specify shots explicitly if needed
        quasi_dist = result.quasi_dists[0]

        # Convert quasi-probabilities to counts by multiplying by shots
        shots = 1024
        counts = {bitstr: int(round(prob * shots)) for bitstr, prob in quasi_dist.items()}

        counts_list.append(counts)

    return counts_list
```

from typing import Dict, Optional, Tuple
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Batch, Sampler
from qiskit.primitives.primitive_job import PrimitiveJob

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
def run_jobs_on_batch() -> Tuple[Dict[str, PrimitiveJob], Optional[str]]:
    """ Generate all four Bell states and execute them on the Fake Cairo V2 backend using SamplerV2 with batch mode. Each Bell state circuit will be transpiled with an optimization level of 3, with the seed 123 for the transpiler.
    Returns a dictionary where the keys are the Bell state names ['phi_plus', 'phi_minus', 'psi_plus', 'psi_minus'] and the values are the corresponding RuntimeJob objects and the batch id.

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `run_jobs_on_batch()` that generates all four Bell states, transpiles them, and runs them in a batch on the Fake Cairo V2 backend using SamplerV2:

---

### 1. Circuit Preparation

- **Number of Qubits:** 2 qubits per circuit (Bell states require 2 qubits).
- **Bell States to Generate:**
  - **phi_plus**  = (|00⟩ + |11⟩) / √2
  - **phi_minus** = (|00⟩ - |11⟩) / √2
  - **psi_plus**  = (|01⟩ + |10⟩) / √2
  - **psi_minus** = (|01⟩ - |10⟩) / √2

- **Quantum Gates for Bell States:**
  - Start with |00⟩ (default initial state)
  - Apply Hadamard (H) gate to qubit 0
  - Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
  - For **phi_minus** and **psi_minus**, apply appropriate Z or X gates or phase flips to create the minus sign:
    - phi_minus: Apply Z gate on qubit 0 or qubit 1 after CNOT
    - psi_plus: Apply X gate on qubit 1 after CNOT
    - psi_minus: Apply X and Z gates or equivalent phase to get the minus sign

---

### 2. Transpilation

- Use the `generate_preset_pass_manager` with:
  - `optimization_level=3`
  - `seed_transpiler=123`
- Transpile each Bell state circuit for the Fake Cairo V2 backend with the above settings.

---

- Use the FakeCairoV2 backend as the target backend.
- Initialize a `Sampler` instance from `qiskit_ibm_runtime` using this backend.
- Use batch mode of the Sampler to submit all four circuits at once.

---

### 4. Batch Execution

- Prepare a batch request containing the four transpiled circuits.
- Submit this batch to the Sampler.
- The batch submission will return a dictionary mapping each circuit name ('phi_plus', 'phi_minus', 'psi_plus', 'psi_minus') to its corresponding `PrimitiveJob` object.
- Also obtain the batch ID from the batch submission response.

---

### 5. Measurement Strategy

- Each circuit ends with implicit measurement since Sampler operates at the primitive level.
- The Sampler runs circuits in the computational basis by default.
- No explicit measurement gates need to be appended since Sampler handles measurement internally.

---

### 6. Expected Outcome

- The function returns:
  - A dictionary with keys: `'phi_plus'`, `'phi_minus'`, `'psi_plus'`, and `'psi_minus'`
  - Values are the corresponding `PrimitiveJob` objects from the batch run.
  - The batch ID string (can be used to track the batch job).
- The results, when fetched from these jobs, will correspond to the expected Bell state measurement distributions.

---

### 7. Acceptance Criteria

- All four Bell state circuits are correctly constructed.
- Circuits are transpiled with optimization level 3 and seed 123.
- Sampler batch mode is used to submit all four circuits simultaneously on FakeCairoV2.
- The function returns the correct mapping of Bell state names to their respective `PrimitiveJob` objects.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from typing import Dict, Optional, Tuple
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Batch, Sampler
from qiskit.primitives.primitive_job import PrimitiveJob
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager

def run_jobs_on_batch() -> Tuple[Dict[str, PrimitiveJob], Optional[str]]:
    """
    Generate all four Bell states and execute them on the Fake Cairo V2 backend using Sampler with batch mode.
    Each Bell state circuit is transpiled with optimization level 3 and seed 123.
    
    Returns:
        Tuple containing:
            - Dictionary mapping Bell state names to their corresponding PrimitiveJob objects.
            - Batch ID string for tracking the batch job.
    """
    # Define Bell state circuits
    bell_circuits = {}

    # phi_plus = (|00> + |11>)/sqrt(2)
    phi_plus = QuantumCircuit(2)
    phi_plus.h(0)
    phi_plus.cx(0, 1)
    bell_circuits['phi_plus'] = phi_plus

    # phi_minus = (|00> - |11>)/sqrt(2)
    phi_minus = QuantumCircuit(2)
    phi_minus.h(0)
    phi_minus.cx(0, 1)
    # Apply Z gate on qubit 0 to introduce the minus sign
    phi_minus.z(0)
    bell_circuits['phi_minus'] = phi_minus

    # psi_plus = (|01> + |10>)/sqrt(2)
    psi_plus = QuantumCircuit(2)
    psi_plus.h(0)
    psi_plus.cx(0, 1)
    # Apply X gate on qubit 1 to flip target qubit
    psi_plus.x(1)
    bell_circuits['psi_plus'] = psi_plus

    # psi_minus = (|01> - |10>)/sqrt(2)
    psi_minus = QuantumCircuit(2)
    psi_minus.h(0)
    psi_minus.cx(0, 1)
    # Apply X gate on qubit 1 and Z gate on qubit 0 to get the minus sign
    psi_minus.x(1)
    psi_minus.z(0)
    bell_circuits['psi_minus'] = psi_minus

    # Initialize the Fake Cairo V2 backend
    backend = FakeCairoV2()

    # Generate the preset pass manager for transpilation with optimization level 3 and seed 123
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(optimization_level=3, seed_transpiler=123)

    # Transpile each circuit with the pass manager for the backend
    transpiled_circuits = {}
    for name, circuit in bell_circuits.items():
        transpiled_circuits[name] = pass_manager.run(circuit, backend=backend)

    # Initialize the Sampler with the Fake Cairo V2 backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Prepare the batch of circuits to run
    batch = Batch(transpiled_circuits)

    # Run the batch on the sampler
    batch_result = sampler.run_batch(batch)

    # Extract the dictionary of jobs and the batch id
    jobs_dict = batch_result.jobs
    batch_id = batch_result.batch_id

    return jobs_dict, batch_id
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import EfficientSU2
from qiskit.quantum_info import SparsePauliOp
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Estimator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.primitive_job import PrimitiveJob
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager

def run_circuit_with_dd_trex() -> PrimitiveJob:
    """
    Run an EfficientSU2 circuit with 5 qubits, 2 repetitions, and pairwise entanglement on the Fake Cairo V2 backend.
    The circuit is transpiled with optimization level 1 and transpiler seed 789.
    Uses 1*Z_0 observable to calculate the expectation value.
    Returns the PrimitiveJob representing the submitted job.
    """
    # Create EfficientSU2 ansatz with 5 qubits, 2 repetitions, pairwise entanglement
    circuit = EfficientSU2(num_qubits=5, reps=2, entanglement='pairwise')

    # Define observable: 1 * Z on qubit 0 (leftmost qubit is qubit 0)
    # Qiskit qubit ordering: qubit 0 is leftmost in string representation
    observable = SparsePauliOp.from_list([("ZIIII", 1)])

    # Select Fake Cairo V2 backend
    backend = FakeCairoV2()

    # Generate preset pass manager with optimization level 1 and seed 789
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(
        optimization_level=1,
        seed_transpiler=789,
        coupling_map=backend.configuration().coupling_map,
        basis_gates=backend.configuration().basis_gates,
    )

    # Transpile the circuit with the pass manager
    transpiled_circuit = pass_manager.run(circuit)

    # Initialize Estimator primitive with the fake backend
    estimator = Estimator(session=backend)

    # Run the estimator job asynchronously to get the expectation value
    job = estimator.run(circuits=transpiled_circuit, observables=observable)

    # Return the PrimitiveJob object
    return job
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import EfficientSU2
from qiskit.quantum_info import SparsePauliOp
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Estimator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager

def run_circuit_with_dd_trex() -> Estimator.job_type:
    """
    Run an EfficientSU2 circuit with 5 qubits, 2 repetitions, and pairwise entanglement on the Fake Cairo V2 backend.
    The circuit is transpiled with optimization level 1 and transpiler seed 789.
    Uses 1*Z_0 observable to calculate the expectation value.
    Returns the PrimitiveJob representing the submitted job.
    """
    # Create EfficientSU2 ansatz with 5 qubits, 2 repetitions, pairwise entanglement
    circuit = EfficientSU2(num_qubits=5, reps=2, entanglement='pairwise')

    # Define observable: 1 * Z on qubit 0 (leftmost qubit is qubit 0)
    # Qiskit qubit ordering: qubit 0 is leftmost in string representation
    observable = SparsePauliOp.from_list([("ZIIII", 1)])

    # Select Fake Cairo V2 backend
    backend = FakeCairoV2()

    # Generate preset pass manager with optimization level 1 and seed 789
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(
        optimization_level=1,
        seed_transpiler=789,
        coupling_map=backend.configuration().coupling_map,
        basis_gates=backend.configuration().basis_gates,
    )

    # Transpile the circuit with the pass manager
    transpiled_circuit = pass_manager.run(circuit)

    # Initialize Estimator primitive with the fake backend
    estimator = Estimator(session=backend)

    # Run the estimator job asynchronously to get the expectation value

    # Return the PrimitiveJob object
    return job
```

=== [37/151] qiskitHumanEval/36 :: bv_function ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
def bv_function(s: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Write a function to design a Bernstein-Vazirani oracle from a bitstring and return it.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the Bernstein-Vazirani (BV) oracle function in Qiskit:

1. **Input and Initialization**
   - Input: A bitstring `s` of length `n` representing the hidden string in the BV problem.
   - Initialize a quantum circuit with `n+1` qubits:
     - The first `n` qubits are the "input" qubits.
     - The last qubit is the "output" or ancilla qubit.

2. **Ancilla Qubit Preparation**
   - Apply an X gate to the ancilla qubit (the last qubit) to flip it to state |1⟩.
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to the ancilla qubit to create a superposition: |−⟩ = (|0⟩ - |1⟩)/√2.

3. **Input Qubits Preparation**
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to each of the `n` input qubits to create an equal superposition over all possible inputs.

4. **Oracle Construction**
   - For each bit `s[i]` in the input string `s`:
     - If `s[i]` == '1', apply a Controlled-NOT (CNOT) gate with control qubit `i` (input qubit) and target qubit the ancilla (last qubit).
     - If `s[i]` == '0', do nothing to that qubit.
   - This implements the oracle Uf such that Uf|x⟩|y⟩ = |x⟩|y ⊕ (s·x)⟩, where s·x is the bitwise inner product modulo 2.

5. **Final Hadamards**
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to each of the `n` input qubits again.
   - No operation is needed on the ancilla qubit after this step for the oracle construction itself, but typically the circuit that uses this oracle applies measurements after these steps.

6. **Measurement**
   - The oracle function itself typically does not include the measurement step, as it is part of the overall BV algorithm.
   - If desired, measurements can be added externally after the oracle is applied.

7. **Expected Outcome**
   - The oracle flips the phase of the states according to the hidden bitstring `s`.
   - When used in the full BV algorithm, this oracle allows the hidden string `s` to be determined with a single query.

**Acceptance Criteria**
- The returned QuantumCircuit:
  - Has `n+1` qubits (input + ancilla).
  - Prepares the ancilla qubit in |−⟩.
  - Applies Hadamard gates to the input qubits before and after the oracle operation.
  - Applies CNOT gates controlled by input qubits corresponding to '1' bits in `s`, targeting the ancilla qubit.
  - Does not include measurement gates.
- The oracle correctly encodes the bitstring `s` for the Bernstein-Vazirani problem.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def bv_function(s: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Constructs a Bernstein-Vazirani oracle circuit for a given bitstring s.

    Args:
        s (str): The hidden bitstring for the BV problem.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit implementing the BV oracle.
    """
    n = len(s)  # Number of input qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n + 1)  # n input qubits + 1 ancilla qubit

    # Prepare ancilla qubit in |-> state: apply X then H
    qc.x(n)
    qc.h(n)

    # Apply Hadamard gates to input qubits to create superposition
    for i in range(n):
        qc.h(i)

    # Oracle: apply CNOT from input qubit i to ancilla if s[i] == '1'
    for i, bit in enumerate(s):
        if bit == '1':
            qc.cx(i, n)

    # Apply Hadamard gates again to input qubits
    for i in range(n):
        qc.h(i)

    # The oracle does not include measurement; it is done externally
    return qc
```

=== [38/151] qiskitHumanEval/37 :: bv_algorithm ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from qiskit.primitives.containers.primitive_result import PrimitiveResult
def bv_algorithm(s: str) -> [list, PrimitiveResult]:
    """ Illustrate a Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm routine on Qiskit and run it using Qiskit Sampler with Aer simulator as backend for a string of 0s and 1s. Return the bit strings of the result and the result itself.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the Bernstein-Vazirani (BV) algorithm function `bv_algorithm(s: str)`:

1. **Initialize the Quantum Circuit**
   - Determine the number of qubits `n` from the length of the input string `s`.
   - Create a quantum circuit with `n + 1` qubits.
     - The first `n` qubits are the input register.
     - The last qubit is the auxiliary qubit (oracle qubit).

2. **Prepare the Initial State**
   - Initialize the auxiliary qubit in the state |1⟩ by applying an X gate.
   - Apply a Hadamard gate to the auxiliary qubit to create the |−⟩ state.
   - Apply Hadamard gates to each of the `n` input qubits to create superposition.

3. **Implement the Oracle**
   - For each bit `s_i` in the secret string `s` (index `i` from 0 to `n-1`):
     - If `s_i` is '1', apply a controlled-NOT (CNOT) gate with the `i`th input qubit as control and the auxiliary qubit as target.
   - This applies the oracle corresponding to the secret string.

4. **Apply Hadamard Gates Post-Oracle**
   - Apply Hadamard gates to each of the `n` input qubits.
   - The auxiliary qubit is not touched in this step.

5. **Measurement**
   - Measure the first `n` qubits (input register).
   - No need to measure the auxiliary qubit.

6. **Execution Using Qiskit Sampler with Aer Simulator**
   - Initialize a `Sampler` instance with the Aer simulator backend.
   - Execute the circuit with the sampler to get bitstring samples and the primitive result object.
   - Use a sufficient number of shots (e.g., 1024) to ensure accurate statistics.

7. **Return Values**
   - Extract the bitstrings measured from the sampler results.
   - Return a tuple containing:
     - The list of measured bitstrings (most likely the secret string in binary).
     - The `PrimitiveResult` object returned by the sampler.

**Expected Outcome:**
- The measurement of the input register will reveal the secret string `s` with high probability (ideally, with certainty).
- The auxiliary qubit's state is not measured and thus does not affect the output.
- The `PrimitiveResult` contains detailed information about the sampling results.

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The function correctly constructs the BV algorithm circuit for any given binary string `s`.
- The sampler runs the circuit on the Aer simulator backend.
- The measured bitstrings correspond to the secret string `s` with high accuracy.
- The function returns both the list of measured bitstrings and the `PrimitiveResult` object.
- The function handles input strings of arbitrary length (within simulator capabilities).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit.primitives.containers.primitive_result import PrimitiveResult

def bv_algorithm(s: str) -> [list, PrimitiveResult]:
    """
    Implement the Bernstein-Vazirani algorithm for a given secret string s.
    Run the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator backend.
    
    Args:
        s (str): Secret binary string (e.g., '1011')
        
    Returns:
        tuple:
            - List of measured bitstrings (most likely the secret string)
            - PrimitiveResult object from the sampler execution
    """
    n = len(s)  # Number of input qubits
    
    # Create a quantum circuit with n input qubits + 1 auxiliary qubit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n + 1, n)
    
    # Step 1: Initialize auxiliary qubit in |1> state
    qc.x(n)
    # Apply Hadamard to auxiliary qubit to create |-> state
    qc.h(n)
    
    # Step 2: Apply Hadamard gates to input qubits to create superposition
    for i in range(n):
        qc.h(i)
    
    # Step 3: Oracle implementation
    # For each bit in s, if bit is '1', apply CNOT with input qubit as control and auxiliary as target
    for i, bit in enumerate(s):
        if bit == '1':
            qc.cx(i, n)
    
    # Step 4: Apply Hadamard gates again to input qubits
    for i in range(n):
        qc.h(i)
    
    # Step 5: Measure input qubits
    for i in range(n):
        qc.measure(i, i)
    
    # Step 6: Execute using Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(session=None, backend=AerSimulator())
    # Run the circuit with 1024 shots for good statistics
    result = sampler.run(qc, shots=1024).result()
    
    # Extract measured bitstrings from the result
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()
    # Sort bitstrings by probability descending
    sorted_bitstrings = sorted(counts, key=counts.get, reverse=True)
    
    return sorted_bitstrings, result
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_quantum_circuit_based_h0_crz01_h1_cry10(theta):
    """
    Build a 2-qubit Quantum Circuit composed by:
    - H gate on qubit 0
    - Controlled-RZ gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1, parameterized by theta
    - H gate on qubit 1
    - Controlled-RY gate with control qubit 1 and target qubit 0, parameterized by theta

    Args:
        theta (float): rotation angle for controlled-RZ and controlled-RY gates

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: the constructed 2-qubit quantum circuit
    """
    # Initialize a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)

    # Apply H gate on qubit 0
    qc.h(0)

    # Apply controlled-RZ gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.crz(theta, 0, 1)

    # Apply H gate on qubit 1
    qc.h(1)

    # Apply controlled-RY gate with control qubit 1 and target qubit 0
    qc.cry(theta, 1, 0)

    return qc

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Statevector
def create_uniform_superposition(n: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Initialize a uniform superposition on n qubits and return statevector.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing create_uniform_superposition(n):

1. Initialize a quantum circuit:
   - The circuit should have n qubits.
   - No classical bits are required if only the statevector is returned.

2. Prepare uniform superposition:
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to each of the n qubits.
   - This transforms each qubit from |0⟩ to (|0⟩ + |1⟩)/√2.
   - The overall state becomes an equal superposition of all 2^n computational basis states.

3. Statevector extraction:
   - Use Qiskit's Statevector class to obtain the statevector from the quantum circuit.
   - This statevector represents the uniform superposition of all basis states.

4. No measurements are needed since the output is the statevector, not measurement outcomes.

Expected outcome:
- The resulting quantum circuit prepares the equal superposition state
  |ψ⟩ = (1/√2^n) Σ_{x=0}^{2^n-1} |x⟩.
- The statevector returned corresponds to this uniform superposition.

Acceptance criteria:
- The returned QuantumCircuit has n qubits.
- All qubits have Hadamard gates applied.
- The Statevector derived from the circuit matches the expected uniform superposition amplitudes.
- No measurements are included in the circuit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Statevector

def create_uniform_superposition(n: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Initialize a uniform superposition on n qubits and return the circuit.
    
    Args:
        n (int): Number of qubits.
        
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: Circuit preparing uniform superposition on n qubits.
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with n qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate to each qubit to create uniform superposition
    for qubit in range(n):
        qc.h(qubit)
    
    return qc

# Example usage and verification
if __name__ == "__main__":
    n = 3
    circuit = create_uniform_superposition(n)
    # Get the statevector from the circuit
    state = Statevector.from_instruction(circuit)
    print("Statevector amplitudes:")
    print(state.data)
    # The amplitudes should all be 1/sqrt(2^n)
    import numpy as np
    expected_amplitude = 1 / np.sqrt(2**n)
    assert np.allclose(np.abs(state.data), expected_amplitude), "Statevector amplitudes do not match uniform superposition."

=== [41/151] qiskitHumanEval/40 :: init_random_3qubit ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Statevector
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.options import SamplerOptions
def init_random_3qubit(desired_vector: [complex])-> dict:
    """ Initialize a non-trivial 3-qubit state for a given desired vector state and return counts after running it using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and ser=t seed as 42.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `init_random_3qubit(desired_vector: [complex]) -> dict`:

1. **Initialize Quantum Circuit:**
   - Create a quantum circuit with 3 qubits and 3 classical bits (for measurement).
   - The number of qubits is fixed to 3 as per the requirement.

2. **Prepare the Desired State:**
   - The input `desired_vector` is a complex vector representing the desired 3-qubit quantum state. It should have length 8 (since 3 qubits → 2^3 = 8 amplitudes).
   - Use Qiskit's `Initialize` instruction or equivalent to prepare the circuit in the desired 3-qubit state from the zero state.
   - This step involves decomposing the desired state vector into gates that prepare that state starting from |000>.

3. **Measurement:**
   - Add measurement operations to all 3 qubits, mapping each qubit's measurement to the corresponding classical bit.
   - This will allow retrieving measurement outcomes from the circuit execution.

4. **Sampler Setup and Execution:**
   - Use Qiskit's IBM Runtime `Sampler` with the AerSimulator backend.
   - Set the seed for the sampler to 42 to ensure reproducibility.
   - Set the number of shots (samples) to a suitable number, e.g., 1024 or 8192, to get meaningful statistics.
   - Run the sampler on the prepared circuit.

5. **Collect and Return Results:**
   - From the sampler result, extract the counts of measurement outcomes.
   - Return the counts as a dictionary mapping bitstrings (e.g., '000', '101') to integer counts.

6. **Acceptance Criteria:**
   - The function should accept any valid 8-element complex vector and produce measurement counts from the initialized state.
   - The returned counts should reflect the probabilities encoded in the input vector within statistical variation.
   - Measurements must be on all 3 qubits.
   - The sampler must use AerSimulator backend with seed set to 42.
   - The function should return counts in the standard Qiskit format (dict with bitstring keys).

---

**Summary:**

- **Quantum Gates and Operations:** Use `Initialize` to prepare the 3-qubit state from the input vector.
- **Circuit Structure:** 3 qubits + 3 classical bits, with initialization and measurement.
- **Measurement:** Measure all qubits to classical bits.
- **Sampler:** IBM Runtime Sampler with AerSimulator backend, seed=42, standard shots.
- **Expected Outcome:** Counts reflecting the probability distribution of the desired input state vector.
- **Acceptance Criteria:** Correct, reproducible counts matching the input state probabilities.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import Initialize
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.options import SamplerOptions
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator

def init_random_3qubit(desired_vector: [complex]) -> dict:
    """
    Initialize a non-trivial 3-qubit state for a given desired vector state and return counts
    after running it using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and set seed as 42.

    Args:
        desired_vector (list of complex): 8-element complex vector representing the desired 3-qubit state.

    Returns:
        dict: Measurement counts as a dictionary mapping bitstrings to counts.
    """
    # Validate input vector length
    if len(desired_vector) != 8:
        raise ValueError("Input vector must have length 8 for a 3-qubit state.")

    # Create a quantum circuit with 3 qubits and 3 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(3, 3)

    # Initialize the circuit to the desired state vector
    init_gate = Initialize(desired_vector)
    qc.append(init_gate, [0, 1, 2])

    # Add measurement operations for all qubits
    qc.measure([0, 1, 2], [0, 1, 2])

    # Setup AerSimulator backend with seed 42
    backend = AerSimulator(seed_simulator=42, seed_transpiler=42)

    # Create Sampler with the AerSimulator backend and set seed
    sampler = Sampler(session=None, backend=backend)

    # Set sampler options: number of shots and seed
    options = SamplerOptions(shots=8192, seed=42)

    # Run the sampler on the circuit
    job = sampler.run(qc, options=options)
    result = job.result()

    # Extract counts from the result
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()

    # Convert probabilities to counts by multiplying by shots and rounding
    shots = options.shots
    counts_int = {bitstr: int(round(prob * shots)) for bitstr, prob in counts.items()}

    return counts_int
```

Plan for implementing the compose_op function that composes the Pauli operator "XZ" with a 3-qubit identity operator:

   - The Pauli string "XZ" applies Pauli X on qubit 0 and Pauli Z on qubit 1.
   - Use the Pauli or Operator class to create this 2-qubit operator.

3. Define the 3-qubit identity operator:
   - This is the identity operator on 3 qubits.
   - Can be constructed using the identity matrix of size 2^(3) = 8.
   - Use either numpy.eye(8) or Operator.from_label("III").

4. Compose the operators:
   - Composition here means the tensor product, i.e., the combined operator acting on (2 + 3) = 5 qubits.
   - Tensor product ordering: The "XZ" operator acts on the first 2 qubits, the identity on the next 3 qubits.
   - Use Operator.tensor() method to perform the tensor product.

5. Return the resulting Operator instance representing "XZ" ⊗ I_3.

Acceptance criteria:
- The returned Operator acts on 5 qubits.
- The operator matrix dimension is 2^5 x 2^5 = 32 x 32.
- The first two qubits correspond to "XZ" Pauli operators.
- The last three qubits correspond to identity.
- The function is well-documented and returns a Qiskit Operator instance.

This plan enables a developer to implement the function compose_op() to produce the desired composed operator.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info.operators import Operator, Pauli
import numpy as np

def compose_op() -> Operator:
    """
    Compose the 2-qubit Pauli operator "XZ" with a 3-qubit identity operator.
    
    The operator "XZ" applies Pauli X on qubit 0 and Pauli Z on qubit 1.
    The 3-qubit identity operator acts on qubits 2, 3, and 4.
    
    Returns:
        Operator: A 5-qubit operator representing "XZ" ⊗ I_3.
    """
    # Create the 2-qubit Pauli operator "XZ"
    # Pauli label "XZ" means X on qubit 0 and Z on qubit 1
    pauli_xz = Pauli("XZ")
    op_xz = Operator(pauli_xz)
    
    # Create the 3-qubit identity operator
    op_identity_3 = Operator.from_label("III")
    
    # Compose the operators via tensor product: "XZ" on first 2 qubits, identity on next 3 qubits
    composed_op = op_xz.tensor(op_identity_3)
    
    return composed_op
```

```python
    The 3-qubit identity operator acts on qubits 2, 3, and 4.
```

Task: Implement a function to construct a combined operator 0.5 * (XX + YY - 3 * ZZ) using Qiskit's Operator class.

**Implementation Plan**

3. **Convert Pauli Operators to Operator Objects:**
   - Convert each Pauli object to a Qiskit Operator instance.

4. **Combine Operators According to Expression:**
   - Calculate the linear combination:
     - combined_op = 0.5 * (XX_operator + YY_operator - 3 * ZZ_operator)

5. **Return the Combined Operator:**
   - The function should return the resulting Operator instance.

---

**Expected Outcome:**

- The returned Operator represents the Hermitian operator corresponding to the weighted sum 0.5 * (XX + YY - 3 * ZZ).

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info.operators import Operator, Pauli
import numpy as np

def combine_op() -> Operator:
    """ Combine the following three operators XX, YY, ZZ as: 0.5 * (XX + YY - 3 * ZZ).

    Returns:
        Operator: The combined 2-qubit operator.
    """
    # Define 2-qubit Pauli operators
    XX = Pauli('XX')
    YY = Pauli('YY')
    ZZ = Pauli('ZZ')

    # Convert Pauli objects to Operator instances
    XX_op = Operator(XX)
    YY_op = Operator(YY)
    ZZ_op = Operator(ZZ)

    # Combine operators according to the expression
    combined_op = 0.5 * (XX_op + YY_op - 3 * ZZ_op)

    return combined_op


# Test the function
if __name__ == "__main__":
    op = combine_op()
    print("Combined operator matrix:\n", np.round(op.data, decimals=3))
    print("Operator acts on", op.num_qubits, "qubits")
```

2. **Identify the Least Busy Backend:**

   - Filter the backends to select only those that are operational and support the required features (e.g., `backend.configuration().open_pulse == False` if applicable).
   - Use the `least_busy()` utility from Qiskit to find the backend with the lowest queue.

3. **Generate the Preset Pass Manager:**
   - Use the `generate_preset_pass_manager` function.
   - Pass the selected backend as the `backend` parameter.
   - Set the `optimization_level` parameter to 3.

4. **Return the Pass Manager Object:**
   - The function should return the instantiated pass manager object, which can be used for transpiling circuits with the specified optimization level for the identified backend.

**Additional Notes:**

- The function signature suggests returning a `QuantumCircuit`, but the docstring and implementation indicate returning a pass manager instance. This should be clarified: the pass manager is not a circuit.
- The function should handle any exceptions or errors related to service connection or backend selection gracefully (optional, based on requirements).

2. **Retrieve available backends and identify the least busy one.**

   - Backend: least busy backend

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService
from qiskit.providers.ibmq import least_busy

def generate_pass_manager_obj():
    """
    Instantiate a preset pass manager using Qiskit for the least busy device available
    with optimization level 3. Return the resulting pass manager instance.
    """
    # Initialize the IBM Quantum Runtime service
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Get the list of backends that are operational and not simulators
    backends = service.backends(
        filters=lambda b: b.status().operational and not b.configuration().simulator
    )

    # Select the least busy backend from the filtered list
    backend = least_busy(backends)

    # Generate the preset pass manager with optimization level 3 for the selected backend
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(optimization_level=3, backend=backend)

    return pass_manager
```

**Note:** The error indicates a version mismatch between Qiskit and qiskit-ibm-provider packages. To fix this, please ensure your Qiskit and IBM provider packages are compatible and up to date by running:

```bash
pip install -U qiskit qiskit-ibm-provider qiskit-ibm-runtime
```

If the environment does not support `qiskit.providers.ibmq.least_busy`, alternatively, you can implement a custom least busy function:

```python
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService

def custom_least_busy(backends):
    """Return the backend with the smallest queue length."""
    return min(backends, key=lambda b: b.status().pending_jobs)

def generate_pass_manager_obj():
    """
    Instantiate a preset pass manager using Qiskit for the least busy device available
    with optimization level 3. Return the resulting pass manager instance.
    """
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    backends = service.backends(
        filters=lambda b: b.status().operational and not b.configuration().simulator
    )

    backend = custom_least_busy(backends)

    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(optimization_level=3, backend=backend)

    return pass_manager
```

    """ Write an example using Qiskit that performs tensor operation on a 1-qubit quantum circuit with an X gate and a 2-qubit quantum circuit with a CRY gate, where the CRY gate has an angle of 0.2 radians and is controlled by qubit 0.
    """

Plan for implementing the requested quantum circuit combining tensor operations:

1. Initialize quantum circuits:
   - Create a 1-qubit quantum circuit (Circuit A).
   - Create a 2-qubit quantum circuit (Circuit B).

2. Apply gates to each circuit:
   - Circuit A: Apply an X gate to its single qubit.
   - Circuit B: Apply a controlled-RY (CRY) gate with an angle of 0.2 radians, controlled by qubit 0 and targeting qubit 1.

3. Perform the tensor product (composition) of the two circuits:
   - The tensor product of Circuit A and Circuit B results in a 3-qubit circuit.
   - The ordering of qubits in the combined circuit will be: qubit 0 from Circuit A, followed by qubits 0 and 1 from Circuit B.
   - The combined circuit applies the gates from Circuit A on its qubit and the gates from Circuit B on its qubits independently, as per the tensor product.

4. Add measurements:
   - Optionally, add measurements to all three qubits to observe the final state after applying the gates.

5. Expected result:
   - The final 3-qubit circuit applies an X gate on qubit 0 (from Circuit A).
   - Applies the CRY(0.2) gate controlled by qubit 1 controlling qubit 2 (from Circuit B).
   - The combined circuit represents the tensor product of the two circuits, acting on three qubits.

Acceptance criteria:
- The code correctly initializes both circuits.
- The X gate is applied on the single qubit of Circuit A.
- The CRY(0.2) gate is applied with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1 in Circuit B.
- The tensor product of the two circuits is computed correctly, resulting in a 3-qubit circuit.
- Optional measurements are included on all qubits.
- The final circuit structure and gate applications match the description above.

This plan can guide the Qiskit developer to implement the function tensor_circuits() as requested.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def tensor_circuits() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a 3-qubit quantum circuit that is the tensor product of:
    - A 1-qubit circuit with an X gate on qubit 0
    - A 2-qubit circuit with a CRY(0.2) gate controlled by qubit 0 and targeting qubit 1
    
    The combined circuit applies the X gate on qubit 0 (from the first circuit),
    and the CRY gate controlled by qubit 1 controlling qubit 2 (from the second circuit).
    Measurements are added to all qubits.
    """
    # Circuit A: 1-qubit circuit with X gate
    circuit_a = QuantumCircuit(1)
    circuit_a.x(0)  # Apply X gate on qubit 0
    
    # Circuit B: 2-qubit circuit with CRY(0.2) gate controlled by qubit 0 targeting qubit 1
    circuit_b = QuantumCircuit(2)
    circuit_b.cry(0.2, 0, 1)  # Controlled-RY with angle 0.2
    
    # Tensor product of circuit_a and circuit_b
    # This creates a 3-qubit circuit where qubit 0 is from circuit_a,
    # and qubits 1 and 2 are from circuit_b
    combined_circuit = circuit_a.tensor(circuit_b)
    
    # Add measurements to all qubits
    combined_circuit.measure_all()
    
    return combined_circuit
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info.random import random_clifford

def get_random_clifford(n_qubits):
    """
    Generate a random Clifford circuit acting on n_qubits qubits.

    Parameters:
        n_qubits (int): Number of qubits (> 0).

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit implementing a random Clifford operation on n_qubits qubits.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If n_qubits is not a positive integer.
    """
    if not isinstance(n_qubits, int) or n_qubits <= 0:
        raise ValueError("n_qubits must be a positive integer.")

    # Generate a random Clifford operator on n_qubits
    clifford_op = random_clifford(n_qubits)

    # Convert the Clifford operator to a QuantumCircuit
    clifford_circuit = clifford_op.to_circuit()

    return clifford_circuit
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import LinearFunction
from qiskit.synthesis.linear.linear_matrix_utils import random_invertible_binary_matrix

def get_random_linear_function(n_qubits, seed):
    """
    Generate a random linear function circuit on n_qubits using a random invertible binary matrix.

    Args:
        n_qubits (int): Number of qubits for the linear function.
        seed (int): Seed for reproducibility of the random matrix.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A circuit implementing the linear reversible function.
    """
    # Generate a random invertible binary matrix of size n_qubits x n_qubits
    matrix = random_invertible_binary_matrix(n_qubits, seed)

    # Create the LinearFunction circuit using the generated matrix
    linear_func_circuit = LinearFunction(matrix)

    return linear_func_circuit
```

=== [48/151] qiskitHumanEval/47 :: random_coin_flip ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
def random_coin_flip(samples: int)->dict:
    """ Design a Quantum Circuit that simulates random coin flips for the given samples using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and outputs the count of heads and tails in a dictionary. The heads should be stored in the dict as 'Heads' and tails as 'Tails'. For example
    random_coin_flip(10) == {'Heads' : 5, 'Tails : 5}
    random_coin_flip(20) == {'Heads' : 9, 'Tails : 11}.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the `random_coin_flip` function simulating random coin flips using Qiskit Sampler and Aer simulator:

1. **Initialize Quantum Circuit:**
   - Create a quantum circuit with a single qubit since one qubit measured in the computational basis can simulate a coin flip (|0⟩ = Heads, |1⟩ = Tails).
   - No classical bits are strictly required because Qiskit Sampler returns measurement results directly.

2. **Prepare the Quantum State:**
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to the qubit to create an equal superposition state: (|0⟩ + |1⟩)/√2.
   - This ensures a 50/50 probability of measuring 0 or 1, simulating a fair coin flip.

3. **Measurement Strategy:**
   - Use Qiskit Sampler to sample the measurement results.
   - Set the number of shots/samples equal to the input parameter `samples`.
   - Measure the qubit in the computational basis (this is implicit in the Sampler's measurement).

4. **Execution Backend:**
   - Use AerSimulator as the backend to run the circuit efficiently on a classical simulator.

5. **Collect and Process Results:**
   - Obtain a dictionary of counts keyed by measurement outcomes ('0' or '1').
   - Map the counts of '0' to 'Heads' and counts of '1' to 'Tails' in the returned dictionary.
   - If either outcome is missing (which can happen if no samples landed on that result), assign a count of zero.

6. **Return Value:**
   - Return a dictionary with keys `'Heads'` and `'Tails'` and their respective counts as values.

**Acceptance Criteria:**

- The function takes an integer `samples` as input.
- The function returns a dictionary with keys `'Heads'` and `'Tails'` and integer counts summing to `samples`.
- The distribution of counts roughly approximates a fair coin flip (close to equal counts for large samples).
- The function uses Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator backend.
- The circuit uses a single qubit with a Hadamard gate to create superposition.

This plan provides a straightforward, efficient way to simulate random coin flips using quantum circuits and Qiskit's sampling capabilities.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def random_coin_flip(samples: int) -> dict:
    """
    Simulate random coin flips using a quantum circuit with Qiskit Sampler and AerSimulator backend.
    Args:
        samples (int): Number of coin flips to simulate.
    Returns:
        dict: Dictionary with counts of 'Heads' and 'Tails'.
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 1 qubit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(1)
    # Apply Hadamard gate to create equal superposition (|0> + |1>)/sqrt(2)
    qc.h(0)

    # Initialize the AerSimulator backend
    backend = AerSimulator()

    # Initialize the Sampler with the AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Run the sampler with the circuit and specified number of shots (samples)
    # The Sampler returns a list of quasi-probabilities for each circuit
    # We use the 'shots' parameter to get sampled measurement results
    job = sampler.run(qc, shots=samples)
    result = job.result()

    # Get counts of measurement outcomes as a dictionary
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()

    # Map counts to Heads ('0') and Tails ('1')
    heads_count = counts.get('0', 0) * samples
    tails_count = counts.get('1', 0) * samples

    # Since counts are probabilities, convert to integers
    heads_count = int(round(heads_count))
    tails_count = int(round(tails_count))

    # Adjust counts to ensure sum equals samples (due to rounding)
    total = heads_count + tails_count
    if total != samples:
        diff = samples - total
        # Adjust heads count to fix the difference
        heads_count += diff

    return {'Heads': heads_count, 'Tails': tails_count}
```

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
def random_number_generator_unsigned_8bit(n: int)->[int]:
    """ Write a function that generates n number of random 8-bit unsigned integers using a Quantum Circuit and outputs a list of integers.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a function to generate n random 8-bit unsigned integers using a quantum circuit:

1. Qubit requirements:
   - To generate an 8-bit unsigned integer, we need 8 qubits (one qubit per bit).
   
2. Circuit initialization:
   - Initialize a quantum circuit with 8 qubits.
   
3. Gate application to generate randomness:
   - Apply Hadamard gates (H) to each of the 8 qubits.
     - This puts each qubit into an equal superposition of |0> and |1>, creating a uniform probability distribution over all 8-bit states.
   
4. Measurement:
   - Measure all 8 qubits into 8 classical bits.
   - This measurement collapses the superposition, resulting in a random 8-bit number.
   
5. Repetition for n samples:
   - Execute the circuit with a number of shots equal to n.
   - Each shot yields one random 8-bit number.
   
6. Post-processing:
   - Collect the bitstrings from the measurement results.
   - Convert each 8-bit binary string into an unsigned integer (0 to 255).
   - Return the list of n integers.
   
7. Simulator or backend:
   - Use a Qiskit Aer simulator (AerSimulator) or an IBMQ backend via Sampler to run the circuit.
   
8. Expected outcome:
   - A list of n integers, each between 0 and 255, uniformly sampled from the quantum measurement outcomes.
   
Acceptance criteria:
- The function accepts an integer n as input.
- The function returns a list of n integers.
- Each integer is in the range [0, 255].
- The results are generated by measuring a quantum circuit with 8 qubits prepared in superposition.
- The distribution of generated numbers approximates uniform randomness when n is large.
- The implementation uses only Qiskit standard quantum gates and measurement operations.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, execute
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator

def random_number_generator_unsigned_8bit(n: int) -> [int]:
    """
    Generates n random 8-bit unsigned integers using a quantum circuit.
    
    Args:
        n (int): Number of random 8-bit integers to generate.
        
    Returns:
        List[int]: List of n random integers in the range [0, 255].
    """
    # Number of qubits and classical bits needed for 8-bit numbers
    num_bits = 8
    
    # Create quantum circuit with 8 qubits and 8 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(num_bits, num_bits)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate to each qubit to create superposition
    for qubit in range(num_bits):
        qc.h(qubit)
    
    # Measure all qubits
    qc.measure(range(num_bits), range(num_bits))
    
    # Use AerSimulator backend
    simulator = AerSimulator()
    
    # Execute the circuit with n shots to get n samples
    job = execute(qc, backend=simulator, shots=n)
    result = job.result()
    
    # Get counts dictionary: bitstring -> counts
    counts = result.get_counts(qc)
    
    # Prepare list to hold all samples
    samples = []
    
    # Expand counts into individual samples
    for bitstring, count in counts.items():
        # Convert bitstring to integer
        value = int(bitstring, 2)
        # Append the integer count times
        samples.extend([value] * count)
    
    return samples

# Example usage:
# print(random_number_generator_unsigned_8bit(10))  # Generate 10 random 8-bit numbers

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
def simple_elitzur_vaidman()->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Return a simple Elitzur Vaidman bomb tester circuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a simple Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester quantum circuit:

1. Qubit and Circuit Initialization:
   - Initialize a QuantumCircuit with 1 qubit and 1 classical bit.
     - The single qubit represents the photon in the interferometer.
     - The classical bit will be used to record the measurement outcome.

2. Quantum Gates and Circuit Structure:
   - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) to the qubit to simulate the photon entering a 50/50 beam splitter.
   - Implement the bomb interaction:
     - If the bomb is present, it acts as a "quantum bomb" that absorbs the photon if the photon takes one path.
     - To simulate this, apply a controlled operation that "measures" or collapses the state if the photon takes the "bomb path."
     - Since this is a simple circuit, model the bomb path by applying a Z gate or a measurement conditional on the photon being in the "bomb" path.
     - Alternatively, use an identity or no gate to simulate no bomb, and a Z or phase gate for bomb presence.
   - Apply a second Hadamard gate to recombine the paths.

3. Measurement:
   - Measure the qubit in the computational basis.
   - Record the measurement result in the classical bit.

4. Expected Outcomes:
   - If the bomb is present and functional:
     - There is a non-zero probability to detect the photon in a way that reveals the bomb without triggering it.
     - Measurement outcomes will differ from the no-bomb case, allowing interaction-free detection.
   - If no bomb is present:
     - The photon will always be detected in a particular output state corresponding to constructive interference.

5. Acceptance Criteria:
   - The circuit must have 1 qubit and 1 classical bit.
   - The circuit must apply a Hadamard, simulate bomb interaction, then another Hadamard.
   - The measurement should be in the computational basis.
   - The circuit should reflect the basic Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester logic enabling interaction-free measurement.

This plan provides a straightforward quantum circuit design for the Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester suitable for implementation in Qiskit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def simple_elitzur_vaidman() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Return a simple Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester circuit.
    
    This circuit simulates the Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester using one qubit and one classical bit.
    The photon enters a Mach-Zehnder interferometer modeled by two Hadamard gates.
    The bomb interaction is simulated by a Z gate representing the bomb's presence on one path.
    Measurement reveals whether the bomb is present without triggering it.
    """
    # Initialize circuit with 1 qubit and 1 classical bit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(1, 1)
    
    # First Hadamard gate simulates the first beam splitter
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Bomb interaction simulated by a Z gate on the qubit
    # This represents the bomb being present on one path
    qc.z(0)
    
    # Second Hadamard gate simulates the second beam splitter recombining paths
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Measure the qubit in the computational basis
    qc.measure(0, 0)
    
    return qc

# Test the circuit creation
circuit = simple_elitzur_vaidman()
print(circuit.draw())

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def simple_elitzur_vaidman() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Return a simple Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester circuit.
    
    This circuit simulates the Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester using one qubit and one classical bit.
    The photon enters a Mach-Zehnder interferometer modeled by two Hadamard gates.
    The bomb interaction is simulated by a Z gate representing the bomb's presence on one path.
    Measurement reveals whether the bomb is present without triggering it.
    """
    # Initialize circuit with 1 qubit and 1 classical bit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(1, 1)
    
    # First Hadamard gate simulates the first beam splitter
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Bomb interaction simulated by a Z gate on the qubit
    # This represents the bomb being present on one path
    qc.z(0)
    
    # Second Hadamard gate simulates the second beam splitter recombining paths
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Measure the qubit in the computational basis
    qc.measure(0, 0)
    
    return qc

# Test the circuit creation
circuit = simple_elitzur_vaidman()
print(circuit.draw())

Task Analysis:
The user wants to implement a function that removes a gate (instruction) at a specific position from a given QuantumCircuit object in Qiskit. QuantumCircuit objects store a sequence of instructions (gates and operations) in order, and the user wants to remove the one at the specified index.

Implementation Plan:

   - Qiskit QuantumCircuit has a property `data` which is a list of tuples, each representing an instruction:
     (Instruction, qargs, cargs)
   - The position parameter refers to the index in this `data` list.

2. Steps to remove a gate at a specific position:
   - Validate the position parameter to ensure it is within bounds (0 <= position < length of circuit.data).
   - Remove the element at the specified index from the circuit.data list.
   - Since Qiskit circuits are mutable, this will update the circuit in-place.

3. Additional considerations:
   - Removing a gate may affect later instructions if they depend on it, but this is beyond the scope.
   - No additional quantum gates or measurement steps are needed here.
   - The function modifies the circuit in-place; optionally, it could return the modified circuit for chaining.

Acceptance Criteria:
- The function removes the instruction at the specified position from the QuantumCircuit without errors.
- If the position is invalid, the function should raise an appropriate exception or handle it gracefully.
- The circuit after removal reflects the updated sequence of instructions.
- No other parts of the circuit are modified or removed.

Summary Plan:
1. Check that position is valid for circuit.data.
2. Remove the instruction at circuit.data[position].
3. No additional gates or measurements required.
4. Function modifies circuit in-place, returning None or the modified circuit is optional.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def remove_gate_in_position(circuit: QuantumCircuit, position: int):
    """Remove the gate (instruction) at the specified position from the given QuantumCircuit.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to modify.
        position (int): The index of the gate to remove.

    Raises:
        IndexError: If the position is out of range of the circuit's instructions.
    """
    # Validate position
    if position < 0 or position >= len(circuit.data):
        raise IndexError(f"Position {position} is out of range for circuit with {len(circuit.data)} instructions.")
    
    # Remove the instruction at the specified position
    del circuit.data[position]
```

=== [52/151] qiskitHumanEval/51 :: quantum_teleportation_circuit ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister, QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Instruction
def quantum_teleportation_circuit(data: [Instruction])->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Write a function to build a quantum teleportation circuit that takes a list of instructions as an argument to transfer the data from the sender to the receiver while taking advantage of dynamic circuits.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a quantum teleportation circuit function with dynamic circuits in Qiskit:

1. **Initialize the quantum and classical registers:**
   - Create a quantum register with 3 qubits:
     - Qubit 0: The data qubit to be teleported (sender's qubit)
     - Qubit 1: The sender's half of the Bell pair (entangled qubit)
     - Qubit 2: The receiver's half of the Bell pair
   - Create two classical registers with 1 bit each to store measurement results from the sender's qubits (for classical communication).

2. **Prepare the data qubit:**
   - Apply the sequence of instructions from the input list `data` to qubit 0. These instructions represent the unknown state to be teleported.

3. **Create entanglement (Bell pair) between qubits 1 and 2:**
   - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) to qubit 1
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 1 and target qubit 2

4. **Bell state measurement on qubits 0 and 1 (sender's side):**
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
   - Apply a Hadamard gate to qubit 0
   - Measure qubit 0 and store the result in the first classical bit
   - Measure qubit 1 and store the result in the second classical bit

5. **Apply conditional corrections on qubit 2 (receiver's qubit) based on measurement outcomes:**
   - Use dynamic circuit features to apply gates conditionally:
     - If classical bit 1 (measurement of qubit 1) is 1, apply an X gate to qubit 2
     - If classical bit 0 (measurement of qubit 0) is 1, apply a Z gate to qubit 2

6. **Finalize the circuit:**
   - The qubit 2 now holds the teleported state.
   - Optionally, measure qubit 2 to verify the teleportation outcome or leave it unmeasured for further quantum processing.

**Quantum gates and operations required:**
- Hadamard (H)
- CNOT
- Conditional X and Z gates based on classical bits
- Measurements of qubits 0 and 1

**Circuit structure and qubit requirements:**
- 3 qubits total
- 2 classical bits for measurement results

**Measurement strategy:**
- Measure sender's qubits (0 and 1) to obtain classical information for correction
- Conditional operations on receiver's qubit depend on measurement results

**Expected outcome:**
- The quantum state prepared on qubit 0 (data qubit) is teleported onto qubit 2 after applying the corrections
- Measurement results provide the classical bits required for conditional corrections

**Acceptance criteria:**
- The function accepts a list of quantum instructions and applies them correctly to the data qubit
- The circuit creates proper entanglement between qubits 1 and 2
- The Bell measurement on qubits 0 and 1 is implemented correctly
- Conditional corrections on qubit 2 are properly implemented using dynamic circuit features (classical control)
- The returned QuantumCircuit is valid and can be executed on simulators or devices supporting dynamic circuits
- Teleportation fidelity is high when tested with known input states (optional verification)

This plan provides a detailed roadmap for implementing the requested function without writing actual code.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister, QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Instruction

def quantum_teleportation_circuit(data: [Instruction]) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Build a quantum teleportation circuit that takes a list of instructions as an argument
    to transfer the data from the sender to the receiver using dynamic circuits.

    Args:
        data (list[Instruction]): List of quantum instructions to prepare the data qubit (qubit 0).

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The constructed quantum teleportation circuit.
    """
    # Create quantum registers: 3 qubits
    # q0: data qubit to teleport
    # q1: sender's half of Bell pair
    # q2: receiver's half of Bell pair
    qr = QuantumRegister(3, name='q')

    # Create classical registers: 2 bits for measurement results of q0 and q1
    cr0 = ClassicalRegister(1, name='cr0')  # for measurement of qubit 0
    cr1 = ClassicalRegister(1, name='cr1')  # for measurement of qubit 1

    # Initialize the quantum circuit with quantum and classical registers
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr0, cr1)

    # Step 1: Prepare the data qubit (qubit 0) by applying the input instructions
    for instr in data:
        # Append each instruction to qubit 0
        # We assume instructions are single-qubit gates or multi-qubit gates acting on qubit 0 only
        # If instructions act on multiple qubits, user must ensure they target qubit 0 only
        circuit.append(instr, [qr[0]])

    # Step 2: Create entanglement (Bell pair) between qubits 1 and 2
    circuit.h(qr[1])          # Hadamard on qubit 1
    circuit.cx(qr[1], qr[2])  # CNOT with control qubit 1 and target qubit 2

    # Step 3: Bell state measurement on qubits 0 and 1 (sender's side)
    circuit.cx(qr[0], qr[1])  # CNOT with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    circuit.h(qr[0])          # Hadamard on qubit 0

    # Measure qubits 0 and 1 into classical bits cr0 and cr1 respectively
    circuit.measure(qr[0], cr0[0])
    circuit.measure(qr[1], cr1[0])

    # Step 4: Apply conditional corrections on qubit 2 based on measurement outcomes
    # If cr1 == 1, apply X gate to qubit 2
    circuit.x(qr[2]).c_if(cr1, 1)
    # If cr0 == 1, apply Z gate to qubit 2
    circuit.z(qr[2]).c_if(cr0, 1)

    # The qubit 2 now holds the teleported state

    return circuit
```

```python
def quantum_teleportation_circuit(data: [Instruction]) -> QuantumCircuit:

    to transfer the data from the sender to the receiver using dynamic circuits.

    Args:
        data (list[Instruction]): List of quantum instructions to prepare the data qubit (qubit 0).

    Returns:

    cr1 = ClassicalRegister(1, name='cr1')  # for measurement of qubit 1
```

from qiskit import QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister, QuantumCircuit
def send_bits(bitstring: str)->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Provide a quantum circuit that enables the transmission of two classical bits from the sender to the receiver through a single qubit of quantum communication, given that the sender and receiver have access to entangled qubits.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the superdense coding protocol to send two classical bits via one qubit using entanglement:

1. **Qubit and Register Setup**
   - Use 2 qubits: one for the sender (Alice) and one for the receiver (Bob).
   - Use 2 classical bits to store the measurement outcomes at the receiver side.
   - Initialize a QuantumCircuit with 2 qubits and 2 classical bits.

2. **Entanglement Preparation**
   - Prepare a Bell pair (maximally entangled state) between the two qubits:
     - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) on the first qubit (Alice’s qubit).
     - Apply a CNOT gate with the first qubit as control and the second qubit (Bob’s qubit) as target.

3. **Encoding the Classical Bits**
   - Based on the input `bitstring` (2 classical bits), apply one of the four Pauli gates to Alice’s qubit to encode the message:
     - "00" → Apply Identity (do nothing)
     - "01" → Apply X gate
     - "10" → Apply Z gate
     - "11" → Apply X gate followed by Z gate (or equivalently Y gate up to a global phase)
   - This modifies Alice’s qubit to encode the two classical bits into the shared entangled state.

4. **Transmission**
   - Conceptually, Alice sends her qubit to Bob (in the circuit, both qubits remain present, but this step is implied).

5. **Decoding at Receiver**
   - Bob applies the decoding operations to recover the classical bits:
     - Apply CNOT gate with Alice’s qubit (now at Bob’s side) as control and Bob’s qubit as target.
     - Apply Hadamard gate to Alice’s qubit.
   
6. **Measurement**
   - Measure both qubits in the computational basis.
   - Store the results in the 2 classical bits.
   - The measurement outcomes correspond to the original 2 classical bits Alice wanted to send.

7. **Expected Outcome**
   - The two classical bits measured at Bob’s side match the input `bitstring`.
   - This demonstrates transmission of two classical bits by sending only one qubit when prior entanglement is shared.

8. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - The circuit uses exactly 2 qubits and 2 classical bits.
   - The Bell pair is properly prepared.
   - Encoding gates correspond correctly to the input bitstring.
   - Decoding operations are applied as specified.
   - Measurement results correctly decode the input bitstring with high probability (ideally 100% in a noiseless simulation).
   - The circuit is well-documented with comments explaining each step.

This plan enables a Qiskit developer to implement the superdense coding protocol to send two classical bits via a single quantum bit channel with prior entanglement.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, Aer, execute

def send_bits(bitstring: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a quantum circuit implementing the superdense coding protocol to send two classical bits
    via one qubit using prior entanglement.

    Args:
        bitstring (str): Two classical bits as a string, e.g., "00", "01", "10", or "11".

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The quantum circuit implementing superdense coding.
    """
    # Validate input
    if bitstring not in {"00", "01", "10", "11"}:
        raise ValueError("bitstring must be one of '00', '01', '10', or '11'.")

    # Initialize a quantum circuit with 2 qubits and 2 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)

    # Step 1 & 2: Prepare entanglement (Bell pair) between qubit 0 (Alice) and qubit 1 (Bob)
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on Alice's qubit
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target

    # Step 3: Encode the classical bits on Alice's qubit (qubit 0)
    if bitstring == "01":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
    elif bitstring == "10":
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate
    elif bitstring == "11":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate

    # Step 5: Decode the message at Bob's side
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on Alice's qubit

    # Step 6: Measure both qubits to classical bits
    qc.measure(0, 0) # Measure Alice's qubit into classical bit 0
    qc.measure(1, 1) # Measure Bob's qubit into classical bit 1

    return qc

# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Test the circuit for all possible bitstrings
    simulator = Aer.get_backend('qasm_simulator')
    for bits in ["00", "01", "10", "11"]:
        circuit = send_bits(bits)
        job = execute(circuit, simulator, shots=1024)
        result = job.result()
        counts = result.get_counts()
        print(f"Input bits: {bits} -> Measurement counts: {counts}")
```

```python
    Args:
        bitstring (str): Two classical bits as a string, e.g., "00", "01", "10", or "11".

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The quantum circuit implementing superdense coding.
```

```python
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target

    # Step 3: Encode the classical bits on Alice's qubit (qubit 0)
    if bitstring == "01":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
    elif bitstring == "10":
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate
    elif bitstring == "11":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate

    # Step 5: Decode the message at Bob's side
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on Alice's qubit

    # Step 6: Measure both qubits to classical bits
    qc.measure(0, 0) # Measure Alice's qubit into classical bit 0
    qc.measure(1, 1) # Measure Bob's qubit into classical bit 1

    return qc

# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    simulator = BasicAer.get_backend('qasm_simulator')

    for bits in ["00", "01", "10", "11"]:
        circuit = send_bits(bits)
        job = execute(circuit, simulator, shots=1024)
        result = job.result()
        counts = result.get_counts()
        print(f"Input bits: {bits} -> Measurement counts: {counts}")
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.providers.fake_provider import FakeBackend
from qiskit.execute_function import execute

def send_bits(bitstring: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a quantum circuit implementing the superdense coding protocol to send two classical bits
    via one qubit using prior entanglement.

    Args:
        bitstring (str): Two classical bits as a string, e.g., "00", "01", "10", or "11".

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The quantum circuit implementing superdense coding.
    """
    # Validate input
    if bitstring not in {"00", "01", "10", "11"}:
        raise ValueError("bitstring must be one of '00', '01', '10', or '11'.")

    # Initialize a quantum circuit with 2 qubits and 2 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)

    # Step 1 & 2: Prepare entanglement (Bell pair) between qubit 0 (Alice) and qubit 1 (Bob)
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on Alice's qubit
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target

    # Step 3: Encode the classical bits on Alice's qubit (qubit 0)
    if bitstring == "01":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
    elif bitstring == "10":
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate
    elif bitstring == "11":
        qc.x(0)      # Apply X gate
        qc.z(0)      # Apply Z gate

    # Step 5: Decode the message at Bob's side
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with Alice's qubit as control and Bob's as target
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on Alice's qubit

    # Step 6: Measure both qubits to classical bits
    qc.measure(0, 0) # Measure Alice's qubit into classical bit 0
    qc.measure(1, 1) # Measure Bob's qubit into classical bit 1

    return qc

# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Use a fake backend as a simulator alternative
    from qiskit.providers.fake_provider import FakeVigo
    simulator = FakeVigo()

    for bits in ["00", "01", "10", "11"]:
        circuit = send_bits(bits)
        job = execute(circuit, simulator, shots=1024)
        result = job.result()
        counts = result.get_counts()
        print(f"Input bits: {bits} -> Measurement counts: {counts}")
```

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `xor_gate(a: int, b: int) -> dict` that takes two 8-bit integers and returns the classical XOR result using a quantum circuit simulated via Qiskit Sampler and Aer simulator:

1. **Initialize the Quantum Circuit:**
   - Use 24 qubits total:
     - 8 qubits to encode the bits of integer `a` (input register A)
     - 8 qubits to encode the bits of integer `b` (input register B)
     - 8 qubits as the output register (to store XOR results)
   - Use 8 classical bits to record the measurement of the output register.

2. **Prepare the Input States:**
   - For each bit in `a` (from least significant bit (LSB) to most significant bit (MSB)):
     - If the bit is 1, apply an X gate on the corresponding qubit in input register A to set it to |1>.
   - For each bit in `b` (LSB to MSB):
     - If the bit is 1, apply an X gate on the corresponding qubit in input register B.

3. **Apply XOR Operation Using Quantum Gates:**
   - The XOR operation on each bit pair can be implemented by applying a CNOT gate with control qubit from input register A and target qubit in the output register initialized to |0>.
   - Then apply a CNOT gate with control qubit from input register B and the same target qubit in the output register.
   - This sequence flips the target qubit if exactly one of the control qubits is |1>, effectively performing XOR.
   - Alternatively, use the `XOR` circuit from `qiskit.circuit.library` on each corresponding bit triplet (a_i, b_i, output_i) if available, but since `XOR` is for 2 qubits, manual CNOTs are clearer.

4. **Measurement:**
   - Measure all 8 output qubits into the 8 classical bits to read out the XOR result.
   - No need to measure input registers since we only want the XOR outputs.

5. **Simulation Setup:**
   - Use `AerSimulator` as the backend.
   - Use `Sampler` from `qiskit_ibm_runtime` initialized with the AerSimulator as backend.
   - Run the sampler with shots (e.g., 1024) to get counts of output bitstrings.

6. **Postprocessing:**
   - The measurement results will be bitstrings representing the XOR of the corresponding bits of `a` and `b`.
   - Return the counts dictionary as the function output.

7. **Expected Outcome:**
   - The output bitstrings correspond to the 8-bit XOR of inputs `a` and `b`.
   - The counts should show one dominant bitstring (the correct XOR result) with almost all the shots.

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The circuit correctly encodes input integers `a` and `b` as qubit states.
- The quantum operations correctly compute the bitwise XOR on the output register.
- Measurement results match the classical XOR bitwise operation on inputs.
- The returned counts dictionary reflects the expected XOR output with high probability.

This plan provides a clear step-by-step guide for a Qiskit developer to implement the `xor_gate` function.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def xor_gate(a: int, b: int) -> dict:
    """
    Given two 8-bit integers a and b, design a Quantum Circuit that acts as a classical XOR gate.
    Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and return the counts of the result.
    """
    # Number of bits
    n = 8

    # Create quantum registers:
    # - input register A (8 qubits)
    # - input register B (8 qubits)
    # - output register (8 qubits)
    qr_a = QuantumRegister(n, name='a')
    qr_b = QuantumRegister(n, name='b')
    qr_out = QuantumRegister(n, name='out')

    # Classical register to measure output qubits
    cr = ClassicalRegister(n, name='c')

    # Create quantum circuit with all registers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr_a, qr_b, qr_out, cr)

    # Prepare input states for register A based on bits of integer a
    for i in range(n):
        if (a >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qr_a[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Prepare input states for register B based on bits of integer b
    for i in range(n):
        if (b >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qr_b[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Apply XOR operation on each bit:
    # output qubit initialized to |0>
    # XOR = CNOT(a_i -> out_i) followed by CNOT(b_i -> out_i)
    for i in range(n):
        qc.cx(qr_a[i], qr_out[i])
        qc.cx(qr_b[i], qr_out[i])

    # Measure output register into classical bits
    qc.measure(qr_out, cr)

    # Setup AerSimulator backend
    backend = AerSimulator()

    # Setup Sampler with AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Run the sampler with 1024 shots
    job = sampler.run(qc, shots=1024)
    result = job.result()

    # Get counts of measurement results
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()

    return counts
```

```python
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def xor_gate(a: int, b: int) -> dict:
    """
    Given two 8-bit integers a and b, design a Quantum Circuit that acts as a classical XOR gate.
    Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and return the counts of the result.
    """
```
