```python
    n = 8

    # Create quantum registers:
    # - input register A (8 qubits)
    # - input register B (8 qubits)
    # - output register (8 qubits)
    qr_a = QuantumRegister(n, name='a')
    qr_b = QuantumRegister(n, name='b')
    qr_out = QuantumRegister(n, name='out')

    # Classical register to measure output qubits
    cr = ClassicalRegister(n, name='c')

    # Create quantum circuit with all registers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr_a, qr_b, qr_out, cr)

    # Prepare input states for register A based on bits of integer a
    for i in range(n):
        if (a >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qr_a[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Prepare input states for register B based on bits of integer b
    for i in range(n):
        if (b >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qr_b[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Apply XOR operation on each bit:
    # output qubit initialized to |0>
    # XOR = CNOT(a_i -> out_i) followed by CNOT(b_i -> out_i)
    for i in range(n):
        qc.cx(qr_a[i], qr_out[i])
        qc.cx(qr_b[i], qr_out[i])

    qc.measure(qr_out, cr)
```

1. **Qubit and Register Initialization**
   - Use 6 qubits to represent the input bits: 3 qubits for integer a and 3 qubits for integer b.
   - Use 3 additional qubits as output qubits to store the bitwise AND results.
   - Total qubits: 9 qubits.
   - Initialize a quantum circuit with these 9 qubits.
   - Initialize classical registers (3 bits) to store measurement results of the output qubits.

2. **Encoding the Input Integers**
   - For each bit of a (3 bits), if the bit is 1, apply an X gate to the corresponding input qubit to set it to |1⟩; else leave in |0⟩.
   - Repeat the same for integer b on its 3 qubits.

3. **Implementing the Bitwise AND operation**
   - For each bit position i (0 to 2):
     - Use a Toffoli (CCX) gate controlled by the a[i] and b[i] qubits targeting the output qubit i.
     - The Toffoli gate flips the output qubit if both inputs are |1⟩, effectively computing AND for that bit.
   - Initially, the output qubits should be initialized to |0⟩.

4. **Measurement**
   - Measure each output qubit into the corresponding classical bit to obtain the classical bitwise AND result.

5. **Simulation using Qiskit Sampler and AerSimulator**
   - Instantiate AerSimulator as the backend for the Sampler.
   - Execute the circuit using the Sampler.
   - Collect the counts of the measured bitstrings.

6. **Expected Output**
   - The counts dictionary should show the bitwise AND result of the two integers a and b.
   - The output bitstring corresponds to the 3-bit AND result, with counts indicating the measurement probability (should be deterministic here).

7. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - The circuit correctly encodes the input integers as qubits.
   - The Toffoli gates correctly compute the bitwise AND on each bit.
   - The measurement results correspond exactly to the classical AND of the inputs.
   - The simulation returns counts with 100% probability on the correct bitwise AND output.

Summary:
- 9 qubits: 3 for a, 3 for b, 3 for output
- 3 classical bits for measurement
- X gates to encode inputs
- Toffoli gates for AND operation
- Measurement of output qubits
- Simulation with Sampler and AerSimulator
- Return counts dictionary with AND result

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def and_gate(a: int, b: int) -> dict:
    """ Given two 3-bit integers a and b, design a quantum circuit that acts as a classical AND gate.
        Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and return the counts of the result.
    """
    # Validate inputs are 3-bit integers
    if not (0 <= a < 8) or not (0 <= b < 8):
        raise ValueError("Inputs must be 3-bit integers (0 to 7).")

    # Create quantum registers: 3 qubits for a, 3 for b, 3 for output
    qa = QuantumRegister(3, name='a')
    qb = QuantumRegister(3, name='b')
    qout = QuantumRegister(3, name='out')

    # Classical register for measurement of output qubits
    c = ClassicalRegister(3, name='c')

    # Create quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qa, qb, qout, c)

    # Encode input integer a into qubits qa
    for i in range(3):
        if (a >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qa[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Encode input integer b into qubits qb
    for i in range(3):
        if (b >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qb[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Apply Toffoli gates to compute bitwise AND into output qubits
    for i in range(3):
        qc.ccx(qa[i], qb[i], qout[i])

    # Measure output qubits into classical bits
    qc.measure(qout, c)

    # Setup AerSimulator backend
    backend = AerSimulator()

    # Setup Sampler with AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Run the circuit using sampler
    job = sampler.run(qc)
    result = job.result()

    # Get counts from the result
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()

    # Return counts dictionary
    return counts

from qiskit import QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister, QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
def or_gate(a: int, b: int)->dict:
    """ Given two 3-bit integers a and b, design a quantum circuit that acts as a classical OR gate. Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and return the counts of the result.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a quantum circuit that computes the classical OR operation on two 3-bit integers a and b, simulates it with Qiskit Sampler using the Aer simulator, and returns the measurement counts:

1. **Circuit and Qubit Setup**
   - Initialize a quantum circuit with:
     - 7 qubits total:
       - 3 qubits for the bits of integer `a` (input register A)
       - 3 qubits for the bits of integer `b` (input register B)
       - 1 ancilla/output qubit initialized to |0⟩, which will store the OR result for each corresponding bit position
     - 3 classical bits to store measurement results of the OR output bits

2. **Encoding Input Bits**
   - For each bit in `a` (3 bits):
     - If the bit is 1, apply an X (NOT) gate to the corresponding qubit in register A to set it to |1⟩
   - Similarly, for each bit in `b` (3 bits):
     - If the bit is 1, apply an X gate to the corresponding qubit in register B

3. **Computing OR for Each Bit Position**
   - For each bit position i (0 to 2):
     - Use a multi-controlled operation to compute the OR of a_i and b_i and store it into the output qubit i
     - Since OR(a,b) = NOT(AND(NOT a, NOT b)), implement the OR using De Morgan's law:
       - Apply X gates to qubits a_i and b_i (to flip input bits)
       - Apply a Toffoli (CCX) gate controlled on the flipped a_i and b_i qubits targeting the output qubit i
       - Flip the output qubit i with an X gate to get the OR result
       - Revert the input qubits a_i and b_i back by applying X gates to restore their original states
     - Alternatively, if you want to store the OR result directly into a fresh ancilla qubit per bit, allocate 3 output qubits, one per bit

4. **Measurement**
   - Measure the 3 output qubits (storing the OR results for each bit) into the 3 classical bits

5. **Simulation**
   - Use the Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator as backend to simulate the circuit
   - Run the circuit with a sufficient number of shots (e.g., 1024) to get measurement statistics

6. **Expected Output**
   - A dictionary with counts for the measured 3-bit OR result string (e.g., {'101': 1024})
   - Since inputs are classical and the circuit is deterministic, expect a single output bitstring representing the bitwise OR of inputs `a` and `b`

7. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - For given inputs a and b (each 3 bits), the circuit returns measurement counts with a single key corresponding to bitwise OR(a,b)
   - The counts sum to the total number of shots
   - The implementation uses Qiskit Sampler with the AerSimulator backend
   - The circuit correctly encodes inputs and computes the OR operation for each bit with proper measurement and outputs

This plan assumes that the OR operation is performed bitwise for the 3 bits of each integer input, producing a 3-bit output. Each output bit is the classical OR of the corresponding bits in a and b.

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def or_gate(a: int, b: int) -> dict:
    """
    Given two 3-bit integers a and b, design a quantum circuit that acts as a classical OR gate.
    Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with the Aer simulator as backend and return the counts of the result.
    """
    # Number of bits
    n_bits = 3

    # Create quantum registers:
    # - 3 qubits for input a
    # - 3 qubits for input b
    # - 3 qubits for output (OR results)
    qa = QuantumRegister(n_bits, name='a')
    qb = QuantumRegister(n_bits, name='b')
    qout = QuantumRegister(n_bits, name='out')

    # Classical register to store measurement results of output qubits
    c = ClassicalRegister(n_bits, name='c')

    # Create quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qa, qb, qout, c)

    # Encode input bits for a
    for i in range(n_bits):
        if (a >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qa[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Encode input bits for b
    for i in range(n_bits):
        if (b >> i) & 1:
            qc.x(qb[i])  # Set qubit to |1> if bit is 1

    # Compute OR for each bit position using De Morgan's law:
    # OR(a,b) = NOT(AND(NOT a, NOT b))
    for i in range(n_bits):
        # Flip inputs
        qc.x(qa[i])
        qc.x(qb[i])

        # Toffoli gate with controls qa[i], qb[i] and target qout[i]
        qc.ccx(qa[i], qb[i], qout[i])

        # Flip output qubit to get OR result
        qc.x(qout[i])

        # Revert input flips
        qc.x(qa[i])
        qc.x(qb[i])

    # Measure output qubits
    qc.measure(qout, c)

    # Setup AerSimulator backend
    backend = AerSimulator()

    # Setup Sampler with AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Run the circuit with 1024 shots
    # Sampler returns quasi-distribution, convert to counts
    job = sampler.run(qc, shots=1024)
    result = job.result()

    # Get quasi-distribution of measurement outcomes
    quasi_distr = result.quasi_dists[0]

    # Convert quasi-distribution to counts dictionary
    counts = {}
    for bitstring, prob in quasi_distr.items():
        # Convert probability to counts by multiplying by shots and rounding
        counts[bitstring] = int(round(prob * 1024))

    return counts
```

Plan to implement the classical NOT gate on an 8-bit integer using Qiskit and simulate it with the Aer simulator via Qiskit Sampler:

1. **Initialize Quantum Circuit**
   - Create a quantum circuit with 8 qubits, each representing one bit of the input integer.
   - Also create 8 classical bits for measurement.

2. **Input Encoding**
   - Convert the input integer `a` (0 to 255) into its 8-bit binary representation.
   - For each bit that is 1, apply an X gate to the corresponding qubit to set it to |1⟩.
   - For bits that are 0, leave the qubit in the |0⟩ state (default).

3. **Apply NOT Gate**
   - Apply an X gate to each qubit. Since X gate flips |0⟩ to |1⟩ and vice versa, this implements a classical bitwise NOT operation on the 8-bit input.

4. **Measurement**
   - Measure all 8 qubits into the corresponding classical bits.

5. **Simulation Setup**
   - Use the AerSimulator backend with Qiskit Sampler runtime interface to execute the circuit.
   - Run the circuit once (or a small number of shots, e.g., 1 or 1024) since the operation is deterministic.

6. **Result Processing**
   - Obtain the counts of the measurement results.
   - The measurement outcome bitstring will represent the bitwise NOT of the input integer.
   - Return the counts dictionary mapping measured bitstrings to their occurrence counts.

7. **Expected Outcome**
   - The output bitstring should be the binary complement of the input integer.
   - For example, if input `a` = 5 (00000101), output should be 250 (11111010).

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The function accepts an integer `a` in the 0 to 255 range.
- The quantum circuit correctly encodes `a` into qubits.
- The circuit applies NOT gates to all qubits.
- Measurements correctly reflect the bitwise NOT of `a`.
- The simulation runs on AerSimulator using Qiskit Sampler.
- The returned counts dictionary correctly maps the NOTed bitstring to the number of shots.
- The solution handles edge cases such as 0 (all zeros) and 255 (all ones) correctly.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def not_gate(a: int) -> dict:
    """
    Given an 8-bit integer 'a', create a quantum circuit that performs a classical bitwise NOT operation.
    Simulate the circuit using Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator backend and return the counts of the result.

    Args:
        a (int): An integer between 0 and 255 inclusive.

    Returns:
        dict: Counts dictionary mapping bitstrings (as measurement results) to their occurrence counts.
    """
    # Validate input
    if not (0 <= a <= 255):
        raise ValueError("Input integer must be between 0 and 255 inclusive.")

    # Number of bits/qubits
    n = 8

    # Create quantum circuit with 8 qubits and 8 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n, n)

    # Convert input integer to 8-bit binary string, MSB on left
    bin_str = format(a, '08b')

    # Initialize qubits according to input bits
    # Qiskit qubit 0 is the leftmost qubit in the circuit diagram,
    # but measurement results are returned with qubit 0 as the rightmost bit.
    # To keep consistency, we map bit 0 (MSB) to qubit 7, bit 7 (LSB) to qubit 0.
    # So we reverse the bit order when applying gates.
    for i, bit in enumerate(reversed(bin_str)):
        if bit == '1':
            qc.x(i)  # Set qubit i to |1>

    # Apply NOT gate (X) to all qubits to flip bits
    for i in range(n):
        qc.x(i)

    # Measure all qubits into classical bits
    qc.measure(range(n), range(n))

    # Setup AerSimulator backend
    backend = AerSimulator()

    # Setup Qiskit Sampler with AerSimulator backend
    sampler = Sampler(backend=backend)

    # Run the circuit with 1024 shots for statistics
    job = sampler.run(qc, shots=1024)
    result = job.result()

    # Get counts dictionary from result
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()

    # Convert probabilities to counts by multiplying by shots and rounding
    counts_dict = {bitstr: int(round(prob * 1024)) for bitstr, prob in counts.items()}

    return counts_dict
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
```

def create_swap_gate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Design a SWAP gate using only CX gates."""
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply the standard SWAP decomposition using 3 CX gates
    qc.cx(0, 1)  # CNOT with qubit 0 as control and qubit 1 as target
    qc.cx(1, 0)  # CNOT with qubit 1 as control and qubit 0 as target
    qc.cx(0, 1)  # CNOT with qubit 0 as control and qubit 1 as target
    
    return qc

# Test the function by creating the circuit and drawing it
if __name__ == "__main__":
    swap_circuit = create_swap_gate()
    print(swap_circuit.draw())

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a Controlled-Hadamard (CH) gate using CX and RY gates in Qiskit:

1. Circuit Initialization:
   - Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits:
     - Qubit 0: control qubit
     - Qubit 1: target qubit

2. Decompose the Hadamard gate on the target qubit using RY and other gates:
   - Recall that the Hadamard gate H can be decomposed as:
     H = RY(π/2) · RZ(π) · RY(π/2)
   - Since the user only wants to use CX and RY, and RZ is not allowed, use an equivalent decomposition:
     - Alternatively, express H as RY(π/2) · RX(π) (RX can be expressed as a combination of RY and CX, but since only CX and RY are allowed, we need to find a suitable decomposition)
   - Another common decomposition of H is:
     H = RZ(π) · RY(π/2) · RX(π)
   - Since only CX and RY are allowed, and no RX or RZ, we must build RX from RY and CX or find an alternative approach. 
   - The known decomposition of RX using RY and CX is not straightforward without additional gates, so instead, use the following standard decomposition for CH gate from literature:
     CH = (I ⊗ RY(π/4)) · CX · (I ⊗ RY(-π/4)) · CX

3. Circuit Construction:
   - Apply RY(π/4) to the target qubit
   - Apply CX (control qubit → target qubit)
   - Apply RY(-π/4) to the target qubit
   - Apply CX again (control qubit → target qubit)

4. Measurement:
   - No measurements are required in the gate construction function itself.
   - Measurements can be added externally if the user wants to test the gate.

5. Expected Outcome:
   - The resulting 2-qubit unitary implements the Controlled-Hadamard operation:
     - When control qubit is |0>, target qubit is unchanged
     - When control qubit is |1>, target qubit undergoes a Hadamard gate

Acceptance Criteria:
- The function returns a QuantumCircuit with 2 qubits
- The circuit implements the CH gate using only CX and RY gates as described
- No other gate types (e.g., RX, RZ, H) are used explicitly
- The control qubit is qubit 0, and target qubit is qubit 1
- The circuit can be used as a subroutine or unitary in a larger circuit

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import pi

def create_ch_gate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Design a Controlled-Hadamard (CH) gate using only CX and RY gates.
    
    The decomposition used is:
    CH = (I ⊗ RY(π/4)) · CX · (I ⊗ RY(-π/4)) · CX
    
    Qubit 0: control qubit
    Qubit 1: target qubit
    """
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    # Apply RY(π/4) to target qubit
    qc.ry(pi/4, 1)
    # Apply CX with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    # Apply RY(-π/4) to target qubit
    qc.ry(-pi/4, 1)
    # Apply CX again
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    return qc

# Test the function by creating the circuit and drawing it
if __name__ == "__main__":
    ch_circuit = create_ch_gate()
    print(ch_circuit.draw())

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_cz_gate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Design a CZ gate using only H and CNOT gates and return the quantum circuit.
    
    The CZ gate is decomposed as:
    CZ = H(target) -> CNOT(control, target) -> H(target)
    """
    # Initialize a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on the target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.h(1)
    
    # Apply CNOT with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate again on the target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.h(1)
    
    return qc
```

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
def create_cy_gate()->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Design a CY gate using only one CX gate and any other single qubit gates.

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to design a controlled-Y (CY) gate using only one CX gate and single-qubit gates:

1. **Initialize the circuit:**
   - Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits.
   - Qubit 0: control qubit
   - Qubit 1: target qubit (the qubit on which the Y operation is conditionally applied)

2. **Decompose the CY gate:**
   - The CY gate applies a Y gate on the target qubit if the control qubit is |1>.
   - The Y gate can be represented as Y = S† X S, where S is the phase gate and S† is its inverse.
   - Using this, CY can be constructed as: CY = (I ⊗ S†) · CX · (I ⊗ S)
     - Apply S† (inverse S gate) on the target qubit.
     - Apply CX with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1.
     - Apply S gate on the target qubit.

3. **Circuit structure and gates:**
   - Apply S† gate on qubit 1 (target).
   - Apply CX gate with qubit 0 as control and qubit 1 as target.
   - Apply S gate on qubit 1 (target).

4. **Measurement:**
   - No measurement needed for gate construction.
   - Measurements can be added later based on user needs.

5. **Expected outcome:**
   - The constructed circuit behaves as a controlled-Y gate.
   - When control qubit is |1>, the Y gate is applied on the target qubit.
   - When control qubit is |0>, the target qubit remains unchanged.

6. **Acceptance criteria:**
   - The circuit uses exactly one CX gate.
   - Only single-qubit gates other than CX are S and S†.
   - The resulting unitary matrix matches the standard CY gate matrix.
   - The circuit operates correctly on arbitrary input states, verified by simulation or unitary comparison.

This plan enables the Qiskit developer to implement the CY gate as requested.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_cy_gate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Design a controlled-Y (CY) gate using only one CX gate and single-qubit gates.
    CY = (I ⊗ S†) · CX · (I ⊗ S)
    
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A 2-qubit circuit implementing the CY gate.
    """
    # Create a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply S† (inverse S) gate on target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.sdg(1)
    
    # Apply CX gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    
    # Apply S gate on target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.s(1)
    
    return qc

# Example usage:
# cy_circuit = create_cy_gate()
# print(cy_circuit.draw())

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_cy_gate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Design a controlled-Y (CY) gate using only one CX gate and single-qubit gates.
    CY = (I ⊗ S†) · CX · (I ⊗ S)
    
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A 2-qubit circuit implementing the CY gate.
    """
    # Create a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply S† (inverse S) gate on target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.sdg(1)
    
    # Apply CX gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    
    # Apply S gate on target qubit (qubit 1)
    qc.s(1)
    
    return qc

# Example usage:
# cy_circuit = create_cy_gate()
# print(cy_circuit.draw())

from qiskit import QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister, QuantumCircuit

def create_quantum_circuit_with_one_qubit_and_measure():
    """ Build a Quantum Circuit by first creating one Quantum Register and one Classical Register and then perform measurement on it.
    """
    # Step 1: Create one Quantum Register with 1 qubit
    qreg = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    # Step 1: Create one Classical Register with 1 bit
    creg = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c')
    
    # Step 2: Initialize Quantum Circuit with the created registers
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(qreg, creg)
    
    # Step 3: Add measurement from the qubit to the classical bit
    circuit.measure(qreg[0], creg[0])
    
    # Step 4: Return the constructed circuit
    return circuit

    """ Construct a BB84 protocol circuit for the sender, inputting both the states and the measured bases.
    """

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the BB84 sender's circuit function in Qiskit:

1. **Initialize circuit and qubits**
   - Create a QuantumCircuit with a number of qubits equal to the length of the input `state` list.
   - No classical bits are strictly required for this sender circuit construction, since measurement is typically done by the receiver. However, if measurements are performed here, classical bits will be needed.

2. **Encode states based on input bits and bases**
   - Inputs:
     - `state`: list of bits (0 or 1) representing the bit values to send.
     - `basis`: list of bits (0 or 1) representing the basis in which to encode each bit:
       - 0 for computational (Z) basis
       - 1 for Hadamard (X) basis
   - For each qubit index `i`:
     - Initialize qubit to |0⟩ by default.
     - If `state[i] == 1`, apply an X gate to prepare |1⟩.
     - If `basis[i] == 1` (Hadamard basis), apply an H gate to move the qubit into the X-basis.
       - This transforms |0⟩ -> |+⟩ and |1⟩ -> |−⟩.
   - This produces the BB84 encoded qubits ready for transmission.

3. **Measurement**
   - Typically, the sender does not measure the qubits in BB84; measurement is performed by the receiver.
   - Therefore, omit measurement operations in this circuit.
   - If required for simulation or testing, measurement can be added to classical bits, but it is not standard for the sender.

4. **Return the constructed QuantumCircuit**
   - The circuit should represent the prepared qubits with the BB84 encoding.
   - No measurement results are included in this circuit.

**Acceptance criteria:**
- The function accepts two lists of equal length: `state` and `basis`.
- The returned QuantumCircuit has a qubit for each bit in `state`.
- Each qubit is prepared as |0⟩ or |1⟩ according to `state`.
- Each qubit is rotated into the Hadamard basis if `basis` is 1.
- The circuit contains no measurements.
- The function is documented clearly with input/output expectations.

This plan provides a clear, step-by-step approach to implement the BB84 sender's circuit in Qiskit.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def bb84_senders_circuit(state: [int], basis: [int]) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Construct a BB84 protocol circuit for the sender, encoding bits into qubits
    according to the specified states and bases.

    Args:
        state (list of int): List of bits (0 or 1) representing the bit values to send.
        basis (list of int): List of bits (0 or 1) representing the basis for each bit:
                             0 for computational (Z) basis,
                             1 for Hadamard (X) basis.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit with qubits prepared in BB84 states.
    """
    if len(state) != len(basis):
        raise ValueError("state and basis lists must have the same length")

    n = len(state)
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)

    for i in range(n):
        # Prepare |1> if state bit is 1, else |0> by default
        if state[i] == 1:
            qc.x(i)
        # Apply Hadamard if basis is 1 to encode in X basis
        if basis[i] == 1:
            qc.h(i)

    return qc
```

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function bb84_circuit_generate_key(senders_basis: [int], circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> str:

1. Inputs:
   - senders_basis: a list of integers where each element is either 0 or 1, representing the basis used by the sender for each qubit (0 = computational/Z basis, 1 = Hadamard/X basis).
   - circuit: a QuantumCircuit instance representing the BB84 states prepared and measured by the receiver.

2. Objective:
   - To extract the raw key bits from the measurement results of the circuit in the BB84 protocol, taking into account the sender’s chosen bases.
   - The function assumes the circuit contains the measurement operations and is ready to be executed on a simulator or backend.

3. Implementation steps:

   a) Circuit Execution:
      - Execute the provided QuantumCircuit on a suitable simulator backend such as AerSimulator or a real quantum device.
      - Use a sampler or simulator to obtain measurement results.
      - Obtain the counts or measurement outcomes as bitstrings.

   b) Measurement Result Processing:
      - The measurement outcomes represent the receiver’s measurement results, in some basis.
      - Since the sender’s basis is known (senders_basis list), compare the sender’s basis with the receiver’s measurement basis to identify which bits are valid.
      - Typically, in BB84, the receiver randomly chooses basis; here since only sender’s basis is given, it is assumed the circuit corresponds to receiver’s measurement in some basis.
      - For bits where the sender and receiver basis match, the measurement results are used to form the key.
      - For bits where the bases differ, the bits are discarded.

   c) Extracting the Key:
      - For each qubit index i:
         - If senders_basis[i] matches receiver’s measurement basis for qubit i:
            - Extract the i-th bit from the measurement result bitstring.
            - Append this bit to the raw key string.
         - Else:
            - Discard the bit.

   d) Return the raw key string as the output.

4. Required quantum gates and operations:
   - None specifically inside this function, as this function processes the existing circuit and measurement results.
   - The circuit is expected to have been prepared with BB84 states and measurement operations already applied.

5. Circuit structure and qubit requirements:
   - The circuit should have as many qubits as the length of senders_basis.
   - Each qubit is prepared by the sender in the basis specified in senders_basis.
   - The receiver’s measurement basis is encoded in the circuit’s measurement operations.

6. Measurement strategy:
   - The circuit should perform measurements on all qubits, recording results in classical bits.
   - The measurement results are obtained as bitstrings from the execution.

7. Expected outcomes:
   - The function returns a string of bits representing the raw key bits where the sender’s and receiver’s bases matched.
   - Length of the key ≤ length of senders_basis depending on basis agreement.

8. Acceptance criteria:
   - The function runs the circuit and obtains measurement results correctly.
   - It correctly identifies matching bases between sender and receiver.
   - It extracts and returns the raw key bits as a string.
   - The function handles cases where no bases match (returns empty string).
   - The function is robust to input length mismatches or invalid inputs (optional).

Summary:

- Run the quantum circuit to get measurement results.
- Compare sender's basis with receiver's measurement basis.
- Extract bits where bases match to form the key.
- Return the key as a bit string.

This plan provides a clear approach for a Qiskit developer to implement bb84_circuit_generate_key.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, transpile
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler

def bb84_circuit_generate_key(senders_basis: [int], circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> str:
    """
    Generate the raw key from the BB84 protocol by executing the given circuit and comparing
    sender's basis with receiver's measurement basis.

    Args:
        senders_basis (list[int]): List of 0 or 1 indicating sender's basis for each qubit
                                   (0 = computational/Z basis, 1 = Hadamard/X basis).
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): QuantumCircuit prepared with BB84 states and measurement.

    Returns:
        str: Raw key bits as a string where sender's and receiver's bases matched.
    """
    # Number of qubits
    n = len(senders_basis)

    # Check circuit qubit count matches sender's basis length
    if circuit.num_qubits != n:
        raise ValueError("Circuit qubit count does not match length of sender's basis list.")

    # Use AerSimulator backend for execution
    simulator = AerSimulator()

    # Transpile circuit for simulator
    transpiled_circuit = transpile(circuit, simulator)

    # Execute the circuit with 1 shot to get a single measurement outcome
    result = simulator.run(transpiled_circuit, shots=1).result()

    # Get counts dictionary: {bitstring: count}
    counts = result.get_counts()

    # Extract the single bitstring result (only one shot)
    measured_bitstring = list(counts.keys())[0]

    # Qiskit returns bitstrings with qubit 0 as rightmost bit, so reverse to align indices
    measured_bitstring = measured_bitstring[::-1]

    # Extract raw key bits where sender's and receiver's bases match
    raw_key_bits = []
    for i in range(n):
        # Receiver's measurement basis is inferred from circuit:
        # If the circuit measured in computational basis, receiver basis = 0
        # If the circuit applied H before measurement, receiver basis = 1
        # Since we don't have receiver basis input, we infer it by checking if the circuit
        # applied H before measurement on each qubit.
        # For simplicity, assume receiver basis = 0 (computational) for all qubits.
        # If receiver basis is known, replace this logic accordingly.

        receiver_basis = 0  # Assumed receiver basis for all qubits

        if senders_basis[i] == receiver_basis:
            raw_key_bits.append(measured_bitstring[i])

    # Join bits to form the raw key string
    raw_key = ''.join(raw_key_bits)

    return raw_key
```

=== [65/151] qiskitHumanEval/64 :: simons_algorithm ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister
def simons_algorithm(s: str)->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Write a function that takes the bitstring 's' as the input and builds a Quantum Circuit such that the output when xor-ed with the input 's' is same as the 's'. When building the quantum circuit make sure the classical registers is named 'c'.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the simons_algorithm function based on the user's request:

1. Understanding the task:
   - Input: A bitstring 's' of length n.
   - Goal: Build a quantum circuit where the output, when XOR-ed with 's', equals 's'.
   - This is related to Simon's problem where the function f(x) = f(x ⊕ s), and s is the secret string.
   - The circuit should have classical registers named 'c'.

2. Key points:
   - The circuit must have 2n qubits: n input qubits and n output qubits (as in Simon's problem standard setup).
   - Classical register size should be n (for measurement of input register).
   - The oracle implementing f(x) such that f(x) = f(x ⊕ s) must be built inside the circuit.
   - The oracle is a black-box function; the user wants a circuit that "when output is XORed with s, the output is s."
   - The circuit should prepare the initial state, apply Hadamards, apply oracle, apply Hadamards, measure, as in Simon's algorithm.

3. Detailed steps for implementation:

Step 1: Quantum and Classical Registers Initialization
- Determine n = length of s.
- Create two Quantum Registers:
  - qr1: n qubits (input register)
  - qr2: n qubits (output register)
- Create Classical Register c with n bits.
- Initialize Quantum Circuit with qr1, qr2, and c.

Step 2: Initialize output register to |0⟩ state (default).
Step 3: Apply Hadamard gates on all qr1 qubits to create superposition.
Step 4: Oracle Implementation
- The oracle U_f acts as |x⟩|y⟩ → |x⟩|y ⊕ f(x)⟩
- f(x) is a 2-to-1 function with period s.
- For Simon's oracle related to s, implement f(x) = f(x ⊕ s).
- Construct oracle such that for each qubit i where s_i=1, apply CNOT from qr1 qubit i to qr2 qubit i.
- This simulates adding s to the output register conditioned on input.
- For bits where s_i=0, the output qubits remain unchanged (or set to some function).
- This construction ensures f(x) = f(x ⊕ s).

Step 5: Apply Hadamard gates on qr1 again.
Step 6: Measure qr1 into classical register c.

4. Expected outcomes:
- The measurement results will give bitstrings y such that y · s = 0 (dot product mod 2).
- Repeated execution and classical post-processing (not required here) can determine s.
- The output of the circuit, when XORed with s, will equal s.

5. Acceptance Criteria:
- The function accepts a bitstring s.
- The circuit has 2n qubits and n classical bits named 'c'.
- The circuit applies Hadamards before and after the oracle.
- The oracle implements the function f(x) = f(x ⊕ s) by applying CNOT gates from input to output qubits according to s.
- Measurement is performed on the input register into the classical register 'c'.
- No actual classical post-processing is required.
- The circuit structure matches Simon's algorithm template with the given oracle.

Summary:
- Input: bitstring s
- Output: QuantumCircuit implementing Simon's algorithm oracle for secret s
- Registers: qr1 (input), qr2 (output), c (classical), all properly sized
- Gates: Hadamards on qr1, oracle CNOTs according to s, Hadamards on qr1, measurement
- Measurement: qr1 → c

This plan is detailed for a Qiskit developer to implement simons_algorithm(s).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister

def simons_algorithm(s: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Constructs a quantum circuit implementing Simon's algorithm oracle for secret string s.
    The circuit has 2n qubits (input and output registers) and n classical bits named 'c'.
    The oracle implements f(x) = f(x ⊕ s) by applying CNOT gates from input to output qubits according to s.
    Measures the input register into classical register 'c'.
    
    Args:
        s (str): The secret bitstring s.
        
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The constructed quantum circuit.
    """
    n = len(s)
    # Create quantum registers: input and output
    qr1 = QuantumRegister(n, name='qr1')  # input register
    qr2 = QuantumRegister(n, name='qr2')  # output register
    # Create classical register named 'c'
    c = ClassicalRegister(n, name='c')
    
    # Initialize quantum circuit with the registers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr1, qr2, c)
    
    # Step 1: Apply Hadamard gates to input register to create superposition
    qc.h(qr1)
    
    # Step 2: Oracle implementation
    # For each bit i where s_i == '1', apply CNOT from qr1[i] to qr2[i]
    for i, bit in enumerate(s):
        if bit == '1':
            qc.cx(qr1[i], qr2[i])
    
    # Step 3: Apply Hadamard gates to input register again
    qc.h(qr1)
    
    # Step 4: Measure input register into classical register 'c'
    qc.measure(qr1, c)
    
    return qc

# Example usage:
# circuit = simons_algorithm('110')
# print(circuit.draw())

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister

def simons_algorithm(s: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Constructs a quantum circuit implementing Simon's algorithm oracle for secret string s.
    The circuit has 2n qubits (input and output registers) and n classical bits named 'c'.
    The oracle implements f(x) = f(x ⊕ s) by applying CNOT gates from input to output qubits according to s.
    Measures the input register into classical register 'c'.
    
    Args:
        s (str): The secret bitstring s.
        
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The constructed quantum circuit.
    """
    n = len(s)
    # Create quantum registers: input and output
    qr1 = QuantumRegister(n, name='qr1')  # input register
    qr2 = QuantumRegister(n, name='qr2')  # output register
    # Create classical register named 'c'
    c = ClassicalRegister(n, name='c')
    
    # Initialize quantum circuit with the registers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr1, qr2, c)
    
    # Step 1: Apply Hadamard gates to input register to create superposition
    qc.h(qr1)
    
    # Step 2: Oracle implementation
    # For each bit i where s_i == '1', apply CNOT from qr1[i] to qr2[i]
    for i, bit in enumerate(s):
        if bit == '1':
            qc.cx(qr1[i], qr2[i])
    
    # Step 3: Apply Hadamard gates to input register again
    qc.h(qr1)
    
    # Step 4: Measure input register into classical register 'c'
    qc.measure(qr1, c)
    
    return qc

=== [66/151] qiskitHumanEval/65 :: QFT ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import pi
def QFT(n: int)->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Design a Quantum Fourier Transform circuit for n qubits using basic Quantum gates.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing a Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit for n qubits:

1. Circuit Initialization
   - Create a quantum circuit with n qubits.
   - Optionally, include n classical bits if measurement is needed later (not mandatory for just QFT circuit design).

2. Apply the QFT gates to the qubits
   - For each qubit i from 0 to n-1 (starting from the most significant qubit):
     a. Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to qubit i.
     b. For each qubit j from i+1 to n-1:
        - Apply a controlled phase rotation (CRk) gate controlled by qubit j and targeting qubit i.
        - The rotation angle for CRk is θ = π / 2^(j - i).
        - In Qiskit, this corresponds to a controlled RZ or controlled U1 gate with angle θ.
   - Note: The controlled phase gates are applied in order from the closest to the farthest qubit.

3. Optional: Apply a qubit swap layer
   - The QFT circuit typically ends with swapping qubit i and qubit n-1-i for i in [0, n/2).
   - This reverses the order of qubits to match the standard QFT output.
   - Swaps can be implemented using three CNOT gates or Qiskit's swap instruction.

4. Measurement (optional)
   - Add measurement instructions if the circuit output needs to be measured.
   - Measure each qubit into a classical bit (if classical register was added).

5. Expected outcome
   - The circuit implements the QFT unitary transformation on an n-qubit input state.
   - If applied to computational basis states, the output is the Fourier transformed state.
   - The circuit depth scales approximately as O(n^2) due to the controlled phase rotations.

Acceptance criteria:
- The circuit uses n qubits.
- The correct sequence of Hadamard and controlled phase rotation gates is applied.
- The rotation angles for controlled phase gates are correct (π / 2^(k)).
- The qubit swap stage is included to reverse qubit order.
- The circuit can be executed on a simulator or real device to verify QFT behavior.
- The circuit is modular and parameterized by n.

This plan provides a clear structure for a developer to implement the QFT function in Qiskit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import pi

def QFT(n: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Design a Quantum Fourier Transform circuit for n qubits using basic Quantum gates."""
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)
    
    # Apply QFT
    for i in range(n):
        # Apply Hadamard gate to qubit i
        qc.h(i)
        # Apply controlled phase rotations
        for j in range(i+1, n):
            # Calculate the rotation angle
            angle = pi / (2 ** (j - i))
            # Apply controlled RZ rotation with control qubit j and target qubit i
            qc.cp(angle, j, i)
    
    # Swap qubits to reverse the order
    for i in range(n // 2):
        qc.swap(i, n - i - 1)
    
    return qc

# Example usage:
# qc = QFT(3)
# print(qc.draw())

def w_state()->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Generate a Quantum Circuit for a W state and measure it.

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement a Qiskit function that generates a W state and measures it:

1. **Initialize the Quantum Circuit**
   - Number of qubits: 3 (standard minimal size for a W state)
   - Number of classical bits: 3 (to store measurement results)

2. **Prepare the W state \(|W\rangle = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|100\rangle + |010\rangle + |001\rangle)\)**
   - The W state is an equal superposition of single-excitation states.
   - Use a sequence of controlled and single-qubit rotations to distribute the amplitude appropriately.
   - One common approach:
     - Start with \(|000\rangle\).
     - Apply a rotation Ry(2 * arccos(1/sqrt(3))) on the first qubit to prepare the amplitude distribution between \(|000\rangle\) and \(|100\rangle\).
     - Use controlled operations (CNOTs and Ry rotations) on subsequent qubits to transfer amplitude from \(|000\rangle\) to \(|010\rangle\) and \(|001\rangle\).
   - This procedure requires:
     - Ry rotations
     - CNOT gates

3. **Measurement**
   - Measure all three qubits into the corresponding classical bits.

4. **Expected outcome**
   - Measurement results should predominantly be one of the three states: '100', '010', or '001' with approximately equal probability (~1/3 each).
   - Other outcomes should have zero or negligible probability.

5. **Acceptance criteria**
   - The circuit must have 3 qubits and 3 classical bits.
   - The circuit prepares the W state on the qubits.
   - All qubits are measured.
   - Running the circuit on a simulator should yield measurement counts close to equal distribution among '100', '010', and '001'.

This design plan provides a clear recipe for a Qiskit developer to implement the W state generation and measurement.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import arccos, sqrt

def w_state() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Generate a Quantum Circuit for a 3-qubit W state and measure all qubits.

    The W state is |W> = (|100> + |010> + |001>) / sqrt(3).

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The circuit preparing the W state and measuring all qubits.
    """
    # Number of qubits and classical bits
    n = 3
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n, n)

    # Calculate angles for Ry rotations
    theta1 = 2 * arccos(1 / sqrt(3))  # For first qubit
    theta2 = 2 * arccos(1 / sqrt(2))  # For second qubit

    # Step 1: Rotate qubit 0 to create superposition between |000> and |100>
    qc.ry(theta1, 0)

    # Step 2: Use CNOT from qubit 0 to qubit 1 and rotate qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    qc.ry(theta2, 1)

    # Step 3: Use CNOT from qubit 1 to qubit 2
    qc.cx(1, 2)

    # Measure all qubits
    qc.measure(range(n), range(n))

    return qc
```

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import pi
def chsh_circuit(alice: int, bob: int)->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Design a CHSH circuit that takes bits of Alice and Bob as input and return the Quantum Circuit after measuring.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the CHSH game quantum circuit function `chsh_circuit(alice: int, bob: int) -> QuantumCircuit`:

1. **Initialize the Quantum Circuit:**
   - Use 2 qubits: qubit 0 for Alice, qubit 1 for Bob.
   - Use 2 classical bits for measurement results: one for Alice, one for Bob.

2. **Prepare the Initial State (Bell state):**
   - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) to qubit 0.
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1.
   - This prepares the Bell state (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2.

3. **Apply Measurement Basis Rotations Depending on Alice's Input Bit:**
   - Alice's input bit (0 or 1) determines the measurement basis applied to qubit 0.
   - If Alice's bit is 0: apply rotation corresponding to measurement angle 0 (Z-basis).
   - If Alice's bit is 1: apply rotation corresponding to measurement angle π/4.
   - This can be implemented as a rotation around the Y-axis by angle `-2 * theta`, where theta = 0 or π/8 (since π/4 = 2*(π/8)). A more direct approach is to apply an Ry rotation with the appropriate angle to align the measurement basis.
   - Specifically:
     - For Alice bit 0: rotate qubit 0 by 0 (no rotation).
     - For Alice bit 1: rotate qubit 0 by -π/4 around Y-axis.

4. **Apply Measurement Basis Rotations Depending on Bob's Input Bit:**
   - Bob's input bit (0 or 1) determines the measurement basis applied to qubit 1.
   - Bob's measurement angles are π/8 and -π/8 for bits 0 and 1 respectively.
   - Implement as rotations around Y-axis:
     - For Bob bit 0: rotate qubit 1 by -π/8.
     - For Bob bit 1: rotate qubit 1 by π/8.

5. **Measurement:**
   - Measure qubit 0 into classical bit 0.
   - Measure qubit 1 into classical bit 1.

6. **Expected Outcome:**
   - The circuit measures correlations between Alice and Bob's measurement results conditioned on their input bits.
   - When run multiple times, the measurement statistics should violate the CHSH inequality, demonstrating quantum nonlocality.

7. **Acceptance Criteria:**
   - The circuit uses 2 qubits and 2 classical bits.
   - The Bell state is prepared correctly.
   - Measurement basis rotations correspond correctly to Alice and Bob's input bits.
   - Measurements are performed on both qubits.
   - The returned QuantumCircuit object includes all these steps.

This plan enables a Qiskit developer to implement the `chsh_circuit` function which constructs the CHSH quantum game circuit based on the inputs from Alice and Bob.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from numpy import pi

def chsh_circuit(alice: int, bob: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Design a CHSH circuit that takes bits of Alice and Bob as input and returns the Quantum Circuit after measuring.
    
    Parameters:
        alice (int): Alice's input bit (0 or 1)
        bob (int): Bob's input bit (0 or 1)
        
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The constructed quantum circuit for the CHSH game
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits and 2 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)
    
    # Prepare the Bell state (|00> + |11>)/sqrt(2)
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    
    # Apply measurement basis rotation for Alice's qubit (qubit 0)
    # Alice's measurement angles: 0 for bit 0, -pi/4 for bit 1
    if alice == 1:
        qc.ry(-pi/4, 0)
    # if alice == 0, no rotation needed
    
    # Apply measurement basis rotation for Bob's qubit (qubit 1)
    # Bob's measurement angles: -pi/8 for bit 0, pi/8 for bit 1
    if bob == 0:
        qc.ry(-pi/8, 1)
    else:
        qc.ry(pi/8, 1)
    
    # Measure both qubits
    qc.measure(0, 0)  # Measure qubit 0 into classical bit 0
    qc.measure(1, 1)  # Measure qubit 1 into classical bit 1
    
    return qc
```

=== [69/151] qiskitHumanEval/68 :: zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from numpy import pi
def zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(bomb_live: bool)->(float, float, float):
    """ Design a Zeno Elitzur Vaidman Bomb Tester circuit which takes the boolean if the bomb is live and outputs the percentage of successful live bomb predictions, dud bomb predictions and bombs that detonated. Use 25 cycles to increase the efficiency of the circuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement a Zeno Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester quantum circuit that simulates interaction-free measurement, here is a detailed plan and breakdown for the Qiskit developer:

---

### Goal:
Design a quantum circuit that tests whether a bomb is live or a dud without detonating it, using interaction-free measurement enhanced by the quantum Zeno effect with 25 cycles. The circuit should output three probabilities:
- Probability of successfully detecting a live bomb without detonation
- Probability of detecting a dud bomb
- Probability that the bomb detonates (explosion)

---

### Step-by-step Implementation Plan:

#### 1. **Initialize Circuit and Qubits**
- Required qubits: 1 qubit for the photon/interferometer path
- Classical bits: 1 classical bit for measurement results
- Initialize the qubit in state |0⟩ representing the photon starting path

#### 2. **Define Quantum Gates and Operations**
- Use a series of small-angle rotations around the Y-axis to implement the gradual rotation representing the quantum Zeno effect.
- The rotation angle θ = π/(2 * number_of_cycles) = π/(2*25) per cycle.
- The rotation simulates the photon gradually transitioning between states |0⟩ and |1⟩.

- In each cycle:
  - Apply Ry(θ) rotation.
  - If the bomb is live:
    - Perform a projective measurement simulating bomb interaction:
      - If the photon is in state |1⟩, the bomb detonates (photon absorbed).
      - This can be simulated by a measurement that collapses the qubit, or by a controlled operation that flags detonation.
  - If the bomb is dud:
    - No measurement, photon passes through undisturbed.

- The key is that the bomb's presence acts as a "measurement" that collapses the state and potentially causes detonation.

#### 3. **Circuit Structure**
- Implement 25 sequential cycles:
  - For each cycle:
    - Apply Ry(θ)
    - If bomb is live, simulate the bomb measurement interaction
- After all cycles:
  - Final measurement of the qubit to determine the photon's state (|0⟩ or |1⟩)

#### 4. **Measurement Strategy**
- For live bomb:
  - Measure after each cycle or simulate absorption (bomb explosion) when photon is in |1⟩ state.
  - Track whether detonation occurred.
- For dud bomb:
  - No intermediate absorption, only final measurement.
- Use classical bits to record:
  - Explosion occurrence (bomb detonated)
  - Final photon state measurement outcome

#### 5. **Simulation and Data Collection**
- Run multiple shots (e.g., 8192) to gather statistics.
- Count outcomes:
  - If bomb detonated (photon absorbed during the cycles)
  - If photon measured in |0⟩: successful live bomb detection (interaction-free measurement)
  - If photon measured in |1⟩: dud bomb detected (no absorption, photon passed through)

#### 6. **Output Probabilities**
- Calculate percentages from counts:
  - Successful live bomb detection = (# of times photon measured |0⟩ without detonation) / total shots
  - Dud bomb detection = (# of times photon measured |1⟩) / total shots (bomb is dud, so no detonation)
  - Bomb detonated = (# of times bomb exploded) / total shots

---

### Summary of Required Components

| Component                      | Description                                                     |
|-------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| Qubits                        | 1 qubit representing photon path                                |
| Quantum gates                 | Ry(θ) rotations per cycle, with θ = π/(2*25)                    |
| Bomb interaction simulation   | Intermediate measurement or controlled operation simulating absorption for live bomb |
| Measurements                  | Final measurement of the qubit; intermediate detection of bomb explosion |
| Shots                        | Large number (e.g., 8192) for statistical accuracy              |

---

### Expected Outcomes

- For a **live bomb**:
  - High probability of detecting the bomb without detonation (photon remains in |0⟩)
  - Low probability of detonation (photon absorbed)
  - Very low probability photon in |1⟩ state

- For a **dud bomb**:
  - Photon passes through → measured in |1⟩ state with high probability
  - No detonation occurs

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- The circuit implements 25 cycles of Ry(θ) rotations with θ = π/(2*25)
- The bomb interaction is simulated correctly, causing detonation when photon collapses to |1⟩ in a live bomb scenario
- The circuit outputs three probabilities matching the interaction-free measurement logic:
  - Successful detection of live bomb without explosion
  - Detection of dud bomb
  - Bomb detonation probability
- The results show increasing efficiency of interaction-free measurement with 25 cycles (higher success rate, lower detonation rate)
- The function returns three floats representing these probabilities

---

This plan provides a clear design for implementing the Zeno Elitzur-Vaidman bomb tester circuit with 25 cycles and simulating bomb presence in Qiskit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister, transpile
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from numpy import pi

def zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(bomb_live: bool) -> (float, float, float):
    """
    Design a Zeno Elitzur-Vaidman Bomb Tester circuit which takes a boolean indicating if the bomb is live,
    and outputs the percentage of successful live bomb predictions (interaction-free detection),
    dud bomb predictions, and bombs that detonated.
    Uses 25 cycles to increase the efficiency of the circuit.
    """
    # Number of cycles for the quantum Zeno effect
    cycles = 25
    theta = pi / (2 * cycles)  # Rotation angle per cycle

    # Quantum register: 1 qubit representing the photon path
    qr = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    # Classical registers:
    # 1 bit for final photon measurement
    cr_photon = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c_photon')
    # 1 bit to flag bomb detonation (explosion)
    cr_explosion = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c_explosion')

    # Create quantum circuit with registers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr_photon, cr_explosion)

    # Initialize explosion flag to 0 (no explosion)
    # We will set it to 1 if explosion occurs during the cycles

    # For live bomb, simulate measurement after each Ry rotation:
    # If photon is in |1>, bomb explodes (photon absorbed)
    # We simulate this by measuring the qubit in computational basis after each rotation,
    # and if result is 1, set explosion flag and stop further rotations by resetting qubit to |0>.
    # For dud bomb, no intermediate measurement, just rotations.

    if bomb_live:
        for _ in range(cycles):
            # Apply small Ry rotation
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
            # Measure qubit to check if photon is in |1> (bomb explodes)
            qc.measure(qr[0], cr_photon[0])
            # If measurement result is 1, set explosion flag and reset qubit to |0>
            # We simulate conditional reset and flag setting using classical control flow
            # Qiskit does not support classical if inside circuit, so we use a workaround:
            # We will do post-processing on measurement results to detect explosion.
            # But to simulate explosion, we reset qubit to |0> if measured 1 to continue circuit.
            # This requires conditional reset which is not natively supported in Qiskit circuit.
            # Instead, we will simulate explosion by stopping the circuit in post-processing.
            # So here, just measure and continue.
            # To continue circuit, reset qubit to |0> unconditionally (safe since explosion means photon absorbed)
            qc.reset(qr[0])
    else:
        # Dud bomb: no intermediate measurement, just rotations
        for _ in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])

    # Final measurement of photon qubit
    qc.measure(qr[0], cr_photon[0])

    # Use AerSimulator for simulation
    simulator = AerSimulator()

    # Execute circuit with sufficient shots for statistics
    shots = 8192
    compiled_circuit = transpile(qc, simulator)
    result = simulator.run(compiled_circuit, shots=shots).result()
    counts = result.get_counts()

    # Counters for outcomes
    explosion_count = 0
    live_bomb_detected = 0
    dud_bomb_detected = 0

    # Interpretation of results:
    # For live bomb:
    # - Explosion occurs if any intermediate measurement was 1 (photon absorbed)
    #   Since we only have final measurement counts, we must infer explosion from the circuit design:
    #   Because we reset qubit after measuring 1 in intermediate steps, final measurement alone is insufficient.
    #   But since we measured after each cycle, the classical register c_photon is overwritten each time,
    #   so final measurement only reflects last measurement.
    #   To properly detect explosion, we need to count how many shots had intermediate measurement 1.
    #   This requires a different approach: use multiple classical bits or a different method.

    # Revised approach:
    # We will simulate explosion by measuring after each cycle and storing results in a classical register.
    # Since Qiskit classical registers cannot be dynamically sized, we will create a classical register with size=cycles
    # to store intermediate measurements.

    # Let's redo the circuit with this approach.

    # --- Redo circuit with intermediate measurements stored in classical register ---

    # Create new registers
    qr = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    cr_intermediate = ClassicalRegister(cycles, 'c_int')  # store intermediate measurements
    cr_final = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c_final')  # final measurement

    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr_intermediate, cr_final)

    if bomb_live:
        for i in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
            qc.measure(qr[0], cr_intermediate[i])
            # If measurement is 1, photon absorbed (bomb explodes), reset qubit to |0> to continue
            qc.reset(qr[0])
    else:
        for i in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
        # No intermediate measurement for dud bomb, so set intermediate bits to 0
        # We cannot set classical bits directly, so we leave them unmeasured (will be 0 by default)

    # Final measurement
    qc.measure(qr[0], cr_final[0])

    compiled_circuit = transpile(qc, simulator)
    result = simulator.run(compiled_circuit, shots=shots).result()
    counts = result.get_counts()

    # Now parse counts:
    # Each key in counts is a bitstring of length cycles + 1 (intermediate bits + final bit)
    # Format: c_final c_int[cycles-1] ... c_int[0]
    # We need to parse the string accordingly.

    explosion_count = 0
    live_bomb_detected = 0
    dud_bomb_detected = 0

    for bitstring, count in counts.items():
        # bitstring example: '0' + '000...0' (final bit + intermediate bits)
        # Qiskit bitstring order: leftmost bit is last classical register bit
        # The order of classical bits in bitstring is reversed order of registers and bits:
        # The bitstring is ordered as cr_final[0], cr_int[cycles-1], ..., cr_int[0]
        # So first bit is final measurement, next bits are intermediate measurements in reverse order

        final_bit = bitstring[0]
        intermediate_bits = bitstring[1:]

        # Check if any intermediate measurement is '1' => explosion
        if bomb_live and '1' in intermediate_bits:
            explosion_count += count
        else:
            # No explosion
            if bomb_live:
                # Successful live bomb detection if final bit is '0' (photon in |0>)
                if final_bit == '0':
                    live_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    # Photon in |1> after cycles without explosion is very unlikely but count as dud detection
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
            else:
                # Dud bomb: no explosion possible
                # Photon measured in |1> means dud bomb detected
                if final_bit == '1':
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    # Photon in |0> for dud bomb is unlikely but count as live bomb detection
                    live_bomb_detected += count

    # Calculate probabilities
    explosion_prob = explosion_count / shots
    live_bomb_prob = live_bomb_detected / shots
    dud_bomb_prob = dud_bomb_detected / shots

    return live_bomb_prob, dud_bomb_prob, explosion_prob


# Example usage:
# live_prob, dud_prob, explosion_prob = zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(True)
# print(f"Live bomb detection: {live_prob:.4f}, Dud bomb detection: {dud_prob:.4f}, Explosion: {explosion_prob:.4f}")

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister, transpile
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from numpy import pi

def zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(bomb_live: bool) -> (float, float, float):
    """
    Design a Zeno Elitzur-Vaidman Bomb Tester circuit which takes a boolean indicating if the bomb is live,
    and outputs the percentage of successful live bomb predictions (interaction-free detection),
    dud bomb predictions, and bombs that detonated.
    Uses 25 cycles to increase the efficiency of the circuit.
    """
    cycles = 25
    theta = pi / (2 * cycles)  # Rotation angle per cycle

    # Quantum register: 1 qubit representing the photon path
    qr = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    # Classical registers:
    # One classical register to store intermediate measurements (one bit per cycle)
    cr_intermediate = ClassicalRegister(cycles, 'c_int')
    # One classical bit for final photon measurement
    cr_final = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c_final')

    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr_intermediate, cr_final)

    if bomb_live:
        # For live bomb, measure after each Ry rotation to simulate bomb interaction
        for i in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
            qc.measure(qr[0], cr_intermediate[i])
            # Reset qubit to |0> after measurement to continue circuit
            qc.reset(qr[0])
    else:
        # For dud bomb, no intermediate measurement, just rotations
        for _ in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
        # No intermediate measurement bits set (will be 0 by default)

    # Final measurement of photon qubit
    qc.measure(qr[0], cr_final[0])

    # Use AerSimulator for simulation
    simulator = AerSimulator()
    compiled_circuit = transpile(qc, simulator)
    shots = 8192
    result = simulator.run(compiled_circuit, shots=shots).result()
    counts = result.get_counts()

    explosion_count = 0
    live_bomb_detected = 0
    dud_bomb_detected = 0

    for bitstring, count in counts.items():
        # bitstring format: c_final[0] c_int[cycles-1] ... c_int[0]
        final_bit = bitstring[0]
        intermediate_bits = bitstring[1:]

        if bomb_live and '1' in intermediate_bits:
            # Explosion occurred if any intermediate measurement is 1
            explosion_count += count
        else:
            # No explosion
            if bomb_live:
                # Successful live bomb detection if final bit is '0'
                if final_bit == '0':
                    live_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    # Photon in |1> after cycles without explosion (rare)
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
            else:
                # Dud bomb: no explosion possible
                if final_bit == '1':
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    live_bomb_detected += count

    total_shots = shots
    explosion_prob = explosion_count / total_shots
    live_bomb_prob = live_bomb_detected / total_shots
    dud_bomb_prob = dud_bomb_detected / total_shots

    return live_bomb_prob, dud_bomb_prob, explosion_prob

# Example usage:
# live_prob, dud_prob, explosion_prob = zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(True)
# print(f"Live bomb detection: {live_prob:.4f}, Dud bomb detection: {dud_prob:.4f}, Explosion: {explosion_prob:.4f}")

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister, transpile
from qiskit_aer import AerSimulator
from numpy import pi

def zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(bomb_live: bool) -> (float, float, float):
    """
    Design a Zeno Elitzur-Vaidman Bomb Tester circuit which takes a boolean indicating if the bomb is live,
    and outputs the percentage of successful live bomb predictions (interaction-free detection),
    dud bomb predictions, and bombs that detonated.
    Uses 25 cycles to increase the efficiency of the circuit.
    """
    cycles = 25
    theta = pi / (2 * cycles)  # Rotation angle per cycle

    # Quantum register: 1 qubit representing the photon path
    qr = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    # Classical registers:
    # One classical register to store intermediate measurements (one bit per cycle)
    cr_intermediate = ClassicalRegister(cycles, 'c_int')
    # One classical bit for final photon measurement
    cr_final = ClassicalRegister(1, 'c_final')

    qc = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr_intermediate, cr_final)

    if bomb_live:
        # For live bomb, measure after each Ry rotation to simulate bomb interaction
        for i in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
            qc.measure(qr[0], cr_intermediate[i])
            # Reset qubit to |0> after measurement to continue circuit
            qc.reset(qr[0])
    else:
        # For dud bomb, no intermediate measurement, just rotations
        for _ in range(cycles):
            qc.ry(theta, qr[0])
        # No intermediate measurement bits set (will be 0 by default)

    # Final measurement of photon qubit
    qc.measure(qr[0], cr_final[0])

    # Use AerSimulator for simulation
    simulator = AerSimulator()
    compiled_circuit = transpile(qc, simulator)
    shots = 8192
    result = simulator.run(compiled_circuit, shots=shots).result()
    counts = result.get_counts()

    explosion_count = 0
    live_bomb_detected = 0
    dud_bomb_detected = 0

    for bitstring, count in counts.items():
        # bitstring format: c_final[0] c_int[cycles-1] ... c_int[0]
        final_bit = bitstring[0]
        intermediate_bits = bitstring[1:]

        if bomb_live and '1' in intermediate_bits:
            # Explosion occurred if any intermediate measurement is 1
            explosion_count += count
        else:
            # No explosion
            if bomb_live:
                # Successful live bomb detection if final bit is '0'
                if final_bit == '0':
                    live_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    # Photon in |1> after cycles without explosion (rare)
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
            else:
                # Dud bomb: no explosion possible
                if final_bit == '1':
                    dud_bomb_detected += count
                else:
                    live_bomb_detected += count

    total_shots = shots
    explosion_prob = explosion_count / total_shots
    live_bomb_prob = live_bomb_detected / total_shots
    dud_bomb_prob = dud_bomb_detected / total_shots

    return live_bomb_prob, dud_bomb_prob, explosion_prob

# Example usage:
# live_prob, dud_prob, explosion_prob = zeno_elitzur_vaidman_bomb_tester(True)
# print(f"Live bomb detection: {live_prob:.4f}, Dud bomb detection: {dud_prob:.4f}, Explosion: {explosion_prob:.4f}")

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import SGate, SdgGate

def create_quantum_circuit_based_h0_cs01_h1_csdg10():
    """
    Build a Quantum Circuit composed by the gates:
    - H on qubit 0
    - Controlled-S gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    - H on qubit 1
    - Controlled-S dagger gate with control qubit 1 and target qubit 0
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Apply Controlled-S gate (S gate controlled by qubit 0 on qubit 1)
    qc.append(SGate().control(1), [0, 1])
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 1
    qc.h(1)
    
    # Apply Controlled-S dagger gate (Sdg gate controlled by qubit 1 on qubit 0)
    qc.append(SdgGate().control(1), [1, 0])
    
    return qc

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import CSdgGate

def create_quantum_circuit_based_h0_cswap012_h1_csdg10():
    """
    Build a Quantum Circuit composed by the gates:
    - H on qubit 0
    - Controlled-SWAP (Fredkin) with control qubit 0 and target qubits 1 and 2
    - H on qubit 1
    - Controlled-S dagger (C-S†) with control qubit 1 and target qubit 0
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 3 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(3)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Apply Controlled-SWAP (Fredkin) gate with control qubit 0 and target qubits 1 and 2
    qc.cswap(0, 1, 2)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 1
    qc.h(1)
    
    # Apply Controlled-S dagger gate with control qubit 1 and target qubit 0
    csdg = CSdgGate()
    qc.append(csdg, [1, 0])
    
    return qc
