=== [73/151] qiskitHumanEval/72 :: gate_if_clbits ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

import contextlib
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Gate
def gate_if_clbits(
    circuit: QuantumCircuit, gate: Gate, qubits: list[int], condition_clbits: list[int]
) -> None:
    """ Apply `gate` to qubits with indices `qubits`, conditioned on all `condition_clbits` being 1.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Task Analysis:
The user wants a method to apply a given quantum gate to specific qubits in a QuantumCircuit, but only if *all* classical bits (clbits) in a specified list are equal to 1. In Qiskit, conditional operations typically condition on a classical register or a classical integer value. Conditioning on multiple classical bits all being 1 means conditioning on the classical register (or bits) representing the binary value with all bits set to 1.

Implementation Plan:

1. Inputs:
   - circuit: QuantumCircuit instance to which the conditional gate will be applied.
   - gate: A Gate object representing the quantum gate to be applied.
   - qubits: List of qubit indices (integers) on which to apply the gate.
   - condition_clbits: List of classical bit indices (integers) that must all be 1 for the gate to be applied.

2. Preconditions:
   - The circuit must have classical bits at the indices specified in condition_clbits.
   - The circuit must have qubits at the indices specified in qubits.
   - The gate must be compatible with the number of qubits specified.

3. Steps:
   a. Identify the classical bits in condition_clbits and combine them into a classical register or a bitstring condition.
      - Qiskit conditions typically use either a ClassicalRegister or a single classical integer value.
      - Since we want to condition on multiple clbits all being 1, we interpret the set of classical bits as a binary number where all bits are 1.
   
   b. Convert the list of classical bits into a single integer condition value:
      - For n classical bits, the condition value to check is (2^n - 1), which corresponds to all bits being 1.
      
   c. Create a classical register or integer condition representing these classical bits.
      - If those bits belong to a single ClassicalRegister, the condition can be applied directly.
      - If they belong to multiple classical registers or are scattered, it may be necessary to re-map or consolidate them.
   
   d. Apply the gate to the specified qubits, conditioned on the classical bits equal to the integer value computed.
      - Use QuantumCircuit’s `.c_if()` method, which takes a ClassicalRegister and an integer value.
      
   e. Because `.c_if()` works with a ClassicalRegister, ensure the classical bits are part of a single ClassicalRegister or combine them accordingly.
      - If bits belong to different registers, or scattered, this might require additional classical registers or workarounds.

4. Circuit structure and qubit requirements:
   - The circuit must have sufficient qubits and classical bits as per input lists.
   - The gate is applied only to the specified qubits.

5. Measurement strategy:
   - Not directly relevant here, but it is assumed classical bits have been measured or set prior to the condition.

6. Expected outcomes:
   - The specified gate is applied only when all classical bits in condition_clbits are 1.
   - Otherwise, the gate is not applied.

Acceptance Criteria:
- The function correctly applies the gate to the specified qubits conditioned on all classical bits being 1.
- The function handles classical bits that belong to the same classical register.
- The function raises a clear error or handles the case where classical bits are from multiple registers or not contiguous.
- The gate is applied with `.c_if()` method or equivalent in Qiskit.
- No gate is applied if the condition is not met.

Summary Plan:
1. Verify input classical bits belong to the same classical register.
2. Calculate integer condition = 2^(number of bits) - 1.
3. Extract the classical register containing those bits.
4. Apply gate.c_if(classical_register, integer_condition) to qubits.
5. Add appropriate error handling for unsupported cases.

This plan provides clear guidance for a Qiskit developer to implement the conditional gate application on multiple classical bits all being 1.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Gate, Clbit

def gate_if_clbits(
    circuit: QuantumCircuit, gate: Gate, qubits: list[int], condition_clbits: list[int]
) -> None:
    """
    Apply `gate` to qubits with indices `qubits`, conditioned on all classical bits
    in `condition_clbits` being 1.

    Parameters:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to modify.
        gate (Gate): The quantum gate to apply conditionally.
        qubits (list[int]): List of qubit indices to which the gate is applied.
        condition_clbits (list[int]): List of classical bit indices that must all be 1.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If classical bits are not all from the same ClassicalRegister,
                    or if qubit or classical bit indices are invalid.
    """
    # Validate qubit indices
    if any(q < 0 or q >= circuit.num_qubits for q in qubits):
        raise ValueError("One or more qubit indices are out of range.")

    # Validate classical bit indices
    if any(c < 0 or c >= circuit.num_clbits for c in condition_clbits):
        raise ValueError("One or more classical bit indices are out of range.")

    # Get Clbit objects for the condition_clbits indices
    clbits = [circuit.clbits[i] for i in condition_clbits]

    # Check that all classical bits belong to the same ClassicalRegister
    # Extract the ClassicalRegister for each clbit
    clbit_regs = [clbit.register for clbit in clbits]

    # If any clbit does not belong to a register, raise error
    if any(reg is None for reg in clbit_regs):
        raise ValueError("All classical bits must belong to a ClassicalRegister.")

    # Check all registers are the same
    first_reg = clbit_regs[0]
    if any(reg != first_reg for reg in clbit_regs):
        raise ValueError("All classical bits must belong to the same ClassicalRegister.")

    # Get the indices of the classical bits within the register
    reg_indices = [first_reg[:].index(clbit) for clbit in clbits]

    # Sort indices and check if they are contiguous
    sorted_indices = sorted(reg_indices)
    if sorted_indices != list(range(min(sorted_indices), max(sorted_indices) + 1)):
        raise ValueError("Classical bits must be contiguous within the ClassicalRegister.")

    # Calculate the integer condition value: all bits set to 1
    n_bits = len(condition_clbits)
    condition_value = (1 << n_bits) - 1  # 2^n_bits - 1

    # The condition is on the bits starting at the lowest index in the register
    # Qiskit's c_if conditions on the entire register, so we need to shift the condition_value
    # to the correct bit positions within the register.

    # Shift condition_value to align with the bits in the register
    shift = min(sorted_indices)
    condition_value_shifted = condition_value << shift

    # Apply the gate conditionally
    # Prepare the qubit objects from indices
    qubit_objs = [circuit.qubits[i] for i in qubits]

    # Create a gate instruction on the qubits
    gate_inst = gate.copy()
    gate_inst.name = gate.name  # ensure name is preserved

    # Append the gate instruction conditionally
    circuit.append(gate_inst, qubit_objs).c_if(first_reg, condition_value_shifted)
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Gate

def gate_if_clbits(
    circuit: QuantumCircuit, gate: Gate, qubits: list[int], condition_clbits: list[int]
) -> None:
    """
    Apply `gate` to qubits with indices `qubits`, conditioned on all classical bits
    in `condition_clbits` being 1.

    Parameters:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to modify.
        gate (Gate): The quantum gate to apply conditionally.
        qubits (list[int]): List of qubit indices to which the gate is applied.
        condition_clbits (list[int]): List of classical bit indices that must all be 1.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If classical bits are not all from the same ClassicalRegister,
                    or if qubit or classical bit indices are invalid.
    """
    # Validate qubit indices
    if any(q < 0 or q >= circuit.num_qubits for q in qubits):
        raise ValueError("One or more qubit indices are out of range.")

    # Validate classical bit indices
    if any(c < 0 or c >= circuit.num_clbits for c in condition_clbits):
        raise ValueError("One or more classical bit indices are out of range.")

    # Get Clbit objects for the condition_clbits indices
    clbits = [circuit.clbits[i] for i in condition_clbits]

    # Check that all classical bits belong to the same ClassicalRegister
    # Extract the ClassicalRegister for each clbit
    clbit_regs = [clbit.register for clbit in clbits]

    # If any clbit does not belong to a register, raise error
    if any(reg is None for reg in clbit_regs):
        raise ValueError("All classical bits must belong to a ClassicalRegister.")

    # Check all registers are the same
    first_reg = clbit_regs[0]
    if any(reg != first_reg for reg in clbit_regs):
        raise ValueError("All classical bits must belong to the same ClassicalRegister.")

    # Get the indices of the classical bits within the register
    reg_indices = [first_reg[:].index(clbit) for clbit in clbits]

    # Sort indices and check if they are contiguous
    sorted_indices = sorted(reg_indices)
    if sorted_indices != list(range(min(sorted_indices), max(sorted_indices) + 1)):
        raise ValueError("Classical bits must be contiguous within the ClassicalRegister.")

    # Calculate the integer condition value: all bits set to 1
    n_bits = len(condition_clbits)
    condition_value = (1 << n_bits) - 1  # 2^n_bits - 1

    # The condition is on the bits starting at the lowest index in the register
    # Qiskit's c_if conditions on the entire register, so we need to shift the condition_value
    # to the correct bit positions within the register.

    # Shift condition_value to align with the bits in the register
    shift = min(sorted_indices)
    condition_value_shifted = condition_value << shift

    # Apply the gate conditionally
    # Prepare the qubit objects from indices
    qubit_objs = [circuit.qubits[i] for i in qubits]

    # Append the gate instruction conditionally
    circuit.append(gate, qubit_objs).c_if(first_reg, condition_value_shifted)
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def x_measurement(circuit: QuantumCircuit, qubit: int, clbit: int) -> None:
    """
    Add an X-basis measurement on qubit at index `qubit`, storing the result to classical bit `clbit`.
    
    This is done by applying a Hadamard gate to rotate the basis from X to Z,
    then performing a standard Z-basis measurement.
    
    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to modify.
        qubit (int): Index of the qubit to measure.
        clbit (int): Index of the classical bit to store the measurement result.
    """
    # Apply Hadamard gate to rotate measurement basis from X to Z
    circuit.h(qubit)
    # Measure the qubit in the Z-basis and store the result in the classical bit
    circuit.measure(qubit, clbit)
```

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import Barrier
def split_circuit_at_barriers(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> list[QuantumCircuit]:
    """ Split `circuit` at each barrier operation. Do not include barriers in the output circuits.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Task: Design a quantum circuit processing function that splits a given QuantumCircuit at each Barrier operation, excluding the Barriers themselves from the resulting subcircuits.

---

### Implementation Plan:

1. **Input and Initialization**
   - Input: A single QuantumCircuit `circuit` with N qubits and possibly some classical bits.
   - Initialize an empty list to hold the resulting subcircuits.
   - Initialize a new empty QuantumCircuit `current_subcircuit` with the same number of qubits and classical bits as the input circuit.
   
2. **Iterate Over Instructions**
   - Loop through all instructions (operations) in the input circuit in order.
   - For each instruction:
     - If the instruction is a Barrier:
       - Append the current_subcircuit to the output list if it contains any operations (i.e., not empty).
       - Re-initialize the current_subcircuit to an empty circuit with the same qubits and classical bits.
       - **Do not** add the Barrier operation to the current_subcircuit.
     - Else:
       - Append the instruction to the current_subcircuit preserving the qubits and classical bits it acts on.
       
3. **Finalization**
   - After iterating through all instructions, append the last current_subcircuit to the output list if it's non-empty.
   
4. **Output**
   - Return the list of QuantumCircuit objects, each representing a segment of the original circuit split at Barriers.
   - None of the returned circuits should contain Barrier instructions.
   - The order of operations and qubits should be preserved within each subcircuit.

---

### Required Quantum Gates and Operations:

- No new gates are applied or modified.
- Only operations from the original circuit are transferred to subcircuits.
- Barriers are identified and used as split points but excluded from output circuits.

---

### Circuit Structure and Qubit/Bit Requirements:

- Each output subcircuit must have the same number of qubits and classical bits as the input circuit, with the same qubit and classical bit ordering.
- The subcircuits preserve the mapping of operations to qubits and classical bits as they were in the original circuit.

---

### Measurement Strategy:

- Measurements that exist in the original circuit should be preserved in the segments they belong to.
- Since measurements are circuit operations, they will be copied to the respective subcircuit containing them.

---

### Expected Outcomes:

- A list of QuantumCircuits, each containing a contiguous subset of the original circuit’s operations, split at Barriers.
- None of the subcircuits includes Barrier instructions.
- The sequence of operations is preserved within each subcircuit.
- The combined execution of all subcircuits in order is equivalent to the original circuit (modulo the removal of Barriers).

---

### Acceptance Criteria:

- The function accepts any QuantumCircuit with zero or more Barrier operations.
- It returns a list of QuantumCircuits with no Barrier instructions inside.
- The concatenation of the subcircuits’ instructions (in order) reconstructs the original circuit instructions, excluding Barriers.
- The qubit and classical bit structure and order are preserved exactly in each subcircuit.
- The function correctly handles edge cases:
  - Circuit with no barriers (returns list with original circuit)
  - Circuit starting or ending with barriers (produces empty subcircuit segments which are ignored)
  - Multiple consecutive barriers (no empty subcircuits included)

---

This plan provides a clear roadmap for implementing `split_circuit_at_barriers` function in Qiskit without including barriers in the output circuits.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import Barrier

def split_circuit_at_barriers(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> list[QuantumCircuit]:
    """
    Split `circuit` at each barrier operation. Do not include barriers in the output circuits.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The input quantum circuit to split.

    Returns:
        list[QuantumCircuit]: List of QuantumCircuit objects split at barriers,
                             none containing barrier instructions.
    """
    subcircuits = []
    # Initialize an empty subcircuit with same qubits and classical bits
    current_subcircuit = QuantumCircuit(circuit.num_qubits, circuit.num_clbits, name="subcircuit")

    for instr, qargs, cargs in circuit.data:
        # Check if the instruction is a Barrier
        if instr.name == "barrier":
            # If current subcircuit has any instructions, append it to the list
            if current_subcircuit.data:
                subcircuits.append(current_subcircuit)
            # Start a new empty subcircuit
            current_subcircuit = QuantumCircuit(circuit.num_qubits, circuit.num_clbits, name="subcircuit")
            # Do not add the barrier itself
        else:
            # Append the instruction to the current subcircuit preserving qubits and clbits
            current_subcircuit.append(instr, qargs, cargs)

    # Append the last subcircuit if it contains any instructions
    if current_subcircuit.data:
        subcircuits.append(current_subcircuit)

    return subcircuits
```

Here is a detailed plan for implementing the function `circuit_to_bools` that takes a Qiskit QuantumCircuit, samples it once, and returns the measurement result as a list of booleans corresponding to classical bits:

---

### Implementation Plan for `circuit_to_bools`

1. **Circuit Preparation**
   - Ensure the input `circuit` includes measurement operations mapping quantum bits to classical bits.
     - If the circuit does not already contain measurements, the function should either:
       - Raise an error or warning, or
       - Append measurements from each quantum bit to corresponding classical bits before sampling.
   - Verify the number of classical bits in the circuit, as these define the length of the output boolean list.

2. **Sampling the Circuit Once**
   - Use the `StatevectorSampler` or an appropriate sampler primitive from Qiskit primitives to sample the circuit exactly once.
   - The sampling should produce a single measurement outcome, typically as a bitstring corresponding to classical bit values.

3. **Extracting Measurement Results**
   - Convert the sampled measurement result (bitstring) into a list of booleans.
     - Each bit corresponds to a classical bit in the circuit.
     - The 0th boolean in the list corresponds to the measurement result of the 0th classical bit.
     - For example, if the measurement outcome is `'101'` for classical bits `[c2, c1, c0]`, ensure the output list reflects `[bool(c0), bool(c1), bool(c2)]`.
   - Confirm the bit order: Qiskit classical bits are conventionally indexed, and mapping the bitstring to a boolean list must respect this indexing.

4. **Return Value**
   - Return the list of booleans with length equal to the number of classical bits.
   - Each boolean represents the corresponding classical bit measurement (False for 0, True for 1).

---

### Required Quantum Gates and Operations
- No additional quantum gates are needed beyond what is already in the input circuit.
- The circuit must have measurement operations (`measure`) from each quantum bit to the corresponding classical bit for accurate sampling.

### Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements
- The function supports any quantum circuit with any number of qubits and classical bits.
- The circuit must have classical bits and measurements defined.
- The number of classical bits determines the output list size.

### Measurement Strategy
- Sample the circuit once using `StatevectorSampler` or a sampler primitive.
- Extract one measurement result from the sample.

### Expected Outcomes
- A list of booleans corresponding to the classical bit measurement outcomes from the sampled circuit.
- The 0th boolean corresponds to the 0th classical bit measurement.
- For example, if classical bits read as `010`, output list is `[False, True, False]`.

---

### Acceptance Criteria
- The function runs without errors on any valid QuantumCircuit with measurements.
- The output list length matches the number of classical bits in the circuit.
- The order of booleans strictly corresponds to classical bit indices.
- Sampling is performed exactly once per function call.
- If the circuit lacks measurements, the function handles this scenario gracefully (e.g., raises an informative error).
- The function does not modify the input circuit permanently (if measurements are appended, do so on a copy or revert changes).

---

This plan provides a clear high-level design for implementing the requested function in Qiskit.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.primitives import Sampler

def circuit_to_bools(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> list[bool]:
    """
    Given a QuantumCircuit, sample it once and convert the measurement result to a list of bools,
    where the 0th bool is the result of the 0th classical bit.

    Parameters:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to sample. Must have classical bits and measurements.

    Returns:
        list[bool]: List of booleans representing measurement results of classical bits.
                    The 0th element corresponds to the 0th classical bit.
    
    Raises:
        ValueError: If the circuit has no classical bits or no measurements.
    """
    # Check if circuit has classical bits
    num_clbits = circuit.num_clbits
    if num_clbits == 0:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no classical bits to measure.")

    # Check if circuit has measurements
    # We check if any instruction is 'measure'
    has_measure = any(instr.operation.name == 'measure' for instr in circuit.data)
    if not has_measure:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no measurement operations.")

    # Use Sampler primitive to sample the circuit once
    sampler = Sampler()

    # Run the sampler once
    job = sampler.run(circuit, shots=1)
    result = job.result()

    # Get the counts from the result
    # The result has quasi-distribution over bitstrings, but with shots=1, only one outcome
    # The result.quasi_dists is a list of quasi-distributions for each circuit
    quasi_dist = result.quasi_dists[0]

    # Extract the single bitstring outcome
    # quasi_dist is a dict {bitstring: probability}
    # Since shots=1, only one key with probability 1.0
    bitstring = next(iter(quasi_dist.keys()))

    # The bitstring is a string of '0' and '1' with length equal to number of classical bits
    # The bitstring is ordered with the leftmost bit as the highest index classical bit
    # For example, for 3 classical bits: bitstring[0] corresponds to c2, bitstring[2] to c0
    # We need to map bitstring so that index 0 corresponds to c0, index 1 to c1, etc.
    # So we reverse the bitstring to align indices
    bitstring_reversed = bitstring[::-1]

    # Convert each bit to bool
    bools = [bit == '1' for bit in bitstring_reversed]

    return bools
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.primitives import StatevectorSampler

def circuit_to_bools(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> list[bool]:
    """
    Given a QuantumCircuit, sample it once and convert the measurement result to a list of bools,
    where the 0th bool is the result of the 0th classical bit.

    Parameters:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to sample. Must have classical bits and measurements.

    Returns:
        list[bool]: List of booleans representing measurement results of classical bits.
                    The 0th element corresponds to the 0th classical bit.
    
    Raises:
        ValueError: If the circuit has no classical bits or no measurements.
    """
    # Check if circuit has classical bits
    num_clbits = circuit.num_clbits
    if num_clbits == 0:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no classical bits to measure.")

    # Check if circuit has measurements
    has_measure = any(instr.operation.name == 'measure' for instr in circuit.data)
    if not has_measure:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no measurement operations.")

    # Use StatevectorSampler to get the statevector
    sampler = StatevectorSampler()
    result = sampler.run(circuit).result()

    # The result is a statevector, but since the circuit has measurements,
    # we cannot directly get measurement outcomes from statevector.
    # Instead, we simulate measurement by sampling from the statevector.

    # Get the statevector
    statevector = result.get_statevector()

    # Sample once from the statevector to get a bitstring outcome
    # The statevector is indexed by computational basis states in binary order
    # We sample one bitstring according to the probability distribution

    import numpy as np

    probabilities = np.abs(statevector.data) ** 2
    # Sample one index according to probabilities
    sampled_index = np.random.choice(len(probabilities), p=probabilities)
    # Convert index to bitstring with length equal to number of qubits
    bitstring = format(sampled_index, f'0{circuit.num_qubits}b')

    # The circuit has measurements mapping qubits to classical bits
    # We need to extract the classical bits from the measured qubits
    # The measurement maps qubit i to classical bit j, but we don't have direct mapping here
    # We assume measurement is from qubit i to classical bit i (common case)
    # So classical bits correspond to qubits 0..num_clbits-1

    # Extract bits for classical bits from bitstring
    # bitstring[0] is the most significant bit (qubit 0)
    # Qiskit uses little endian convention for qubit ordering in bitstrings:
    # The rightmost bit corresponds to qubit 0
    # So we reverse bitstring to align qubit indices
    bitstring_reversed = bitstring[::-1]

    # For each classical bit index, get the corresponding qubit bit
    bools = []
    for clbit_index in range(num_clbits):
        # Get bit of qubit clbit_index
        bit = bitstring_reversed[clbit_index]
        bools.append(bit == '1')

    return bools
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.primitives import StatevectorSampler

def circuit_to_bools(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> list[bool]:
    """
    Given a QuantumCircuit, sample it once and convert the measurement result to a list of bools,
    where the 0th bool is the result of the 0th classical bit.

    Parameters:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to sample. Must have classical bits and measurements.

    Returns:
        list[bool]: List of booleans representing measurement results of classical bits.
                    The 0th element corresponds to the 0th classical bit.
    
    Raises:
        ValueError: If the circuit has no classical bits or no measurements.
    """
    # Check if circuit has classical bits
    num_clbits = circuit.num_clbits
    if num_clbits == 0:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no classical bits to measure.")

    # Check if circuit has measurements
    has_measure = any(instr.operation.name == 'measure' for instr in circuit.data)
    if not has_measure:
        raise ValueError("Circuit has no measurement operations.")

    # Use StatevectorSampler to get the statevector
    sampler = StatevectorSampler()
    result = sampler.run(circuit).result()

    # Get the statevector
    statevector = result.get_statevector()

    # Sample once from the statevector to get a bitstring outcome
    import numpy as np

    probabilities = np.abs(statevector.data) ** 2
    sampled_index = np.random.choice(len(probabilities), p=probabilities)
    bitstring = format(sampled_index, f'0{circuit.num_qubits}b')

    # Reverse bitstring to align qubit indices (rightmost bit is qubit 0)
    bitstring_reversed = bitstring[::-1]

    # Extract bits for classical bits assuming classical bit i corresponds to qubit i
    bools = [bitstring_reversed[i] == '1' for i in range(num_clbits)]

    return bools
```

    """

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the transpiler pass `create_hxh_pass()` that replaces all non-controlled Z gates with H-X-H sequences:

1. **Quantum Circuit Analysis and Traversal**
   - The pass will operate on a DAGCircuit representation of a quantum circuit.
   - Traverse the DAG to identify all nodes corresponding to Z gates (`ZGate`).
   - Filter out any Z gates that are controlled (i.e., only target un-controlled Z gates).

2. **Gate Replacement Logic**
   - For each identified non-controlled Z gate node:
     - Replace the single Z gate operation on the target qubit with a sequence of three gates: H, X, and H, applied in that order.
   - The H-X-H sequence is equivalent to Z in the single-qubit gate context.

3. **Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements**
   - No additional qubits are needed since the replacement is a local transformation on the same qubit.
   - The pass should preserve the circuit structure, only substituting the Z gate with three gates.

4. **Implementation Details**
   - Create a subclass of `TransformationPass`.
   - Implement the `run()` method that takes a `DAGCircuit` as input.
   - Within `run()`, perform the traversal and replacement as described.
   - Ensure the replaced gates maintain the same qubit and classical bit mappings.
   - Return the modified `DAGCircuit`.

5. **Measurement Strategy**
   - The pass does not add or remove measurements.
   - It preserves existing measurements and their placement.

6. **Expected Outcome**
   - After running the pass, the circuit will have no non-controlled Z gates.
   - Each original Z gate will be replaced by a sequence of gates: H, X, H on the same qubit.
   - Controlled-Z gates remain unchanged.

7. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - The pass correctly identifies and replaces all non-controlled Z gates.
   - Controlled-Z gates are left intact.
   - The circuit remains functionally equivalent regarding the replaced gates.
   - The pass operates without errors on arbitrary DAGCircuit inputs.
   - No other gates or circuit structure are unintentionally modified.

This plan can be directly implemented by a Qiskit developer to create a custom transpiler pass performing the specified gate replacement.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler.basepasses import TransformationPass
from qiskit.dagcircuit import DAGCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import ZGate, HGate, XGate

def create_hxh_pass() -> TransformationPass:
    """Return a transpiler pass that replaces all non-controlled Z gates with H-X-H gate sequences."""

    class HxHPass(TransformationPass):
        def run(self, dag: DAGCircuit) -> DAGCircuit:
            # Iterate over all nodes in the DAG that are Z gates
            for node in dag.op_nodes(ZGate):
                # Only replace if the Z gate is not controlled (no control qubits)
                if not node.op.num_ctrl_qubits:
                    qargs = node.qargs  # qubits the gate acts on
                    cargs = node.cargs  # classical bits (usually empty for Z)
                    # Remove the original Z gate node
                    dag.remove_op_node(node)
                    # Insert H gate
                    dag.apply_operation_back(HGate(), qargs, cargs)
                    # Insert X gate
                    dag.apply_operation_back(XGate(), qargs, cargs)
                    # Insert H gate
                    dag.apply_operation_back(HGate(), qargs, cargs)
            return dag

    return HxHPass()
```

import math
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
def circuit_from_probability_dist(probability_dist: dict[int, float]) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Given a distribution as a dictionary of the form { measurement: probability }, return a quantum circuit that produces that distribution.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Task Analysis:
The user wants a function that, given a classical probability distribution over bitstrings, constructs a quantum circuit whose measurement outcomes reproduce that distribution. This is a known problem of preparing a quantum state whose measurement in the computational basis yields the specified probabilities.

Implementation Plan:

1. Input:
   - A dictionary mapping integer measurement outcomes (interpreted as bitstrings) to their corresponding probabilities.
   - Assume the keys are integers representing bitstrings of length n.
   - The probabilities sum to 1.

2. Determine the number of qubits (n):
   - Find the maximum key in the dictionary.
   - Compute the bit-length of the maximum key to determine n.
   - Alternatively, determine n from the length of the bitstring representation of any key (all keys should have consistent length).

3. Initialize the quantum circuit:
   - Create a QuantumCircuit with n qubits and n classical bits (for measurement).

4. Prepare the quantum state:
   - The goal is to prepare the quantum state |ψ⟩ = ∑_x sqrt(p_x) |x⟩, where p_x are the probabilities.
   - This can be done using amplitude encoding.
   - The preparation method depends on the size of n and the sparsity of the distribution.
   - For small n, an exact state preparation can be done using known algorithms (e.g., using qiskit's Initialize instruction or decomposition into gates).
   - For larger n, approximate or more efficient methods might be needed, but out of scope here.

5. Steps to prepare the state:
   - Compute the vector of amplitudes: for each basis state |x⟩, amplitude = sqrt(probability[x]).
   - Use the state initialization method to set the quantum state to this amplitude vector.
     - The Initialize instruction can be used (though not required to specify code).
   - The Initialize instruction is decomposed into elementary gates internally.

6. Add measurement:
   - Measure all qubits into classical bits.

7. Expected outcome:
   - Measuring the circuit outputs bitstrings with probabilities matching the input distribution.

Acceptance Criteria:
- The circuit should have n qubits and n classical bits.
- The circuit prepares a quantum state with amplitudes sqrt of the input probabilities.
- Measuring the circuit yields the desired probability distribution.
- The circuit includes measurement operations on all qubits.
- The function handles distributions over arbitrary bit-length integers.
- The function is generic and can handle any valid probability distribution input.

Summary Plan:

1. Extract n from the maximum key in probability_dist.
2. Initialize a QuantumCircuit with n qubits and n classical bits.
3. Create an amplitude vector of size 2^n, with zeros for states not in the dictionary.
4. For each key in probability_dist:
   - Convert key to bitstring index.
   - Set amplitude = sqrt(probability).
5. Use a state preparation method (e.g., Initialize) to prepare the quantum state with these amplitudes.
6. Add measurement operations on all qubits.
7. Return the constructed QuantumCircuit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
import math
import numpy as np
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.extensions import Initialize

def circuit_from_probability_dist(probability_dist: dict[int, float]) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Given a distribution as a dictionary of the form { measurement: probability },
    return a quantum circuit that produces that distribution upon measurement.

    Args:
        probability_dist (dict[int, float]): Mapping from integer measurement outcomes
                                             to their probabilities. The keys represent
                                             bitstrings as integers.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit that prepares the state corresponding to the
                        given probability distribution and measures all qubits.
    """
    if not probability_dist:
        raise ValueError("Input probability distribution is empty.")

    # Determine number of qubits needed from the maximum key
    max_key = max(probability_dist.keys())
    n = max_key.bit_length()  # number of qubits

    # If max_key is 0, bit_length() returns 0, so handle that case:
    if n == 0:
        n = 1

    dim = 2 ** n  # dimension of the state vector

    # Initialize amplitude vector with zeros
    amplitudes = np.zeros(dim, dtype=complex)

    # Fill amplitudes with sqrt of probabilities
    for key, prob in probability_dist.items():
        if key >= dim:
            raise ValueError(f"Key {key} exceeds the maximum state for {n} qubits.")
        if prob < 0:
            raise ValueError(f"Probability for key {key} is negative.")
        amplitudes[key] = math.sqrt(prob)

    # Normalize amplitudes to handle any floating point rounding errors
    norm = np.linalg.norm(amplitudes)
    if norm == 0:
        raise ValueError("Sum of probabilities is zero.")
    amplitudes /= norm

    # Create quantum circuit with n qubits and n classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n, n)

    # Initialize the quantum state to the desired amplitudes
    init_gate = Initialize(amplitudes)
    qc.append(init_gate, qc.qubits)

    # Add measurement on all qubits
    qc.measure(range(n), range(n))

    return qc
```

```python
def circuit_from_probability_dist(probability_dist: dict[int, float]) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Given a distribution as a dictionary of the form { measurement: probability },
    return a quantum circuit that produces that distribution upon measurement.

    Args:
        probability_dist (dict[int, float]): Mapping from integer measurement outcomes
                                             to their probabilities. The keys represent
                                             bitstrings as integers.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit that prepares the state corresponding to the
                        given probability distribution and measures all qubits.
    """

        raise ValueError("Input probability distribution is empty.")

    max_key = max(probability_dist.keys())
    n = max_key.bit_length()  # number of qubits

    # If max_key is 0, bit_length() returns 0, so handle that case:
    if n == 0:
        n = 1

    dim = 2 ** n  # dimension of the state vector

    # Initialize amplitude vector with zeros
    amplitudes = np.zeros(dim, dtype=complex)

    # Fill amplitudes with sqrt of probabilities
    for key, prob in probability_dist.items():

            raise ValueError(f"Key {key} exceeds the maximum state for {n} qubits.")
        if prob < 0:
            raise ValueError(f"Probability for key {key} is negative.")
        amplitudes[key] = math.sqrt(prob)

    norm = np.linalg.norm(amplitudes)

    state = Statevector(amplitudes)
```

=== [79/151] qiskitHumanEval/78 :: qft_no_swaps ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import QFT


def qft_no_swaps(num_qubits: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Return an inverse quantum Fourier transform circuit without the swap gates.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing an inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit without swap gates:

1. **Initialize Quantum Circuit**  
   - Create a quantum circuit with `num_qubits` qubits.  
   - No classical bits are needed unless measurement is desired later.

2. **Implement Inverse QFT Logic Without Swaps**  
   - The inverse QFT applies the inverse of the QFT unitary, which involves:  
     a) For qubit index `j` in [0, num_qubits-1] (starting from the most significant qubit):  
        - Apply a Hadamard gate on qubit `j`.  
        - Apply controlled phase rotations (with negative angles) from qubits with indices less than `j` to qubit `j`.  
     b) Unlike the standard QFT, **do not apply any swap gates** that reverse the order of qubits at the end.  
   - Use controlled phase rotations with angles:  
     \[
     R_k^\dagger = \begin{bmatrix}1 & 0 \\ 0 & e^{-2\pi i/2^k} \end{bmatrix}
     \]  
     where k is the distance between control and target qubits.

3. **Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements**  
   - The circuit uses `num_qubits` quantum registers.  
   - No ancilla qubits are required.  
   - The circuit depth scales approximately as O(num_qubits^2).

4. **Measurement Strategy**  
   - Measurement is not part of the inverse QFT itself and should be added separately if needed.  
   - The function returns the circuit implementing the inverse QFT without swaps, ready for integration in larger algorithms.

5. **Expected Outcomes**  
   - The resulting circuit performs the inverse QFT transform on the input quantum state, but the qubit order remains unaltered (i.e., no bit reversal occurs).  
   - This is suitable when the user wants to avoid the overhead of swap gates or handle qubit reordering classically.

6. **Acceptance Criteria**  
   - The circuit matches the standard inverse QFT unitary modulo the lack of swap gates.  
   - For input states, the output amplitudes correspond to the inverse QFT without qubit reordering.  
   - The code is well-structured, with clear documentation stating that swaps are omitted intentionally.  
   - The function is general for any `num_qubits >= 1`.  
   - The circuit uses only standard gates (Hadamard, controlled phase rotations) and no custom gates.  

This plan can be implemented directly using Qiskit's QuantumCircuit methods such as `h()`, `cp()` (controlled phase), and standard qubit indexing.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
import numpy as np

def qft_no_swaps(num_qubits: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create an inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit without the final swap gates.

    This circuit applies the inverse QFT unitary on `num_qubits` qubits but does NOT
    perform the usual qubit order reversal (swap gates) at the end.

    Args:
        num_qubits (int): Number of qubits in the circuit (>=1).

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit implementing the inverse QFT without swaps.
    """
    qc = QuantumCircuit(num_qubits)

    # Apply inverse QFT without swaps
    # Iterate over qubits from most significant (0) to least significant (num_qubits-1)
    for j in range(num_qubits):
        # Apply controlled phase rotations with negative angles from qubits with index < j to qubit j
        for k in range(j):
            # Calculate the angle for the controlled phase rotation
            angle = -2 * np.pi / (2 ** (j - k))
            qc.cp(angle, k, j)  # Controlled phase rotation with negative angle

        # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit j
        qc.h(j)

    return qc
```

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `count_gates(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> int` that returns the total number of unitary gates in a given Qiskit quantum circuit:

1. **Understand the task:**
   - The function input is a `QuantumCircuit` object.
   - The goal is to count the total number of *unitary gates* in the circuit.
   - Unitary gates include all quantum gates that transform the qubits' states unitarily.
   - Exclude non-unitary elements such as measurements, resets, barriers, and classical operations.

2. **Required quantum gates and operations:**
   - Consider standard unitary gates (e.g., X, H, CX, U3, RX, RY, RZ, etc.).
   - Custom gates that are unitary should also be counted.
   - Exclude instructions like `measure`, `reset`, `barrier`, `snapshot`, or classical instructions.

3. **Circuit structure and qubit requirements:**
   - The function does not need to modify the circuit or require additional qubits.
   - The function should iterate over the circuit’s instructions.

4. **Measurement strategy:**
   - No measurement or additional circuit operations are required.
   - The function only analyzes the circuit structure to count gates.

5. **Implementation steps:**
   - Access the list of instructions (operations) in the circuit, typically via `circuit.data`.
   - For each instruction, check its type or name.
   - Determine whether the instruction corresponds to a unitary gate:
     - Instructions with `instruction.definition` or `instruction.to_gate()` that are unitary.
     - Use Qiskit's `Gate` class to identify gates.
     - Filter out instructions named `measure`, `reset`, `barrier`, etc.
   - Count all instructions that qualify as unitary gates.
   - Return the total count.

6. **Expected outcome:**
   - The function returns an integer representing the total number of unitary gates in the input circuit.

7. **Acceptance criteria:**
   - The function accurately counts all unitary gates in circuits with standard and custom gates.
   - It excludes non-unitary instructions such as measurements, resets, barriers, and classical instructions.
   - It works for circuits of any size and complexity.
   - The function does not modify the input circuit.
   - The function handles empty circuits gracefully by returning zero.

This plan can be given to a Qiskit developer to implement the `count_gates` function as specified.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Gate

def count_gates(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> int:
    """Return the total number of unitary gates in the circuit.

    This counts all instructions that are instances of Gate,
    excluding non-unitary instructions like measure, reset, barrier, etc.
    """
    count = 0
    # List of instruction names to exclude (non-unitary)
    non_unitary_instructions = {'measure', 'reset', 'barrier', 'snapshot', 'delay', 'initialize'}

    for instruction, qargs, cargs in circuit.data:
        # Exclude known non-unitary instructions by name
        if instruction.name in non_unitary_instructions:
            continue
        # Count if the instruction is a Gate (unitary)
        if isinstance(instruction, Gate):
            count += 1
        else:
            # Some instructions may not be Gate but still unitary (e.g., composite instructions)
            # Try to convert to gate and check
            try:
                gate = instruction.to_gate()
                if isinstance(gate, Gate):
                    count += 1
            except Exception:
                # If cannot convert to gate, skip
                pass

    return count
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def convert_qasm_string_to_quantum_circuit() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Generate a QASM 2 string representing a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit.
    Then, convert this QASM 2 string into a QuantumCircuit object and return it.
    
    The Phi plus Bell state |Φ+> = (|00> + |11>) / √2 is prepared by:
    - Applying a Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    - Applying a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    
    # Export the circuit to a QASM 2 string
    qasm_str = qc.qasm()
    
    # Convert the QASM 2 string back into a QuantumCircuit object
    qc_from_qasm = QuantumCircuit.from_qasm_str(qasm_str)
    
    return qc_from_qasm

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def convert_qasm_string_to_quantum_circuit() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Generate a QASM 2 string representing a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit.
    Then, convert this QASM 2 string into a QuantumCircuit object and return it.
    
    The Phi plus Bell state |Φ+> = (|00> + |11>) / √2 is prepared by:
    - Applying a Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    - Applying a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    qc.h(0)
    
    # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    
    # Export the circuit to a QASM 2 string
    qasm_str = qc.qasm()
    
    # Convert the QASM 2 string back into a QuantumCircuit object
    qc_from_qasm = QuantumCircuit.from_qasm_str(qasm_str)
    
    return qc_from_qasm

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit import qpy

def create_binary_serialization():
    """Create a file containing the binary serialization of a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit
    and write it as 'bell.qpy' in binary mode.
    """
    # Step 1: Initialize a quantum circuit with 2 qubits and 2 classical bits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)
    
    # Step 2: Prepare the Phi plus Bell state
    qc.h(0)          # Apply Hadamard gate to qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    
    # Step 3: Add measurements to both qubits
    qc.measure(0, 0)
    qc.measure(1, 1)
    
    # Step 4: Serialize the circuit to binary using qpy and write to 'bell.qpy'
    with open('bell.qpy', 'wb') as file:
        qpy.dump([qc], file)
```

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import DensityMatrix, concurrence
def calculate_bell_state_properties():
    """ Construct a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit, compute its Density Matrix and Concurrence, and return these results in a tuple in the same order.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function to construct a Phi plus Bell state, compute its density matrix and concurrence:

1. Initialize the quantum circuit:
   - Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits, since a Bell state involves 2 qubits.

2. Prepare the Phi plus Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2:
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to qubit 0 to create superposition (|0⟩ + |1⟩)/√2.
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1 to entangle the two qubits, resulting in the Bell state.

3. Compute the density matrix:
   - Use the `DensityMatrix` class from Qiskit's `quantum_info` module to obtain the density matrix of the state prepared by the circuit.

4. Compute the concurrence:
   - Use the `concurrence` function from Qiskit's `quantum_info` module, passing in the density matrix to compute the concurrence, which quantifies the degree of entanglement.

5. Return results:
   - Return a tuple containing the density matrix and the concurrence value, in that order.

Acceptance criteria:
- The circuit must correctly prepare the Phi plus Bell state.
- The density matrix must represent the pure Bell state.
- The concurrence must be 1 for the maximally entangled Bell state.
- The function returns a tuple (DensityMatrix object, concurrence float).

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import DensityMatrix, concurrence

def calculate_bell_state_properties():
    """ 
    Construct a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit, compute its Density Matrix and Concurrence,
    and return these results in a tuple in the same order.
    """
    # Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Prepare the Phi plus Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2
    qc.h(0)        # Apply Hadamard gate to qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)    # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    
    # Obtain the density matrix of the state prepared by the circuit
    dm = DensityMatrix.from_instruction(qc)
    
    # Compute the concurrence of the density matrix
    conc = concurrence(dm)
    
    # Return the density matrix and concurrence as a tuple
    return dm, conc

# Test the function
if __name__ == "__main__":
    density_matrix, conc = calculate_bell_state_properties()
    print("Density Matrix:\n", density_matrix)
    print("Concurrence:", conc)

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeBelemV2
from qiskit.pulse import Schedule, DriveChannel, Gaussian

def define_gaussian_pulse_schedule():
    """
    Using Qiskit Pulse, create a Gaussian pulse schedule on drive channel 0 with a duration of 128
    and name this schedule 'gaussian_pulse_schedule'. Configure it for the FakeBelem backend and
    return the resulting pulse schedule.
    """
    # Instantiate the FakeBelemV2 backend to access its configuration and defaults
    backend = FakeBelemV2()

    # Define Gaussian pulse parameters
    duration = 128
    amplitude = 0.1  # Chosen amplitude, can be adjusted
    sigma = duration / 4  # Smooth Gaussian shape
    offset = 0  # Centered pulse

    # Create the Gaussian pulse
    gaussian_pulse = Gaussian(duration=duration, amp=amplitude, sigma=sigma, name="gaussian_pulse")

    # Create a pulse schedule and add the Gaussian pulse on DriveChannel(0) at time 0
    drive_chan = DriveChannel(0)
    gaussian_pulse_schedule = Schedule(name="gaussian_pulse_schedule")
    gaussian_pulse_schedule += gaussian_pulse(0, drive_chan)

    return gaussian_pulse_schedule
```

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeBelemV2
from qiskit.pulse import Schedule, DriveChannel, Gaussian

def define_gaussian_pulse_schedule():
    """
    Using Qiskit Pulse, create a Gaussian pulse schedule on drive channel 0 with a duration of 128
    and name this schedule 'gaussian_pulse_schedule'. Configure it for the FakeBelem backend and
    return the resulting pulse schedule.

    # Instantiate the FakeBelemV2 backend to access its configuration and defaults
    backend = FakeBelemV2()

    drive_chan = DriveChannel(0)
    gaussian_pulse_schedule = Schedule(name="gaussian_pulse_schedule")

    return gaussian_pulse_schedule
```

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeBelemV2
```
```python
from qiskit.providers.fake_provider.pulse import Schedule, DriveChannel, Gaussian

def define_gaussian_pulse_schedule():
    """
    Using Qiskit Pulse, create a Gaussian pulse schedule on drive channel 0 with a duration of 128
    and name this schedule 'gaussian_pulse_schedule'. Configure it for the FakeBelem backend and
    return the resulting pulse schedule.
    """
    backend = FakeBelemV2()

    duration = 128
    amplitude = 0.1
    sigma = duration / 4

    gaussian_pulse = Gaussian(duration=duration, amp=amplitude, sigma=sigma, name="gaussian_pulse")

    drive_chan = DriveChannel(0)
    gaussian_pulse_schedule = Schedule(name="gaussian_pulse_schedule")
    gaussian_pulse_schedule += gaussian_pulse(0, drive_chan)

    return gaussian_pulse_schedule
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def convert_quantum_circuit_to_qasm_string(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> str:
    """Given a QuantumCircuit, convert it into a QASM 2.0 string and return it.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): The quantum circuit to convert.

    Returns:
        str: The QASM 2.0 string representation of the circuit.

    Raises:
        QiskitError: If the circuit contains operations that cannot be converted to QASM 2.0.
    """
    # Use the built-in qasm() method to get the QASM 2.0 string representation
    qasm_str = circuit.qasm()
    return qasm_str
```

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeBelemV2
from qiskit.pulse import Schedule, DriveChannel, Constant, Play, Delay

def pulse_schedule_with_constant_and_delay():
    """ Using Qiskit Pulse, create a schedule with a constant pulse on drive channel 0,
    featuring a duration of 160 and an amplitude of 0.1 and name this schedule
    "pulse_schedule_with_constant_and_delay". Use the FakeBelem backend for configuration.
    After creating the pulse, add a delay of 400 to the schedule, then replay the same pulse.
    Return the completed pulse schedule.
    """
    # Load the FakeBelemV2 backend to get configuration (not strictly needed here but per instructions)
    backend = FakeBelemV2()

    # Define pulse parameters
    duration = 160
    amplitude = 0.1

    # Create a constant pulse with given duration and amplitude
    const_pulse = Constant(duration=duration, amp=amplitude)

    # Define the drive channel for qubit 0
    drive_chan = DriveChannel(0)

    # Create a new schedule with the specified name
    sched = Schedule(name="pulse_schedule_with_constant_and_delay")

    # Add the first constant pulse starting at time 0
    sched += Play(const_pulse, drive_chan)  # starts at t=0

    # Add a delay of 400 time units after the first pulse
    sched += Delay(400, drive_chan)

    # Add the same constant pulse again after the delay
    sched += Play(const_pulse, drive_chan)

    return sched
```

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def quantum_circuit_with_delay():
    """
    Create a one-qubit quantum circuit, apply Hadamard gate,
    then add a delay of 100 units, then apply Hadamard gate again,
    and return the circuit.
    """
    # Initialize a quantum circuit with 1 qubit
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(1)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate to put qubit in superposition
    circuit.h(0)
    
    # Add a delay of 100 units on the qubit
    circuit.delay(100, 0)
    
    # Apply Hadamard gate again to return qubit to computational basis
    circuit.h(0)
    
    return circuit

# Example usage:
# qc = quantum_circuit_with_delay()
# print(qc.draw())

=== [89/151] qiskitHumanEval/88 :: create_conditional_circuit ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister
from qiskit.circuit import IfElseOp
def create_conditional_circuit():
    """ Create a one-qubit quantum circuit, apply a Hadamard gate, and then measure it. Based on the classical output, use an IfElseOp operation: if the output is 1, append a one-qubit quantum circuit with a Z gate; if the output is 0, append a one-qubit quantum circuit with an X gate. Finally, add a measurement to the appended circuit and return the complete quantum circuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the requested conditional quantum circuit using Qiskit:

1. Quantum and Classical Registers Initialization:
   - Initialize a quantum register with 1 qubit (q0).
   - Initialize a classical register with 1 bit (c0) for measurement results.

2. Initial Circuit Construction:
   - Create a quantum circuit with the above quantum and classical registers.
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to the qubit q0 to create a superposition.
   - Measure qubit q0 into classical bit c0.

3. Conditional Circuit Branches:
   - Define two one-qubit subcircuits to be conditionally applied:
     a) Circuit_If_One:
        - Single qubit quantum circuit with 1 qubit.
        - Apply a Z gate to the qubit.
        - Add a measurement of this qubit to a new classical bit.
     b) Circuit_If_Zero:
        - Single qubit quantum circuit with 1 qubit.
        - Apply an X gate to the qubit.
        - Add a measurement of this qubit to a new classical bit.

4. Integration of Conditional Operation:
   - Use Qiskit's IfElseOp or equivalent conditional operation to append Circuit_If_One if classical bit c0 == 1.
   - Append Circuit_If_Zero if classical bit c0 == 0.

5. Circuit Structure and Qubit Management:
   - To avoid qubit overlaps and conflicts:
     - The original qubit q0 can be reused since the conditional operations happen after measurement.
     - Alternatively, use a second qubit (q1) for the conditional subcircuits to keep circuits modular and measurements distinct.
   - Similarly, allocate separate classical bits for measurements in the conditional subcircuits.

6. Measurement Strategy:
   - The first measurement collapses the qubit state and determines which conditional subcircuit is applied.
   - The conditional subcircuit's measurement captures the effect of either Z or X gate.
   - All classical bits should be distinct to store all measurement outcomes clearly.

7. Expected Outcome:
   - The first measurement yields 0 or 1 with equal probability (due to Hadamard).
   - If the outcome is 1, the Z gate is applied and measured (should flip phase but measurement in computational basis remains the same).
   - If the outcome is 0, the X gate is applied and measured (flips the qubit state).
   - Final measurements reflect the application of the conditional gates based on the initial measurement.

8. Acceptance Criteria:
   - The circuit contains at least one qubit and one classical bit for the initial measurement.
   - Conditional application of Z or X gates is correctly implemented using IfElseOp or equivalent.
   - Measurements exist after both the initial Hadamard circuit and the conditional gate applications.
   - Classical bits are distinct and correctly assigned.
   - The circuit executes without errors and returns measurement results consistent with the conditional logic.

Summary:
- Initialize 1 qubit and 1 classical bit.
- Apply H, measure, store in c0.
- Based on c0, conditionally apply either Z or X gate on a qubit (same or separate).
- Measure the qubit again into a new classical bit.
- Use IfElseOp to implement conditional branching.
- Return the composed quantum circuit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister
from qiskit.circuit import IfElseOp

def create_conditional_circuit():
    """
    Create a one-qubit quantum circuit, apply a Hadamard gate, and then measure it.
    Based on the classical output, use an IfElseOp operation:
    - if the output is 1, append a one-qubit quantum circuit with a Z gate;
    - if the output is 0, append a one-qubit quantum circuit with an X gate.
    Finally, add a measurement to the appended circuit and return the complete quantum circuit.
    """
    # Initialize quantum and classical registers
    qr = QuantumRegister(2, 'q')  # q[0] for initial H and measurement, q[1] for conditional gates
    cr = ClassicalRegister(2, 'c')  # c[0] for initial measurement, c[1] for conditional measurement

    # Create main circuit
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr)

    # Step 1: Apply Hadamard to q0 and measure into c0
    circuit.h(qr[0])
    circuit.measure(qr[0], cr[0])

    # Define the subcircuits for conditional operations on q1
    # Circuit if classical bit c0 == 1: apply Z gate and measure
    circuit_if_one = QuantumCircuit(1, 1)
    circuit_if_one.z(0)
    circuit_if_one.measure(0, 0)

    # Circuit if classical bit c0 == 0: apply X gate and measure
    circuit_if_zero = QuantumCircuit(1, 1)
    circuit_if_zero.x(0)
    circuit_if_zero.measure(0, 0)

    # Create IfElseOp with condition on classical bit c0
    # Condition: cr[0] == 1
    condition = (cr, 0, 1)  # (classical register, bit index, value)

    # Create IfElseOp
    if_else_op = IfElseOp(condition, circuit_if_one.to_instruction(), circuit_if_zero.to_instruction())

    # Append the conditional operation on qubit q1 and classical bit c1
    # Note: The subcircuits use 1 qubit and 1 classical bit, so we map them to qr[1] and cr[1]
    circuit.append(if_else_op, [qr[1]], [cr[1]])

    return circuit
```

=== [90/151] qiskitHumanEval/89 :: create_controlled_hgate ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.circuit.library import HGate
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister
def create_controlled_hgate()->QuantumCircuit:
    """ Construct a quantum circuit with a three-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate, using qubit 0 and qubit 1 as the control bits and qubit 2 as the target bit. Return the circuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement a three-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate with qubits 0 and 1 as controls and qubit 2 as the target, here is a detailed plan:

**Plan for Implementing a Three-Qubit Controlled-Hadamard Gate**

1. **Qubits and Circuit Initialization**
   - Initialize a quantum circuit with 3 qubits (qubit 0, qubit 1, and qubit 2).
   - Qubit 0 and qubit 1 will serve as control qubits.
   - Qubit 2 will serve as the target qubit for the Hadamard operation.

2. **Gate Requirements**
   - The Hadamard gate (H) is the target operation.
   - There is no direct 3-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate in the standard Qiskit gate library.
   - You will need to decompose the controlled-controlled-Hadamard (CC-H) gate into basic gates or use a custom decomposition.

3. **Possible Decomposition Approach**
   - Use two-controlled gates such as Toffoli (CCX) and single-qubit gates to construct the controlled-Hadamard.
   - Alternatively, use ancilla qubits if necessary, but since only 3 qubits are allowed, decomposition must be done without ancillas.
   - One known approach:
     - Express the controlled-Hadamard as a controlled-controlled rotation around the Y or Z axis combined with single-qubit gates. For example, the Hadamard can be represented as rotations, and controlled-controlled rotations can be implemented using standard gates.
   - Another approach is to use the decomposition technique based on the fact that H = RY(pi/2) * RZ(pi).
   - Implement controlled-controlled-RY and controlled-controlled-RZ gates using standard controlled gates (CCX and single controlled gates) and rotations.

4. **Circuit Structure**
   - Start with the control qubits.
   - Apply the necessary gates to implement the CC-H gate on the target qubit conditioned on both control qubits being in the |1⟩ state.
   - The circuit will include:
     - Controlled-controlled rotations (implemented via Toffoli gates and single-qubit rotations)
     - Possibly single-qubit gates on the target qubit before and after these controlled operations to match the Hadamard gate

5. **Measurement**
   - Since the function only returns the circuit with the controlled-Hadamard gate, no measurement is required at this stage.
   - The circuit should be ready for integration into larger circuits or simulations.

6. **Expected Outcome**
   - The resulting circuit applies the Hadamard gate to the target qubit if and only if both control qubits are in the |1⟩ state.
   - For all other control states, the target qubit remains unchanged.

7. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - The circuit contains 3 qubits, with qubits 0 and 1 as controls and qubit 2 as target.
   - The circuit implements a three-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate without ancilla qubits.
   - No direct CC-H gate is used (since it does not exist natively).
   - The circuit matches the decomposition of the controlled-controlled-Hadamard gate.
   - The function returns the constructed QuantumCircuit object.

**Summary:**
- Initialize a 3-qubit circuit.
- Decompose the CC-H gate into a sequence of standard gates (Toffoli, controlled rotations, single-qubit rotations).
- Apply these gates conditioned on control qubits 0 and 1.
- Return the final circuit without measurements.

This plan can be handed off to a Qiskit developer to implement the `create_controlled_hgate` function correctly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from math import pi

def create_controlled_hgate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Construct a quantum circuit with a three-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate,
    using qubit 0 and qubit 1 as the control bits and qubit 2 as the target bit.
    Return the circuit.
    """
    # Initialize a 3-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(3)

    # The Hadamard gate can be decomposed as: H = RY(pi/2) * RZ(pi)
    # We want to implement a controlled-controlled-Hadamard (CC-H) gate on qubit 2
    # controlled by qubits 0 and 1.

    # Step 1: Apply controlled-controlled-RZ(pi) on qubit 2 with controls 0 and 1
    # CC-RZ(pi) can be implemented by conjugating a Toffoli with RZ(pi/2) rotations:
    # CC-RZ(θ) = Toffoli -> RZ(θ) -> Toffoli
    # But since RZ is diagonal, we can implement CC-RZ(pi) as:
    # Toffoli(controls 0,1 target 2)
    # RZ(pi) on qubit 2
    # Toffoli(controls 0,1 target 2)

    # Step 2: Apply controlled-controlled-RY(pi/2) on qubit 2 with controls 0 and 1
    # CC-RY can be decomposed using single-qubit rotations and Toffoli gates:
    # CC-RY(θ) = Ry(θ/2) -> Toffoli -> Ry(-θ/2) -> Toffoli

    # Implement CC-RZ(pi)
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli
    qc.rz(pi, 2)             # RZ(pi) on target
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli

    # Implement CC-RY(pi/2)
    qc.ry(pi/4, 2)           # Ry(pi/4) on target (half of pi/2)
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli
    qc.ry(-pi/4, 2)          # Ry(-pi/4) on target
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli

    return qc
```

I will now test this code to ensure it runs correctly.

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from math import pi

def create_controlled_hgate() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Construct a quantum circuit with a three-qubit controlled-Hadamard gate,
    using qubit 0 and qubit 1 as the control bits and qubit 2 as the target bit.
    Return the circuit.
    """
    # Initialize a 3-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(3)

    # The Hadamard gate can be decomposed as: H = RY(pi/2) * RZ(pi)
    # We want to implement a controlled-controlled-Hadamard (CC-H) gate on qubit 2
    # controlled by qubits 0 and 1.

    # Step 1: Apply controlled-controlled-RZ(pi) on qubit 2 with controls 0 and 1
    # CC-RZ(pi) can be implemented by conjugating a Toffoli with RZ(pi) rotations:
    # CC-RZ(θ) = Toffoli -> RZ(θ) -> Toffoli

    # Step 2: Apply controlled-controlled-RY(pi/2) on qubit 2 with controls 0 and 1
    # CC-RY(θ) = Ry(θ/2) -> Toffoli -> Ry(-θ/2) -> Toffoli

    # Implement CC-RZ(pi)
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli
    qc.rz(pi, 2)             # RZ(pi) on target
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli

    # Implement CC-RY(pi/2)
    qc.ry(pi/4, 2)           # Ry(pi/4) on target (half of pi/2)
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli
    qc.ry(-pi/4, 2)          # Ry(-pi/4) on target
    qc.ccx(0, 1, 2)          # Toffoli

    return qc
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_custom_controlled() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Create a custom 2-qubit gate with an X gate on qubit 0 and an H gate on qubit 1.
        Then, add two control qubits to this gate. Apply this controlled gate to a 4-qubit circuit,
        using qubits 0 and 3 as controls and qubits 1 and 2 as targets.
        Return the final circuit.
    """
    # Create a 4-qubit circuit for the final circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(4)

    # Define the 2-qubit custom gate circuit
    custom_gate_circuit = QuantumCircuit(2, name='custom')
    custom_gate_circuit.x(0)  # X gate on qubit 0 of the custom gate
    custom_gate_circuit.h(1)  # H gate on qubit 1 of the custom gate

    # Convert the custom circuit to a gate
    custom_gate = custom_gate_circuit.to_gate()

    # Add 2 control qubits to the custom gate
    controlled_custom_gate = custom_gate.control(num_ctrl_qubits=2)

    # Apply the controlled custom gate on the 4-qubit circuit
    # Control qubits: 0 and 3
    # Target qubits: 1 and 2
    # The controlled gate expects qubits in order: controls then targets
    qc.append(controlled_custom_gate, [0, 3, 1, 2])

    return qc

# Test the function by creating the circuit and drawing it
if __name__ == "__main__":
    circuit = create_custom_controlled()
    print(circuit.draw(output='text'))

Plan for implementing the function `convert_circuit_to_instruction` that creates a Bell state |Φ+⟩ and converts it to a quantum instruction:

1. Initialize a quantum circuit with 2 qubits:
   - The Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2 requires 2 qubits.

2. Prepare the Bell state |Φ+⟩:
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to qubit 0. This creates a superposition (|0⟩ + |1⟩)/√2.
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1. This entangles the two qubits producing the Bell state |Φ+⟩.

3. Convert the circuit to a quantum instruction:
   - Use `circuit_to_instruction` function from Qiskit converters to convert the above circuit into an instruction.
   - Name this instruction `bell_instruction`.

4. No measurement is needed in this function as the goal is to create and convert the circuit to an instruction, not to measure outcomes.

5. Expected outcome:
   - A quantum instruction named `bell_instruction` that, when appended to other circuits, produces the |Φ+⟩ Bell state on two qubits.

Acceptance criteria:
- The function defines and returns (or assigns) an instruction named `bell_instruction`.
- The instruction corresponds exactly to the circuit that creates the |Φ+⟩ Bell state.
- The instruction can be used as a reusable component in other circuits.

This plan can be translated into a Qiskit implementation by a developer.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.converters import circuit_to_instruction

def convert_circuit_to_instruction():
    """Create a circuit that produces a phi plus Bell state and convert it into a quantum instruction.

    Returns:
        QuantumInstruction: An instruction that prepares the |Φ+⟩ Bell state on 2 qubits.
    """
    # Initialize a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    bell_circuit = QuantumCircuit(2, name='bell_circuit')

    # Apply Hadamard gate to qubit 0 to create superposition
    bell_circuit.h(0)

    # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1 to entangle qubits
    bell_circuit.cx(0, 1)

    # Convert the circuit to a quantum instruction named 'bell_instruction'
    bell_instruction = circuit_to_instruction(bell_circuit)
    bell_instruction.name = 'bell_instruction'

    return bell_instruction
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import StabilizerState

def calculate_stabilizer_state_info():
    """ 
    Construct a Phi plus Bell state quantum circuit, compute its stabilizer state, 
    and return both the stabilizer state and a dictionary of the stabilizer state measurement probabilities.
    """
    # Initialize a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    
    # Prepare the |Φ+⟩ Bell state: (|00⟩ + |11⟩) / sqrt(2)
    qc.h(0)       # Apply Hadamard gate on qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)   # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    
    # Create the stabilizer state from the circuit
    stab_state = StabilizerState(qc)
    
    # Get the measurement probabilities in computational basis
    probs = stab_state.probabilities_dict()
    
    # Return the stabilizer state and the probabilities dictionary
    return stab_state, probs

# Example usage:
# stab_state, probs = calculate_stabilizer_state_info()
# print(probs)  # Expected: {'00': 0.5, '11': 0.5}

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.synthesis import synth_clifford_full
from qiskit.quantum_info.random import random_clifford

def synthesize_clifford_circuit(n_qubits: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a random Clifford circuit using the random_clifford function for a given number of qubits
    with seed 1234 and synthesize it using synth_clifford_full method.

    Args:
        n_qubits (int): Number of qubits for the Clifford circuit.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit implementing the synthesized Clifford operator.
    """
    # Generate a random Clifford operator on n_qubits with fixed seed for reproducibility
    clifford_op = random_clifford(n_qubits, seed=1234)

    # Synthesize the Clifford operator into a QuantumCircuit
    clifford_circuit = synth_clifford_full(clifford_op)

    return clifford_circuit
```

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
import qiskit.qasm3

def convert_quantum_circuit_to_qasm_string(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> str:
    """ Given a Quantum Circuit as the argument, convert it into qasm3 string and return it.
    """
    # Use qasm3.dumps to serialize the QuantumCircuit into a QASM3 formatted string
    qasm3_string = qiskit.qasm3.dumps(circuit)
    return qasm3_string


# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Create a simple quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)
    qc.h(0)
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    qc.measure([0, 1], [0, 1])

    # Convert to QASM3 string
    qasm3_str = convert_quantum_circuit_to_qasm_string(qc)
    print("QASM3 output:\n", qasm3_str)
