from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.transpiler.passes import RemoveBarriers
def remove_barrier(circuit: QuantumCircuit):
    """ For a given Quantum Circuit remove all the barriers from it and return.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement a function that removes all barriers from a given QuantumCircuit:

1. Input:
   - A QuantumCircuit object which may contain barrier instructions interspersed among other gates.

2. Objective:
   - Remove all barrier instructions from the circuit while preserving the order and functionality of all other gates.
   - Return a new QuantumCircuit object (or modify the input circuit) with no barrier instructions.

3. Approach:
   - QuantumCircuit objects store instructions in an ordered list called `data`.
   - Each element in `data` is a tuple containing (Instruction, qargs, cargs).
   - Barrier instructions are identifiable by their instruction name being "barrier".
   - To remove barriers, iterate over `circuit.data` and filter out instructions whose name is "barrier".
   - Create a new QuantumCircuit with the same number of qubits and classical bits.
   - Append all instructions except barriers to the new circuit in the same order.

4. Required Quantum Gates and Operations:
   - No quantum gates are added or modified; only barrier instructions are removed.
   - The circuit's quantum and classical registers remain unchanged.

5. Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements:
   - The new circuit should have the same qubits and classical bits as the original.

6. Measurement Strategy:
   - No changes to measurement instructions.

7. Expected Outcome:
   - Output circuit is functionally identical to the original but without any barrier instructions.
   - The output circuit can be executed or transpiled without barriers.

8. Acceptance Criteria:
   - All barrier instructions are removed.
   - The relative order of other instructions remains unchanged.
   - The number of qubits and classical bits remains the same.
   - The output circuit produces the same results as the input circuit when executed, modulo any effects barriers might have on transpilation or optimization.
   - The function returns a QuantumCircuit object.

Summary:
- Input: QuantumCircuit with barriers.
- Process: Filter out barrier instructions.
- Output: QuantumCircuit without barriers, identical otherwise.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def remove_barrier(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """For a given Quantum Circuit remove all the barriers from it and return a new circuit."""
    # Create a new QuantumCircuit with the same quantum and classical registers
    new_circuit = QuantumCircuit(*circuit.qregs, *circuit.cregs)
    
    # Iterate over all instructions in the original circuit
    for instr, qargs, cargs in circuit.data:
        # Append only instructions that are not barriers
        if instr.name != "barrier":
            new_circuit.append(instr, qargs, cargs)
    
    return new_circuit

# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    from qiskit import Aer, execute
    # Create a circuit with barriers
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)
    qc.h(0)
    qc.barrier()
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    qc.barrier()
    qc.measure([0,1], [0,1])
    
    # Remove barriers
    qc_no_barrier = remove_barrier(qc)
    
    # Execute both circuits to verify they produce the same results
    backend = Aer.get_backend('qasm_simulator')
    result_with_barrier = execute(qc, backend, shots=1024).result()
    result_no_barrier = execute(qc_no_barrier, backend, shots=1024).result()
    
    counts_with_barrier = result_with_barrier.get_counts()
    counts_no_barrier = result_no_barrier.get_counts()
    
    print("Counts with barrier:", counts_with_barrier)
    print("Counts without barrier:", counts_no_barrier)

from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
def bell_state_noisy() -> dict:
    """ Create the phi+ Bell state, run it in the FakeKyoto backend and return the counts. Use seed 42 for the sampler.
    """

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `bell_state_noisy()` as requested:

1. **Initialize Quantum Circuit:**
   - Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits (qubit 0 and qubit 1).
   - Add 2 classical bits to store measurement results.

2. **Prepare the Bell State |Φ+⟩:**
   - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) to qubit 0.
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1.
   - This prepares the Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2.

3. **Measurement:**
   - Measure both qubits into their corresponding classical bits.

4. **Use FakeKyoto Backend:**
   - Instantiate the `FakeKyoto` backend from `qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider`.
   - This simulates a noisy environment similar to IBM's Kyoto quantum device.

5. **Configure Sampler:**
   - Create a `Sampler` object using the `FakeKyoto` backend.
   - Set the random seed to 42 to ensure reproducibility.
   - Optionally, generate and set a preset pass manager for optimization using `generate_preset_pass_manager` if desired.

6. **Run Sampling:**
   - Execute the sampler on the prepared circuit.
   - Use a suitable number of shots, e.g., 1024, to get statistically significant results.

7. **Return Measurement Counts:**
   - Extract the counts (measurement results histogram) from the sampler's output.
   - Return the counts as a dictionary mapping bitstrings to counts.

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The function returns a dictionary of counts with bitstrings of length 2 (e.g., '00', '11', '01', '10').
- The counts reflect the noisy Bell state from the FakeKyoto backend.
- The sampling uses seed 42 to guarantee reproducibility.
- The circuit preparation correctly creates the |Φ+⟩ Bell state.
- The entire process uses Qiskit's IBM Runtime `Sampler` API with the `FakeKyoto` backend.

This plan enables a Qiskit developer to implement `bell_state_noisy()` fulfilling the requirements of creating and sampling a noisy Bell state with a fixed random seed on the FakeKyoto backend.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeKyoto
from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Sampler
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.options import SamplerOptions
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager

def bell_state_noisy() -> dict:
    """Create the phi+ Bell state, run it in the FakeKyoto backend and return the counts.
    Uses seed 42 for the sampler to ensure reproducibility.
    """
    # Create a 2-qubit quantum circuit with 2 classical bits for measurement
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2, 2)
    
    # Prepare the Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2
    qc.h(0)          # Hadamard on qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)      # CNOT with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    
    # Measure both qubits
    qc.measure([0, 1], [0, 1])
    
    # Instantiate the FakeKyoto backend to simulate noise
    backend = FakeKyoto()
    
    # Generate a preset pass manager for optimization targeting the backend
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(backend=backend, optimization_level=3)
    
    # Create a Sampler with the FakeKyoto backend and set seed for reproducibility
    sampler = Sampler(
        backend=backend,
        options=SamplerOptions(seed=42, shots=1024),
        pass_manager=pass_manager
    )
    
    # Run the sampler on the circuit
    result = sampler.run(qc).result()
    
    # Extract counts from the result
    counts = result.quasi_dists[0].binary_probabilities()
    
    # Convert probabilities to counts by multiplying by shots and rounding
    shots = 1024
    counts_dict = {bitstr: int(round(prob * shots)) for bitstr, prob in counts.items()}
    
    return counts_dict
```

Here is a detailed plan to implement the `two_qubit_connections` function as described:

---

### Task
Implement a function `two_qubit_connections` that, given a backend instance (either `FakeBackendV2` or `IBMBackend`), returns a list of the two-qubit connections available on that backend.

---

### Implementation Plan

1. **Function Input:**
   - Accept a single argument `backend` which can be an instance of either:
     - `FakeBackendV2` (a simulated backend for testing)
     - `IBMBackend` (a real or simulated IBM Quantum backend)

2. **Retrieve Coupling Map:**
   - Both backend types provide a coupling map or similar attribute that describes the allowed two-qubit gates (connections) between qubits.
   - For `FakeBackendV2`:
     - Use the method or property that returns the coupling map. Typically, `backend.configuration().coupling_map` or a similar attribute.
   - For `IBMBackend`:
     - The coupling map can be accessed via `backend.coupling_map` or from `backend.backend_properties()`.
   - Ensure the code is robust to access the coupling map regardless of backend type.

3. **Format Output:**
   - The coupling map is usually represented as a list of pairs (tuples or lists), where each pair `[q1, q2]` indicates a directional two-qubit gate is possible from qubit `q1` to `q2`.
   - Return this list as-is.

4. **Edge Cases and Validation:**
   - Validate the `backend` argument type and raise a meaningful exception if the input is invalid.
   - If the coupling map is not available or empty, return an empty list.

5. **Documentation:**
   - The function docstring should clearly describe the input and output.
   - Mention that the connections are directional (if applicable).

---

### Summary of Required Operations

- Query the backend configuration or properties for coupling map.
- Extract the list of two-qubit connections.
- Return the list in a consistent format.

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- The function accepts an instance of either `FakeBackendV2` or `IBMBackend`.
- The function returns a list of two-qubit connections as pairs of qubit indices.
- The returned list matches the backend's actual coupling map.
- The function handles invalid inputs by raising an appropriate exception or returning an empty list.
- The function is documented with clear input/output descriptions.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider.fake_backend import FakeBackendV2
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import IBMBackend
from typing import Union, List, Tuple


def two_qubit_connections(
    backend: Union[FakeBackendV2, IBMBackend]
) -> List[Tuple[int, int]]:
    """
    Return the two-qubit connections (coupling map) for the given backend.

    Args:
        backend (Union[FakeBackendV2, IBMBackend]): The backend instance to query.

    Returns:
        List[Tuple[int, int]]: A list of tuples representing directional two-qubit connections.
                               Each tuple (q1, q2) means a two-qubit gate is possible from q1 to q2.

    Raises:
        TypeError: If the backend is not an instance of FakeBackendV2 or IBMBackend.
    """
    # Validate backend type
    if not isinstance(backend, (FakeBackendV2, IBMBackend)):
        raise TypeError(
            "backend must be an instance of FakeBackendV2 or IBMBackend"
        )

    # Try to get coupling map from backend configuration
    coupling_map = None

    # For FakeBackendV2, coupling map is in configuration().coupling_map
    if isinstance(backend, FakeBackendV2):
        config = backend.configuration()
        coupling_map = config.coupling_map if hasattr(config, "coupling_map") else None

    # For IBMBackend, coupling map is directly accessible as a property
    elif isinstance(backend, IBMBackend):
        # IBMBackend has coupling_map attribute
        coupling_map = getattr(backend, "coupling_map", None)

        # If coupling_map is None, try to get from backend properties
        if coupling_map is None:
            # backend_properties() returns BackendProperties object, which does not have coupling_map
            # So fallback to configuration
            config = backend.configuration()
            coupling_map = config.coupling_map if hasattr(config, "coupling_map") else None

    # If coupling_map is None or empty, return empty list
    if not coupling_map:
        return []

    # Ensure output is list of tuples
    return [tuple(connection) for connection in coupling_map]
```

=== [99/151] qiskitHumanEval/98 :: qubit_with_least_readout_error ===

import numpy as np
from typing import Union

def qubit_with_least_readout_error(backend: Union[IBMBackend, FakeBackendV2])-> float:
    """ Return the minimum readout error of any input backend of type FakeBackendV2, IBMBackend.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function `qubit_with_least_readout_error`:

1. **Function Input:**
   - The function takes a single argument `backend`, which can be either an instance of `IBMBackend` or `FakeBackendV2`.

2. **Accessing Readout Error Data:**
   - Both `IBMBackend` and `FakeBackendV2` provide backend properties, accessible via `backend.properties()`.
   - The `backend.properties()` returns an object containing calibration data, including readout errors per qubit.
   - Readout errors are typically found in the measurement assignment probabilities or as a property/measurement error in the backend properties.
   
   Specifically:
   - For each qubit, the readout error can be derived from the assignment probabilities of measuring |0> as |1> and vice versa.
   - Alternatively, the backend properties may include measurement error rates directly under measurement calibration data.
   
3. **Extracting Readout Errors per Qubit:**
   - Iterate over all qubits in the backend.
   - For each qubit, extract the readout error rate.
   - The error is usually defined as the probability that the measurement result is flipped (false positive or false negative).
   - If the backend provides assignment probabilities, calculate readout error as:
     ```
     readout_error = 1 - (probability of correct assignment)
     ```
     For example, if the probability of measuring |0> when the qubit is actually in |0> is p0, and the probability of measuring |1> when the qubit is actually in |1> is p1, then:
     ```
     average_readout_error = 1 - (p0 + p1)/2
     ```
   - If the backend properties provide readout errors directly, use those values.

4. **Finding the Minimum Readout Error:**
   - After computing the readout error per qubit, find and return the minimum among these values.

5. **Return Value:**
   - The function returns a `float` representing the smallest readout error among all qubits in the given backend.

---

**Acceptance Criteria:**

- The function accepts a backend instance of type `IBMBackend` or `FakeBackendV2`.
- It correctly accesses the backend properties and extracts readout error information for each qubit.
- It computes the readout errors accurately.
- It returns the minimum readout error as a `float`.
- The function handles backends that have consistent measurement calibration data.
- If measurement data is absent or incomplete, the function should handle gracefully (e.g., raise an informative exception or skip qubits without data).
- The function does not perform any quantum circuit operations or measurements.
- The function is well documented with docstrings explaining input, output, and logic.

---

This plan provides the detailed requirements and design for a Qiskit developer to implement the function according to the request.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
import numpy as np
from typing import Union
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider.fake_backend import FakeBackendV2
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import IBMBackend

def qubit_with_least_readout_error(backend: Union[IBMBackend, FakeBackendV2]) -> float:
    """
    Return the minimum average readout error of any qubit in the given backend.

    The readout error per qubit is computed as:
        1 - (p0_correct + p1_correct) / 2
    where p0_correct is the probability of measuring '0' when the qubit is in |0>,
    and p1_correct is the probability of measuring '1' when the qubit is in |1>.

    Args:
        backend (Union[IBMBackend, FakeBackendV2]): The backend instance to query.

    Returns:
        float: The minimum average readout error among all qubits.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If no measurement assignment probabilities are found in backend properties.
    """
    properties = backend.properties()
    if properties is None:
        raise ValueError("Backend properties are not available.")

    readout_errors = []

    # Iterate over qubits in the backend properties
    for qubit_index in range(len(properties.qubits)):
        # For each qubit, find measurement assignment probabilities
        # These are stored as backend properties of type 'measurement_assignment'
        # with parameters indicating the assignment probabilities.
        # We look for parameters with name 'assignment' and target qubit index.
        assignment_probs = {}
        for prop in properties.qubits[qubit_index]:
            if prop.name == "assignment":
                # prop.value is the assignment probability
                # prop.parameter_name indicates the bitstring measured
                # prop.parameter_name is not always present, so we check prop.parameter_name or prop.parameter_label
                # In Qiskit, the parameter name for assignment probabilities is stored in prop.parameter_name or prop.parameter_label
                # But in IBMBackendProperties, the parameter name is in prop.parameter_name or prop.parameter_label
                # We check prop.parameter_name or prop.parameter_label for the bitstring
                bitstring = None
                if hasattr(prop, "parameter_name") and prop.parameter_name is not None:
                    bitstring = prop.parameter_name
                elif hasattr(prop, "parameter_label") and prop.parameter_label is not None:
                    bitstring = prop.parameter_label
                else:
                    # fallback: use prop.name (unlikely)
                    bitstring = prop.name

                # We expect bitstring to be '0' or '1' for single qubit
                assignment_probs[bitstring] = prop.value

        # If assignment probabilities for both '0' and '1' are found, compute readout error
        if '0' in assignment_probs and '1' in assignment_probs:
            p0_correct = assignment_probs['0']  # prob(measure 0 | prepared 0)
            p1_correct = assignment_probs['1']  # prob(measure 1 | prepared 1)
            avg_readout_error = 1 - (p0_correct + p1_correct) / 2
            readout_errors.append(avg_readout_error)
        else:
            # If assignment probabilities are not found for this qubit, skip it
            continue

    if not readout_errors:
        raise ValueError("No measurement assignment probabilities found in backend properties.")

    return min(readout_errors)
```

from qiskit.circuit import Parameter, QuantumCircuit

def remove_unassigned_parameterized_gates(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Remove all the gates with unassigned parameters from the given circuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement the function `remove_unassigned_parameterized_gates(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> QuantumCircuit` that removes all gates containing unassigned (unbound) parameters from a given parameterized quantum circuit, here is a detailed plan:

---

### Implementation Plan

1. **Input**: A `QuantumCircuit` object that may contain parameterized gates with some parameters assigned (bound) and some unassigned (unbound).

2. **Goal**: Produce a new `QuantumCircuit` object where all gates that depend on any unassigned parameters are removed. Gates with fully assigned (bound) parameters or no parameters at all should remain.

3. **Required Quantum Gates and Operations**:
   - No new quantum gates need to be added or changed.
   - The function acts as a filter on the existing circuit operations.

4. **Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements**:
   - The new circuit should have the same number of qubits and classical bits as the input circuit.
   - The order of remaining gates should be preserved.

5. **Detailed Steps**:
   - Initialize a new empty circuit with the same number of qubits and classical bits as the input.
   - Iterate over all instructions in the input circuit.
   - For each instruction:
     - Identify if the instruction is parameterized:
       - Check if the instruction has parameters.
     - For parameterized instructions:
       - Check if all parameters are bound (i.e., replaced by numeric values).
       - If any parameter is unassigned (still a `Parameter` object), skip this gate.
     - For non-parameterized instructions or parameterized instructions with all parameters bound:
       - Append this instruction to the new circuit, preserving its qubits and clbits.
   - Return the new circuit.

6. **Measurement Strategy**:
   - Measurements are preserved if they remain in the circuit.
   - There is no change to measurement operations; if they are parameterized (rare), they are treated the same way.

7. **Expected Outcome**:
   - The returned circuit contains only gates without unassigned parameters.
   - The circuit is otherwise identical in qubit count, classical bits, and gate order (minus removed gates).

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- The output circuit has the same number of qubits and classical bits as the input.
- All gates in the output circuit contain only assigned parameters or are non-parameterized.
- No gate with any unassigned parameter remains in the output circuit.
- The relative order of the remaining gates is preserved.
- The function handles empty circuits and circuits without parameterized gates gracefully.
- The function does not modify the input circuit in place.

---

This plan provides a clear pathway for a Qiskit developer to implement the `remove_unassigned_parameterized_gates` function correctly and efficiently.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit import Parameter, QuantumCircuit

def remove_unassigned_parameterized_gates(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Remove all gates with unassigned (unbound) parameters from the given circuit.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): Input quantum circuit possibly containing parameterized gates.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: New circuit with all gates containing unassigned parameters removed.
    """
    # Create a new circuit with the same number of qubits and classical bits
    new_circuit = QuantumCircuit(circuit.num_qubits, circuit.num_clbits, name=circuit.name)

    for instr, qargs, cargs in circuit.data:
        # Check if the instruction has parameters
        if instr.params:
            # Check if any parameter is unassigned (still a Parameter object)
            # Parameters can be nested in expressions, so we check if any parameter is an instance of Parameter
            # in the instruction's params list.
            # instr.params can contain floats or ParameterExpressions, so we check recursively.
            # Qiskit provides a method to check if a parameter is bound: ParameterExpression.is_parameterized()
            # But instr.params are numeric values or ParameterExpressions, so we check if any param is parameterized.
            # If any param is parameterized, skip this gate.
            if any(param.is_parameterized() for param in instr.params):
                continue  # Skip this gate as it has unassigned parameters

        # Append the instruction to the new circuit preserving qubits and clbits
        new_circuit.append(instr, qargs, cargs)

    return new_circuit
```

      - The basis should be set to ['h', 't', 'tdg'].

   - The output circuit contains only single-qubit gates from the set {'h', 't', 'tdg'}.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler.passes import SolovayKitaev
from qiskit.transpiler import PassManager
from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit

def sol_kit_decomp(circuit: QuantumCircuit) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Decompose all single-qubit gates in the input circuit into gates from the dense subset ['h', 't', 'tdg']
    using the Solovay-Kitaev algorithm.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): Input quantum circuit.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: Transformed circuit with single-qubit gates decomposed into ['h', 't', 'tdg'].
    """
    # Instantiate the Solovay-Kitaev pass with the specified dense subset basis
    solovay_kitaev_pass = SolovayKitaev(basis_gates=['h', 't', 'tdg'])

    # Create a PassManager and add the Solovay-Kitaev pass
    pass_manager = PassManager(solovay_kitaev_pass)

    # Run the pass manager on the input circuit to get the transformed circuit
    transformed_circuit = pass_manager.run(circuit)

    return transformed_circuit
```

def get_graph_state() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Return the circuit for the graph state of the coupling map of the Fake Kyoto backend. Hint: Use the networkx library to convert the coupling map to a dense adjacency matrix.
    """

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function get_graph_state():

2. Convert the coupling map to an undirected graph:
   - Use networkx to create a directed graph from the coupling map edges.
   - Convert this directed graph to an undirected graph to represent symmetric qubit connections (important for graph states).

3. Generate the adjacency matrix:
   - From the undirected graph, generate a dense adjacency matrix (numpy array).
   - Ensure the adjacency matrix is symmetric and binary (entries 0 or 1) representing edges.

4. Create the GraphState circuit:
   - Use qiskit.circuit.library.GraphState and pass the adjacency matrix to initialize the graph state circuit.
   - The number of qubits in the circuit will be equal to the size of the adjacency matrix.

5. Return the resulting QuantumCircuit.

Acceptance criteria:
- The returned circuit corresponds exactly to the graph state defined by the Fake Kyoto backend coupling map.
- The circuit correctly initializes qubits in |+> states and applies controlled-Z gates according to the adjacency matrix.
- The circuit contains the correct number of qubits matching the backend.
- No measurements are included in the graph state preparation circuit.

Summary:
- Required gates: Hadamard gates on each qubit, controlled-Z gates for edges.
- Circuit structure: One qubit per node, controlled-Z between connected qubits.
- Measurements: None (preparation circuit only).
- Expected outcome: A QuantumCircuit preparing the graph state for the Fake Kyoto coupling map.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeKyoto
from qiskit.circuit.library import GraphState
import networkx as nx
from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit

def get_graph_state() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Return the circuit for the graph state of the coupling map of the Fake Kyoto backend.
    
    The graph state is prepared by initializing each qubit in the |+> state and applying controlled-Z gates
    between qubits connected in the backend's coupling map.
    """
    # Instantiate the Fake Kyoto backend
    backend = FakeKyoto()
    
    # Extract the coupling map edges (directed)
    coupling_map = backend.coupling_map.get_edges()
    
    # Create a directed graph from the coupling map edges
    directed_graph = nx.DiGraph()
    directed_graph.add_edges_from(coupling_map)
    
    # Convert to undirected graph to represent symmetric connections
    undirected_graph = directed_graph.to_undirected()
    
    # Generate the dense adjacency matrix (numpy array)
    adjacency_matrix = nx.to_numpy_array(undirected_graph, dtype=int)
    
    # Create the graph state circuit from the adjacency matrix
    graph_state_circuit = GraphState(adjacency_matrix)
    
    return graph_state_circuit
```

def backend_with_least_instructions() -> str:
    """ Transpile the circuit for the phi plus bell state for FakeKyoto, FakeKyiv and FakeAuckland using the level 1 preset pass manager and return the backend name with the lowest number of instructions.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function `backend_with_least_instructions`:

1. **Initialize the Phi Plus Bell State Circuit:**
   - Create a quantum circuit with 2 qubits.
   - Prepare the Bell state |Φ+⟩ = (|00⟩ + |11⟩)/√2 by:
     - Applying a Hadamard (H) gate to qubit 0.
     - Applying a CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1.

2. **Define the Backends to Compare:**
   - Use the fake backend classes: `FakeKyoto`, `FakeKyiv`, and `FakeAuckland`.
   - Instantiate each backend.

3. **Transpilation Setup:**
   - For each backend:
     - Generate a preset pass manager at optimization level 1 using `generate_preset_pass_manager`.
     - Transpile the Bell state circuit using this pass manager and the respective backend.

4. **Instruction Counting:**
   - For each transpiled circuit, determine the total number of instructions.
     - Instructions correspond to the number of circuit operations, i.e., count the length of `circuit.data`.

5. **Identify the Backend with Least Instructions:**
   - Compare the instruction counts for all three transpiled circuits.
   - Determine which backend yields the minimal number of instructions.

6. **Return Value:**
   - Return the name of the backend class (e.g., `"FakeKyoto"`) with the lowest instruction count as a string.

---

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The function returns a string corresponding to one of the backend names: `"FakeKyoto"`, `"FakeKyiv"`, or `"FakeAuckland"`.
- The returned backend is the one whose transpiled Bell state circuit has the fewest instructions after applying the level 1 preset pass manager.
- The function is deterministic and reproducible given the same environment and Qiskit versions.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, transpile
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeKyoto, FakeKyiv, FakeAuckland
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager

def backend_with_least_instructions() -> str:
    """ Transpile the circuit for the phi plus bell state for FakeKyoto, FakeKyiv and FakeAuckland
        using the level 1 preset pass manager and return the backend name with the lowest number of instructions.
    """
    # Step 1: Create the Phi Plus Bell state circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    qc.h(0)       # Hadamard on qubit 0
    qc.cx(0, 1)   # CNOT with control 0 and target 1

    # Step 2: Instantiate the fake backends
    backends = {
        "FakeKyoto": FakeKyoto(),
        "FakeKyiv": FakeKyiv(),
        "FakeAuckland": FakeAuckland()
    }

    # Step 3 & 4: Transpile and count instructions
    instruction_counts = {}
    for name, backend in backends.items():
        # Generate preset pass manager at optimization level 1
        pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(1, backend=backend)
        # Transpile the circuit with the pass manager and backend
        transpiled_circuit = transpile(qc, backend=backend, pass_manager=pass_manager)
        # Count the number of instructions (operations)
        instruction_counts[name] = len(transpiled_circuit.data)

    # Step 5: Find the backend with the least instructions
    backend_with_min_instructions = min(instruction_counts, key=instruction_counts.get)

    # Step 6: Return the backend name
    return backend_with_min_instructions
```

I will now test this code to ensure it runs correctly.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
import inspect
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import fake_backend
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider.fake_backend_v2 import FakeBackendV2

def fake_providers_v2_with_ecr() -> list:
    """
    Return the list of names of all the fake providers of type FakeBackendV2
    which contain 'ecr' gates in their available operations.
    """
    matching_backends = []

    # Inspect all members of the fake_backend module
    for name, obj in inspect.getmembers(fake_backend):
        # Check if the member is a class and subclass of FakeBackendV2 (excluding FakeBackendV2 itself)
        if inspect.isclass(obj) and issubclass(obj, FakeBackendV2) and obj is not FakeBackendV2:
            try:
                # Instantiate the backend (assuming no-arg constructor)
                backend_instance = obj()
                # Check if 'ecr' is in the backend's operation names
                if 'ecr' in backend_instance.operation_names:
                    matching_backends.append(name)
            except Exception:
                # If instantiation fails, skip this backend
                continue

    return matching_backends
```

def backend_with_lowest_complexity():
    """ Transpile the 4-qubit QFT circuit using preset passmanager with optimization level 3 and seed transpiler = 1234 in FakeOsaka, FakeSherbrooke and FakeBrisbane. Compute the cost of the instructions by penalizing the two qubit gate with a cost of 5, rz gates with a cost 1 and other gates with a cost 2 and return the value of the highest cost among these backends.
    """

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function `backend_with_lowest_complexity`:

1. **Circuit Initialization**
   - Create a 4-qubit Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit using Qiskit's `QFT` library circuit.
   - This circuit will be the input to the transpiler.

2. **Backends Selection**
   - Use the three fake backends from `qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider`:
     - `FakeOsaka`
     - `FakeSherbrooke`
     - `FakeBrisbane`
   - Each provides different device topology and noise characteristics, affecting transpilation.

3. **Transpilation Setup**
   - For each backend, generate a preset pass manager with:
     - Optimization level 3
     - Seed for transpiler set to 1234 (to ensure deterministic transpilation)
   - Transpile the 4-qubit QFT circuit for each backend using the corresponding preset pass manager.

4. **Cost Computation**
   - After transpilation, extract the instructions (gates) from the transpiled circuit.
   - Define gate costs as follows:
     - Two-qubit gates (e.g., CX, CZ, etc.): cost = 5
     - Rz gates: cost = 1
     - All other single-qubit gates: cost = 2
   - For each transpiled circuit, sum the costs of all gates according to the above penalty scheme.

5. **Result Calculation**
   - Among the three total costs (one per backend), identify the highest cost value.
   - Return this highest cost value.

6. **Acceptance Criteria**
   - The function returns an integer or float representing the highest cumulative cost among the three transpiled circuits.
   - The cost calculation correctly accounts for gate types as specified.
   - Transpilation uses optimization level 3 and seed 1234 preset pass managers.
   - The QFT circuit is of size 4 qubits.
   - The function runs without errors using the specified fake backends.

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeOsaka, FakeSherbrooke, FakeBrisbane
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
from qiskit.circuit.library import QFT
from qiskit.transpiler import transpile

def backend_with_lowest_complexity():
    """ 
    Transpile the 4-qubit QFT circuit using preset passmanager with optimization level 3 and seed transpiler=1234 
    in FakeOsaka, FakeSherbrooke and FakeBrisbane. Compute the cost of the instructions by penalizing the two qubit 
    gate with a cost of 5, rz gates with a cost 1 and other gates with a cost 2 and return the value of the highest cost 
    among these backends.
    """
    # Create 4-qubit QFT circuit
    qft_circuit = QFT(4, do_swaps=False).decompose()  # decompose to get basic gates
    
    # List of fake backends
    backends = [FakeOsaka(), FakeSherbrooke(), FakeBrisbane()]
    
    costs = []
    
    for backend in backends:
        # Generate preset pass manager with optimization level 3 and seed 1234
        pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(optimization_level=3, backend=backend, seed_transpiler=1234)
        
        # Transpile the circuit for the backend using the pass manager
        transpiled_circuit = transpile(qft_circuit, backend=backend, pass_manager=pass_manager)
        
        # Initialize cost for this transpiled circuit
        total_cost = 0
        
        # Calculate cost based on gate types
        for instr, qargs, cargs in transpiled_circuit.data:
            name = instr.name
            num_qubits = instr.num_qubits
            
            if num_qubits == 2:
                # Two-qubit gate cost
                total_cost += 5
            elif name == 'rz':
                # Rz gate cost
                total_cost += 1
            else:
                # Other single-qubit gates cost
                total_cost += 2
        
        costs.append(total_cost)
    
    # Return the highest cost among the three backends
    return max(costs)
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import CNOTDihedral

def initialize_cnot_dihedral() -> CNOTDihedral:
    """
    Initialize a CNOTDihedral element from a 2-qubit QuantumCircuit
    with a CNOT gate (control qubit 0, target qubit 1) and a T gate on qubit 0.

    Returns:
        CNOTDihedral: The CNOTDihedral element corresponding to the circuit.
    """
    # Create a 2-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(2)
    # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc.cx(0, 1)
    # Apply T gate on qubit 0
    qc.t(0)
    # Initialize and return the CNOTDihedral element from the circuit
    return CNOTDihedral(qc)
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import CNOTDihedral

def compose_cnot_dihedral() -> CNOTDihedral:
    """ Create two Quantum Circuits of 2 qubits. First quantum circuit should have a cx gate on qubits 0 and 1 and a T gate on qubit 0. The second one is the same but with an additional X gate on qubit 1. Convert the two quantum circuits into CNOTDihedral elements and return the composed circuit.
    """
    # Create first quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc1 = QuantumCircuit(2)
    qc1.cx(0, 1)  # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc1.t(0)      # Apply T gate on qubit 0

    # Create second quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc2 = QuantumCircuit(2)
    qc2.cx(0, 1)  # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc2.t(0)      # Apply T gate on qubit 0
    qc2.x(1)      # Apply X gate on qubit 1

    # Convert quantum circuits to CNOTDihedral elements
    cnot_d1 = CNOTDihedral.from_circuit(qc1)
    cnot_d2 = CNOTDihedral.from_circuit(qc2)

    # Compose the two CNOTDihedral elements (sequential application)
    composed = cnot_d1.compose(cnot_d2)

    return composed

    qc1.cx(0, 1)  # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc1.t(0)      # Apply T gate on qubit 0

    # Create second quantum circuit with 2 qubits
    qc2 = QuantumCircuit(2)
    qc2.cx(0, 1)  # Apply CNOT gate with control qubit 0 and target qubit 1
    qc2.t(0)      # Apply T gate on qubit 0

    cnot_d2 = CNOTDihedral.from_circuit(qc2)

from qiskit.quantum_info import ScalarOp

def compose_scalar_ops() -> ScalarOp:
    """ Create two ScalarOp objects with dimension 2 and coefficient 2,
        compose them together, and return the resulting ScalarOp.
    """
    # Create the first ScalarOp with dimension 2 and coefficient 2
    scalar_op1 = ScalarOp(2, 2)
    # Create the second ScalarOp with dimension 2 and coefficient 2
    scalar_op2 = ScalarOp(2, 2)
    # Compose the two ScalarOps by multiplying their coefficients
    composed_scalar_op = scalar_op1 * scalar_op2
    return composed_scalar_op

# Test the function

print(f"Dimension: {result.dim}, Coefficient: {result.coeff}")

from qiskit.quantum_info import Choi
import numpy as np
def initialize_adjoint_and_compose(data1: np.ndarray, data2: np.ndarray) -> (Choi, Choi, Choi):
    """ Initialize Choi matrices for the given data1 and data2 as inputs. Compute data1 adjoint, and then return the data1 Choi matrix, its adjoint and the composed choi matrices in order.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `initialize_adjoint_and_compose` that operates on Choi matrices from input numpy arrays:

1. **Inputs:**
   - `data1`: a numpy ndarray representing a quantum channel or operation in a suitable form (e.g., matrix form compatible with Choi initialization).
   - `data2`: another numpy ndarray representing a second quantum channel.

2. **Initialize Choi Matrices:**
   - Use `Choi(data1)` to create the Choi matrix representation of the first quantum operation.
   - Similarly, use `Choi(data2)` to create the Choi matrix of the second operation.
   - This step requires that `data1` and `data2` are in a format acceptable to `Choi` constructor (e.g., a superoperator, Kraus operators, or a state vector representing the channel).

3. **Compute the Adjoint of the First Choi Matrix:**
   - The adjoint (dagger) of a Choi matrix corresponds to the adjoint channel.
   - Use the `.adjoint()` method of the `Choi` object representing `data1` to get its adjoint channel's Choi matrix.

4. **Compose the Two Channels:**
   - Compose the adjoint of `data1`'s channel with the channel represented by `data2`.
   - Perform composition using `.compose()` method of `Choi` class: `composed_choi = adjoint_data1.compose(choi_data2)`.
   - The composition represents the sequential application of channels: first `adjoint_data1` then `data2`.

5. **Return:**
   - Return a tuple containing:
     - The original Choi matrix from `data1`
     - The adjoint Choi matrix of `data1`
     - The composed Choi matrix of `(adjoint_data1) ∘ (data2)`

---

**Summary of Steps:**

1. Initialize `choi_data1 = Choi(data1)`
2. Initialize `choi_data2 = Choi(data2)`
3. Compute `adjoint_data1 = choi_data1.adjoint()`
4. Compute `composed_choi = adjoint_data1.compose(choi_data2)`
5. Return `(choi_data1, adjoint_data1, composed_choi)`

---

**Acceptance Criteria:**

- The function accepts two numpy arrays `data1` and `data2` representing quantum operations.
- Returns three `Choi` objects as specified.
- The adjoint is correctly computed via the `.adjoint()` method.
- The composition is correctly performed via `.compose()`.
- No exceptions or errors in the conversion and operations.
- The returned Choi matrices correspond to valid quantum channels (completely positive and trace preserving if input data is valid).

---

**Notes:**

- The user must ensure `data1` and `data2` numpy arrays encode valid quantum operations compatible with `Choi`.
- Handling input validation or exceptions is recommended but not specified in the request.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info import Choi
import numpy as np

def initialize_adjoint_and_compose(data1: np.ndarray, data2: np.ndarray) -> (Choi, Choi, Choi):
    """
    Initialize Choi matrices for the given data1 and data2 as inputs.
    Compute the adjoint of the Choi matrix from data1, and then compose it with the Choi matrix from data2.

    Args:
        data1 (np.ndarray): Numpy array representing a quantum channel (e.g., superoperator matrix).
        data2 (np.ndarray): Numpy array representing a second quantum channel.

    Returns:
        tuple: (choi_data1, adjoint_data1, composed_choi)
            - choi_data1: Choi matrix of data1
            - adjoint_data1: Adjoint Choi matrix of data1
            - composed_choi: Composition of adjoint_data1 with choi_data2
    """
    # Initialize Choi matrices from input data
    choi_data1 = Choi(data1)
    choi_data2 = Choi(data2)

    # Compute the adjoint (dagger) of the first Choi matrix
    adjoint_data1 = choi_data1.adjoint()

    # Compose the adjoint of data1 with data2
    composed_choi = adjoint_data1.compose(choi_data2)

    return choi_data1, adjoint_data1, composed_choi
```

=== [110/151] qiskitHumanEval/109 :: circuit ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit, Parameter
def circuit()-> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Create a parameterized quantum circuit using minimum resources whose statevector output cover the equatorial plane of the surface of the bloch sphere.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to create a parameterized quantum circuit that produces states covering the equatorial plane of the Bloch sphere using minimum quantum resources:

1. Qubit requirements:
   - Use a single qubit to minimize quantum resources.

2. Circuit structure and parameterization:
   - Introduce one parameter, θ, to control the rotation.
   - Initialize the qubit in the |0⟩ state (default).
   - Apply a Hadamard gate (H) to bring the qubit to the |+⟩ = (|0⟩ + |1⟩)/√2 state, which lies on the equator at phase zero.
   - Apply a parameterized rotation around the Z-axis, Rz(θ), to rotate the state along the equatorial plane of the Bloch sphere.
     - This rotation changes the relative phase between |0⟩ and |1⟩, covering the full equator as θ varies from 0 to 2π.

3. Measurement strategy:
   - No measurements are required in the circuit definition since the goal is to produce a parameterized statevector.
   - Measurements can be added later depending on the analysis or experiment.

4. Expected outcomes:
   - For θ in [0, 2π), the circuit outputs states of the form:
     |ψ(θ)⟩ = (|0⟩ + e^{iθ}|1⟩)/√2
   - These states lie on the equator of the Bloch sphere, covering all possible phases.

5. Acceptance criteria:
   - The circuit uses exactly one qubit.
   - Exactly one parameter is defined.

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Parameter

def circuit() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a parameterized 1-qubit quantum circuit whose output statevector covers
    the equatorial plane of the Bloch sphere.

    The circuit applies:
    - Hadamard gate to prepare |+> = (|0> + |1>)/sqrt(2)
    - Parameterized Rz rotation to add a phase θ, producing states of the form:
      |ψ(θ)> = (|0> + e^{iθ}|1>)/sqrt(2)

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The parameterized quantum circuit with one qubit and one parameter.

    theta = Parameter('θ')

    # Initialize a 1-qubit quantum circuit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(1)

    # Apply Hadamard gate to create |+> state on the equator at phase 0
    qc.h(0)

    # Apply parameterized Rz rotation to cover the equatorial plane
    qc.rz(theta, 0)

def equivalent_clifford_circuit(circuit: QuantumCircuit,n: int)->list:

    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement the function `equivalent_clifford_circuit` that generates a list of n random Clifford circuits equivalent to a given Clifford circuit (up to a tolerance), here is a detailed plan:

---

### Objective:
Given an input Clifford circuit, generate `n` random Clifford circuits that are functionally equivalent to the input circuit within specified tolerances (relative and absolute tolerance of 0.4).

---

### Implementation Plan:

1. **Input and Output:**
   - **Input:** 
     - `circuit`: A `QuantumCircuit` instance representing a Clifford circuit.
     - `n`: Number of equivalent random Clifford circuits to generate.
   - **Output:** 
     - A list of `n` `QuantumCircuit` instances, each representing a Clifford circuit functionally equivalent to the input.

2. **Pre-requisites:**
   - Confirm the input circuit is a Clifford circuit. If needed, validate or convert.
   - Use Qiskit's `random_clifford` to generate random Clifford operators.
   - Use Qiskit's `Operator` class to get the matrix representation of circuits.
   - Use tolerance checks to compare equivalence.

3. **Step-by-step Approach:**

   **Step 1: Obtain the operator of the input circuit**
   - Convert the input circuit to an operator `U`.
   - This operator will be the reference for equivalence checks.

   **Step 2: Generate random Clifford operators**
   - For each of the `n` outputs:
     - Generate a random Clifford operator `C` on the same number of qubits as the input circuit.
     - Obtain the operator matrix `C_op` for `C`.

   **Step 3: Construct equivalent Clifford circuits**
   - To create a Clifford circuit equivalent to the input circuit, consider the following:
     - Since Clifford group is closed under multiplication, an equivalent Clifford circuit can be:
       ```
       C * U * C_dagger
       ```
       where `C` is a random Clifford, and `C_dagger` is its inverse.
     - Compute the operator `C * U * C_dagger`.
     - Convert this operator back to a Clifford circuit.
   
   **Step 4: Convert operator to circuit**
   - Use Qiskit Clifford or Operator to convert the resulting operator to a circuit.
   
   **Step 5: Verify equivalence with tolerances**
   - For each generated circuit, compute its operator `V`.
   - Check if `V` is equivalent to `U` up to the specified relative and absolute tolerance (0.4).
     - Use a norm or fidelity metric for this comparison.
   - Only include circuits passing this tolerance check.

   **Step 6: Collect and return all valid circuits**
   - If a generated circuit does not meet the tolerance, generate another until `n` valid circuits are collected.
   - Return the list of such circuits.

4. **Quantum gates and operations involved:**
   - Clifford group operations (Hadamard, Phase, CNOT, etc. implicitly via Clifford objects)
   - Matrix multiplication of operators
   - Conversion between Clifford operators and QuantumCircuits

5. **Circuit structure and qubit requirements:**
   - Number of qubits: same as the input circuit
   - Circuits are Clifford circuits (composed of Clifford gates)
   - Output circuits should be valid QuantumCircuit instances with the same qubit count

6. **Measurement strategy:**
   - No measurement is required as the task involves unitary operator equivalence.
   - The output circuits are intended for further use or verification, not immediate measurement.

7. **Expected outcomes:**
   - A list of `n` QuantumCircuits, each representing a Clifford circuit equivalent to the input circuit within tolerance.
   - Each circuit should be distinct (due to randomization) but functionally equivalent.

---

### Acceptance Criteria:

- The returned list contains exactly `n` QuantumCircuit objects.
- Each circuit acts on the same number of qubits as the input circuit.
- For each circuit `C_i` in the output list, the operator `U_i` satisfies:
  ```
  || U - U_i || <= 0.4
  ```
  where `||.||` is a suitable operator norm or fidelity difference.
- Each output circuit is a valid Clifford circuit.
- The function runs efficiently for typical Clifford circuits (e.g., up to 5 qubits).

---

This plan can be handed over to a developer to implement the function using Qiskit’s Clifford and Operator utilities without ambiguity.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import random_clifford, Operator, Clifford
import numpy as np

def equivalent_clifford_circuit(circuit: QuantumCircuit, n: int) -> list:
    """
    Given a Clifford circuit, return a list of n random Clifford circuits which are equivalent
    to the given circuit up to a relative and absolute tolerance of 0.4.

    Args:
        circuit (QuantumCircuit): Input Clifford circuit.
        n (int): Number of equivalent random Clifford circuits to generate.

    Returns:
        list: List of n QuantumCircuit instances equivalent to the input circuit.
    """
    # Number of qubits in the input circuit
    num_qubits = circuit.num_qubits

    # Reference operator of the input circuit
    U = Operator(circuit)

    # List to store equivalent Clifford circuits
    equivalent_circuits = []

    # Tolerance for equivalence check
    tol = 0.4

    while len(equivalent_circuits) < n:
        # Generate a random Clifford on the same number of qubits
        C = random_clifford(num_qubits)

        # Compute the operator: C * U * C_dagger
        C_op = Operator(C)
        C_dagger_op = Operator(C.adjoint())
        new_op = C_op @ U @ C_dagger_op

        # Convert the operator back to a Clifford
        try:
            new_clifford = Clifford(new_op)
        except Exception:
            # If conversion fails, skip and try again
            continue

        # Convert Clifford to QuantumCircuit
        new_circuit = new_clifford.to_circuit()

        # Compute operator of the new circuit
        V = Operator(new_circuit)

        # Check equivalence using operator norm difference
        # ||U - V|| <= tol
        diff_norm = np.linalg.norm(U.data - V.data)

        if diff_norm <= tol:
            equivalent_circuits.append(new_circuit)

    return equivalent_circuits
```

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit import Parameter

def circuit():
    """
    Return an ansatz to create a quantum dataset of pure states distributed equally across the Bloch sphere.
    The ansatz uses minimal gates: RY(theta) followed by RZ(phi) on a single qubit.
    
    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A 1-qubit parameterized circuit with parameters theta and phi.
    """
    # Define parameters for polar and azimuthal angles
    theta = Parameter('θ')  # Polar angle, range [0, π]
    phi = Parameter('φ')    # Azimuthal angle, range [0, 2π)
    
    # Create a quantum circuit with 1 qubit
    qc = QuantumCircuit(1)
    
    # Apply RY rotation by theta to set polar angle
    qc.ry(theta, 0)
    
    # Apply RZ rotation by phi to set azimuthal angle
    qc.rz(phi, 0)
    
    return qc
```

from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator

from qiskit.synthesis import LieTrotter
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Pauli, SparsePauliOp
def create_product_formula_circuit(pauli_strings: list, times: list, order: int, reps: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Create a quantum circuit using LieTrotter for a list of Pauli strings and times. Each Pauli string is associated with a corresponding time in the 'times' list. The function should return the resulting QuantumCircuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing create_product_formula_circuit(pauli_strings, times, order, reps):

1. Input and initial checks:
   - Inputs:
     * pauli_strings: a list of Pauli strings, each representing an operator term (e.g. 'XIZY', 'ZZII', etc.)
     * times: a list of floats, each the evolution time corresponding to the Pauli string at the same index
     * order: integer specifying the order of the Lie-Trotter product formula (e.g. 1 for first order)
     * reps: integer specifying how many times the product formula should be repeated (to improve accuracy)
   - Verify that pauli_strings and times have the same length.
   - Determine the number of qubits by the length of the first Pauli string.

2. Representing the input Pauli terms:
   - Convert each Pauli string into a SparsePauliOp object (from qiskit.quantum_info) for compatibility.
   - Multiply each SparsePauliOp by the corresponding time scalar to get scaled operators.

3. Create evolution gates for each scaled operator:
   - For each scaled SparsePauliOp, create a PauliEvolutionGate.
     * Use the specified order for the Lie-Trotter product formula.
     * Use reps to repeat the product formula inside the PauliEvolutionGate or implement repetition manually by repeating the sequence of gates.

4. Combine the evolution gates using LieTrotter:
   - Use qiskit.synthesis.LieTrotter to synthesize the overall evolution operator by combining the scaled Pauli operators.
   - The LieTrotter class can take a list of Operators or SparsePauliOps and produce a QuantumCircuit approximating the combined evolution.
   - Use the order and reps parameters in the LieTrotter synthesis.

5. Construct the final QuantumCircuit:
   - Initialize a QuantumCircuit with the number of qubits determined in step 1.
   - Append the synthesized LieTrotter circuit to this QuantumCircuit.

6. Output:
   - Return the constructed QuantumCircuit implementing the product formula evolution for the given Pauli terms and times.

Acceptance criteria:
- The returned QuantumCircuit acts on the correct number of qubits.
- The circuit correctly approximates the combined evolution U = product over j of exp(-i * times[j] * pauli_strings[j]) using Lie-Trotter formula of specified order and reps.
- The circuit is compatible with Qiskit transpiler and simulators.
- The function handles invalid input gracefully (e.g., mismatched lengths of pauli_strings and times) by raising errors.

Summary:

1. Verify input lengths and determine number of qubits.
2. Convert Pauli strings to SparsePauliOp and scale by respective times.
3. Use LieTrotter synthesis with specified order and reps to generate approximated evolution circuit.
4. Initialize QuantumCircuit with appropriate qubits.
5. Append synthesized circuit representing the product formula evolution.
6. Return the final QuantumCircuit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import SparsePauliOp
from qiskit.synthesis import LieTrotter

def create_product_formula_circuit(pauli_strings: list, times: list, order: int, reps: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a quantum circuit using LieTrotter product formula for a list of Pauli strings and times.
    Each Pauli string is associated with a corresponding time in the 'times' list.
    The function returns the resulting QuantumCircuit approximating the combined evolution:
        U = product_j exp(-i * times[j] * P_j)
    where P_j is the Pauli operator from pauli_strings[j].

    Args:
        pauli_strings (list[str]): List of Pauli strings (e.g. 'XIZY', 'ZZII', etc.)
        times (list[float]): List of evolution times corresponding to each Pauli string.
        order (int): Order of the Lie-Trotter product formula (e.g. 1 for first order).
        reps (int): Number of repetitions of the product formula to improve accuracy.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: Quantum circuit implementing the product formula evolution.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If lengths of pauli_strings and times do not match.
    """
    # Input validation
    if len(pauli_strings) != len(times):
        raise ValueError("pauli_strings and times must have the same length.")

    if len(pauli_strings) == 0:
        raise ValueError("pauli_strings list cannot be empty.")

    # Determine number of qubits from length of first Pauli string
    num_qubits = len(pauli_strings[0])

    # Convert each Pauli string to SparsePauliOp and scale by corresponding time
    scaled_ops = []
    for p_str, t in zip(pauli_strings, times):
        if len(p_str) != num_qubits:
            raise ValueError("All Pauli strings must have the same length.")
        op = SparsePauliOp.from_list([(p_str, 1.0)])
        # Scale operator by time (scalar multiplication)
        scaled_op = op * t
        scaled_ops.append(scaled_op)

    # Combine scaled operators into a single SparsePauliOp list for LieTrotter
    # LieTrotter expects a list of Operators or SparsePauliOps
    # Use LieTrotter to synthesize the product formula circuit
    lie_trotter = LieTrotter(scaled_ops, reps=reps, order=order)
    product_formula_circuit = lie_trotter.synthesize()

    # Initialize final QuantumCircuit with correct number of qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(num_qubits)
    # Append the synthesized LieTrotter circuit
    qc.compose(product_formula_circuit, inplace=True)

    return qc
```

def calculate_depth_after_barrier_removal(qc: QuantumCircuit) -> PropertySet:
    """ Remove barriers from the given quantum circuit and calculate the depth before and after removal.
    Return a PropertySet with 'depth_before', 'depth_after', and 'width' properties.
    The function should only remove barriers and not perform any other optimizations.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function `calculate_depth_after_barrier_removal(qc: QuantumCircuit) -> PropertySet`:

1. **Input and Initialization:**
   - Input: A `QuantumCircuit` object `qc`.
   - Initialize a `PropertySet` object to store the results.

2. **Calculate Depth Before Barrier Removal:**
   - Use the quantum circuit's `depth()` method directly to get the depth before removing barriers.
   - Store the value as `'depth_before'` in the `PropertySet`.

3. **Remove Barriers Only:**
   - Create a `PassManager` with a single transpiler pass: `RemoveBarriers()`.
   - Apply this pass manager to the input circuit `qc`, producing a new circuit with barriers removed.
   - Ensure no other transpiler passes or optimizations are performed.

4. **Calculate Depth After Barrier Removal:**
   - Compute the depth of the new circuit (without barriers) using the `depth()` method.
   - Store the value as `'depth_after'` in the `PropertySet`.

5. **Calculate Width:**
   - Determine the circuit width (number of qubits) using the `width()` method of the original circuit.
   - Store the value as `'width'` in the `PropertySet`.

6. **Return the PropertySet:**
   - Return the `PropertySet` containing `'depth_before'`, `'depth_after'`, and `'width'`.

---

**Required Qiskit Components:**
- `QuantumCircuit.depth()`
- `QuantumCircuit.width()`
- `PassManager`
- `RemoveBarriers` transpiler pass
- `PropertySet` for storing results

---

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The function returns a `PropertySet` with exactly three keys: `'depth_before'`, `'depth_after'`, and `'width'`.
- `'depth_before'` is the depth of the input circuit before barrier removal.
- `'depth_after'` is the depth of the circuit after only barrier removal (no other changes).
- `'width'` equals the number of qubits in the input circuit.
- No other circuit transformations or optimizations are performed besides barrier removal.
- The function works correctly for circuits with and without barriers.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.transpiler import PassManager, PropertySet
from qiskit.transpiler.passes import RemoveBarriers

def calculate_depth_after_barrier_removal(qc: QuantumCircuit) -> PropertySet:
    """
    Remove barriers from the given quantum circuit and calculate the depth before and after removal.
    Return a PropertySet with 'depth_before', 'depth_after', and 'width' properties.
    The function only removes barriers and does not perform any other optimizations.
    """
    # Initialize PropertySet to store results
    props = PropertySet()

    # Calculate depth before barrier removal
    depth_before = qc.depth()
    props['depth_before'] = depth_before

    # Create PassManager with only RemoveBarriers pass
    pass_manager = PassManager(RemoveBarriers())

    # Run the pass manager on the input circuit to remove barriers
    qc_no_barriers = pass_manager.run(qc)

    # Calculate depth after barrier removal
    depth_after = qc_no_barriers.depth()
    props['depth_after'] = depth_after

    # Calculate width (number of qubits) of the original circuit
    width = qc.width()
    props['width'] = width

    return props
```

```python
from qiskit.transpiler import CouplingMap

def create_and_modify_coupling_map() -> CouplingMap:
    """
    Create a CouplingMap with a specific coupling list, then modify it by adding an edge and a physical qubit.
    The initial coupling list is [[0, 1], [1, 2], [2, 3], [3, 4], [4, 5]].
    Add an edge (5, 6), and add a physical qubit "7".
    """
    # Initial coupling list representing directed edges between qubits
    initial_coupling = [[0, 1], [1, 2], [2, 3], [3, 4], [4, 5]]
    
    # Create CouplingMap with initial edges
    coupling_map = CouplingMap(initial_coupling)
    
    # Add edge (5, 6)
    coupling_map.add_edge(5, 6)
    
    # Add physical qubit 7 by increasing the number of physical qubits
    # The CouplingMap class does not have a direct method to add isolated qubits,
    # but we can set the number of physical qubits to 8 to include qubit 7.
    coupling_map._physical_qubits = 8  # Set number of physical qubits to 8 (0 through 7)
    
    return coupling_map
```

I will now test this code to ensure it works correctly.

def create_and_modify_coupling_map() -> CouplingMap:
    """

    The initial coupling list is [[0, 1], [1, 2], [2, 3], [3, 4], [4, 5]].
    Add an edge (5, 6), and add a physical qubit "7".
    """
    # Initial coupling list representing directed edges between qubits
    initial_coupling = [[0, 1], [1, 2], [2, 3], [3, 4], [4, 5]]

    coupling_map = CouplingMap(initial_coupling)
    
    # Add edge (5, 6)

    # Add physical qubit 7 by increasing the number of physical qubits
    # The CouplingMap class does not have a direct method to add isolated qubits,

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler import Target, InstructionProperties
from qiskit.circuit.library import UGate, CXGate
from qiskit.circuit import Parameter

def create_target() -> Target:
    """ Create a Target object for a 2-qubit system and add UGate and CXGate instructions with specific properties.
    - Add UGate for both qubits (0 and 1) with parameters 'theta', 'phi', and 'lambda'.
    - Add CXGate for qubit pairs (0,1) and (1,0).
    - All instructions have nonzero 'duration' and 'error' properties set.
    """
    # Initialize Target for 2 qubits
    target = Target(num_qubits=2)

    # Define parameters for UGate
    theta = Parameter('θ')
    phi = Parameter('φ')
    lam = Parameter('λ')

    # Duration and error for single-qubit UGate
    u_duration = 50  # arbitrary positive duration units
    u_error = 0.001  # small error rate

    # Add UGate for qubit 0 and qubit 1 with properties
    for qubit in [0, 1]:
        # InstructionProperties takes duration and error as arguments
        props = InstructionProperties(duration=u_duration, error=u_error)
        # Add UGate with parameters to the target for the specific qubit
        target.add_instruction(UGate(theta, phi, lam), [qubit], props)

    # Duration and error for two-qubit CXGate
    cx_duration = 200  # longer duration than single-qubit gate
    cx_error = 0.01    # higher error rate than single-qubit gate

    # Add CXGate for qubit pairs (0,1) and (1,0) with properties
    for control, target_qubit in [(0, 1), (1, 0)]:
        props = InstructionProperties(duration=cx_duration, error=cx_error)
        target.add_instruction(CXGate(), [control, target_qubit], props)

    return target
```

from qiskit.circuit.library import PauliEvolutionGate
from qiskit.synthesis import MatrixExponential
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Pauli, Operator
import numpy as np
def synthesize_evolution_gate(pauli_string: str, time: float) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Synthesize an evolution gate using MatrixExponential for a given Pauli string and time.
    The Pauli string can be any combination of 'I', 'X', 'Y', and 'Z'.
    Return the resulting QuantumCircuit.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan to implement the function `synthesize_evolution_gate(pauli_string: str, time: float) -> QuantumCircuit`:

1. **Input parsing and validation**
   - Ensure `pauli_string` is a non-empty string consisting only of characters from {'I', 'X', 'Y', 'Z'}.
   - Ensure `time` is a float (or castable to float).

2. **Determine number of qubits**
   - The length of `pauli_string` determines the number of qubits (denote as n).

3. **Create the Pauli operator for the given string**
   - Use Qiskit's `Pauli` class to construct a Pauli operator from `pauli_string`.
   - This object represents the Pauli operator \( P \) corresponding to the given string.

4. **Construct the Hamiltonian matrix**
   - Convert the Pauli operator to its matrix representation (2^n x 2^n) using `Operator` or directly via `Pauli` methods.
   - The Hamiltonian \( H \) is defined as \( H = P \).

5. **Compute the unitary evolution operator**
   - The evolution operator is \( U = e^{-i H t} \).
   - Use `MatrixExponential` from Qiskit's synthesis module with input \(-i H t\) to create the evolution gate.
   - Specifically:
     - Multiply the Hamiltonian matrix by \(-i t\).
     - Pass this matrix to `MatrixExponential` to obtain a `QuantumCircuit` implementing \( U \).

6. **Build and return the QuantumCircuit**
   - The resulting `QuantumCircuit` from `MatrixExponential` acts on n qubits.
   - Return this circuit.

**Circuit structure and qubit requirements:**
- Number of qubits = length of `pauli_string`.
- Circuit implements the unitary evolution \( e^{-i P t} \).

**Measurement strategy:**
- Not required in this function, as it only synthesizes the evolution gate.

**Expected outcomes:**
- The returned `QuantumCircuit` implements the time evolution operator for the given Pauli string and time.
- When applied to the appropriate quantum state, it corresponds to evolution under the Hamiltonian \( P \) for time \( t \).

**Acceptance criteria:**
- The function accepts any valid Pauli string and time.
- The returned circuit has the correct number of qubits.
- The circuit implements \( e^{-i P t} \) within numerical precision.
- No errors occur during construction.
- The code uses `MatrixExponential` as specified.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import PauliEvolutionGate
from qiskit.synthesis import MatrixExponential
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.quantum_info import Pauli, Operator
import numpy as np

def synthesize_evolution_gate(pauli_string: str, time: float) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Synthesize an evolution gate using MatrixExponential for a given Pauli string and time.
    The Pauli string can be any combination of 'I', 'X', 'Y', and 'Z'.
    Return the resulting QuantumCircuit implementing U = exp(-i * P * t).
    """
    # Validate pauli_string
    if not pauli_string or any(c not in {'I', 'X', 'Y', 'Z'} for c in pauli_string):
        raise ValueError("pauli_string must be a non-empty string containing only 'I', 'X', 'Y', 'Z' characters.")
    
    # Validate and cast time
    try:
        time = float(time)
    except Exception as e:
        raise ValueError("time must be a float or castable to float.") from e
    
    n = len(pauli_string)  # number of qubits
    
    # Create Pauli operator from string
    pauli_op = Pauli(pauli_string)
    
    # Get matrix representation of the Pauli operator (2^n x 2^n)
    H = Operator(pauli_op).data  # numpy array
    
    # Construct the matrix for the evolution operator exponent: -i * H * t
    matrix_for_exp = -1j * H * time
    
    # Use MatrixExponential to synthesize the evolution gate
    evolution_gate = MatrixExponential(matrix_for_exp)
    
    # The MatrixExponential gate acts on n qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)
    qc.append(evolution_gate, range(n))
    
    return qc
```

from qiskit.synthesis import TwoQubitBasisDecomposer
from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator
from qiskit.circuit.library import CXGate
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def decompose_unitary(unitary: Operator) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Decompose a 4x4 unitary using the TwoQubitBasisDecomposer with CXGate as the basis gate.
    Return the resulting QuantumCircuit.
    """
    # Validate that the operator acts on 2 qubits (dimension 4)
    if unitary.dim != (4, 4):
        raise ValueError("Input operator must be a 4x4 unitary acting on 2 qubits.")
    
    # Initialize the decomposer with CXGate as the basis gate
    decomposer = TwoQubitBasisDecomposer(CXGate())
    
    # Decompose the unitary operator into a QuantumCircuit
    decomposed_circuit = decomposer(unitary)
    
    return decomposed_circuit

from qiskit.synthesis import TwoQubitBasisDecomposer
from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator, random_unitary
from qiskit.circuit.library import CXGate
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, transpile, Aer, execute
import numpy as np

def decompose_unitary(unitary: Operator) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Decompose a 4x4 unitary using the TwoQubitBasisDecomposer with CXGate as the basis gate.
    Return the resulting QuantumCircuit.
    """
    # Validate that the operator acts on 2 qubits (dimension 4)
    if unitary.dim != (4, 4):
        raise ValueError("Input operator must be a 4x4 unitary acting on 2 qubits.")
    
    # Initialize the decomposer with CXGate as the basis gate
    decomposer = TwoQubitBasisDecomposer(CXGate())
    
    # Decompose the unitary operator into a QuantumCircuit
    decomposed_circuit = decomposer(unitary)
    
    return decomposed_circuit

# Test the function with a random 2-qubit unitary
if __name__ == "__main__":
    # Generate a random 4x4 unitary operator
    random_op = Operator(random_unitary(4))
    
    # Decompose the unitary
    circuit = decompose_unitary(random_op)
    
    # Verify the unitary of the decomposed circuit matches the original operator
    backend = Aer.get_backend('unitary_simulator')
    transpiled_circuit = transpile(circuit, backend)
    job = execute(transpiled_circuit, backend)
    result = job.result()
    decomposed_unitary = result.get_unitary(transpiled_circuit)
    
    # Check if the matrices are close within a tolerance
    if np.allclose(random_op.data, decomposed_unitary, atol=1e-8):
        print("Decomposition successful: The decomposed circuit matches the original unitary.")
    else:
        print("Decomposition failed: The decomposed circuit does not match the original unitary.")

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.circuit.library import C3SXGate

def create_c3sx_circuit() -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Create a QuantumCircuit with a C3SXGate applied to the first four qubits.
    """
    # Initialize a QuantumCircuit with 4 qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(4)
    
    # Apply the C3SXGate to qubits 0,1,2 (controls) and 3 (target)
    qc.append(C3SXGate(), [0, 1, 2, 3])
    
    return qc

# Test the function by creating the circuit and drawing it
if __name__ == "__main__":
    circuit = create_c3sx_circuit()
    print(circuit.draw())

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import CDKMRippleCarryAdder
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def create_ripple_carry_adder_circuit(num_state_qubits: int, kind: str) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """
    Create a QuantumCircuit with a CDKMRippleCarryAdder applied to the qubits.
    The kind of adder can be 'full', 'half', or 'fixed'.

    Parameters:
        num_state_qubits (int): Number of qubits representing the binary numbers to add.
        kind (str): Type of CDKMRippleCarryAdder to use. Must be one of 'full', 'half', or 'fixed'.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: A quantum circuit implementing the specified ripple carry adder.
    """
    # Validate kind parameter
    valid_kinds = {'full', 'half', 'fixed'}
    if kind not in valid_kinds:
        raise ValueError(f"Invalid kind '{kind}'. Must be one of {valid_kinds}.")

    # Instantiate the CDKMRippleCarryAdder circuit
    adder = CDKMRippleCarryAdder(num_state_qubits=num_state_qubits, kind=kind)

    # The CDKMRippleCarryAdder circuit has qubits arranged as:
    # - first num_state_qubits qubits: first addend register
    # - next num_state_qubits qubits: second addend register
    # - carry qubits: number depends on kind (adder.num_carry_qubits)

    # Create a QuantumCircuit with the same number of qubits as the adder circuit
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(adder.num_qubits)

    # Append the adder circuit to the main circuit
    circuit.compose(adder, inplace=True)

    return circuit
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import Diagonal
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
import math

def create_diagonal_circuit(diag: list) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """ Create a QuantumCircuit with a Diagonal gate applied to the qubits.
    The diagonal elements are provided in the list 'diag'.

    Args:
        diag (list): List of diagonal elements of the unitary matrix.
                     Length must be a power of two.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: Circuit with the Diagonal gate applied.
    """
    # Validate that length of diag is a power of two
    length = len(diag)
    if length == 0 or (length & (length - 1)) != 0:
        raise ValueError("Length of diag must be a power of two and non-zero.")

    # Calculate number of qubits
    n = int(math.log2(length))

    # Initialize quantum circuit with n qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)

    # Create Diagonal gate with the given diagonal elements
    diag_gate = Diagonal(diag)

    # Append the Diagonal gate to the circuit on all qubits
    qc.append(diag_gate, qc.qubits)

    return qc
```
