from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister
def conditional_two_qubit_circuit():
    """ Create a quantum circuit with one qubit and two classical bits. The qubit's operation depends on its measurement outcome: if it measures to 1 (|1> state), it flips the qubit's state back to |0> using an X gate. The qubit's initial state is randomized using a Hadamard gate. When building the quantum circuit make sure the classical registers is named 'c'.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the described quantum circuit:

1. Initialize Circuit:
   - Create a quantum register with 1 qubit.
   - Create a classical register with 2 bits, named 'c' as specified.
   - Initialize a QuantumCircuit with these registers.

2. Prepare Initial State:
   - Apply a Hadamard (H) gate to the single qubit.
   - This puts the qubit into an equal superposition of |0> and |1>, effectively randomizing its initial state.

3. Measurement:
   - Measure the qubit into the first classical bit (c[0]).
   - This collapses the qubit state to either |0> or |1> and stores the result in c[0].

4. Conditional Operation:
   - Apply an X gate to the qubit conditioned on the measurement result being 1.
   - Use a classical conditional operation that checks if c[0] == 1, then applies X to the qubit.
   - This flips the qubit from |1> back to |0> if the measurement was 1.

5. Final Measurement:
   - Measure the qubit again into the second classical bit (c[1]).
   - This confirms the final state of the qubit after the conditional operation.

Expected Outcome:
- Initially, the qubit is in superposition.
- After the first measurement, the qubit collapses to either |0> or |1>.
- If it was measured as |1>, the conditional X gate flips it back to |0>.
- The second measurement should always report 0, confirming the correction.
- The classical register 'c' contains two bits: the first is the initial measurement, the second is the post-correction measurement.

Acceptance Criteria:
- The circuit has exactly 1 qubit and 2 classical bits.
- The classical register is named 'c'.
- The initial Hadamard gate is applied to the qubit.
- The first measurement stores the qubit's state in c[0].
- The X gate is applied conditionally on c[0] == 1.
- The second measurement stores the qubit's final state in c[1].
- The circuit behavior matches the described conditional logic.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister

def conditional_two_qubit_circuit():
    """
    Create a quantum circuit with one qubit and two classical bits.
    The qubit's operation depends on its measurement outcome:
    if it measures to 1 (|1> state), it flips the qubit's state back to |0> using an X gate.
    The qubit's initial state is randomized using a Hadamard gate.
    The classical register is named 'c'.
    """
    # Create a quantum register with 1 qubit
    qreg = QuantumRegister(1, 'q')
    # Create a classical register with 2 bits named 'c'
    creg = ClassicalRegister(2, 'c')
    # Initialize the quantum circuit with the quantum and classical registers
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(qreg, creg)

    # Apply Hadamard gate to put qubit in superposition
    circuit.h(qreg[0])

    # Measure the qubit into the first classical bit c[0]
    circuit.measure(qreg[0], creg[0])

    # Apply X gate conditionally if measurement result c[0] == 1
    circuit.x(qreg[0]).c_if(creg, 1)  # c_if checks the integer value of classical bits; here c=2 bits, so 1 means c[0]=1 and c[1]=0

    # Measure the qubit again into the second classical bit c[1]
    circuit.measure(qreg[0], creg[1])

    return circuit
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.circuit.library import EfficientSU2
from qiskit_ibm_transpiler.transpiler_service import TranspilerService

def ai_transpiling(num_qubits):
    """
    Generate an EfficientSU2 circuit with the given number of qubits, 1 repetition,
    and circular entanglement. Then transpile the circuit using the Qiskit TranspilerService
    with AI transpiler enabled, targeting the ibm_brisbane backend and optimization level 3.

    Args:
        num_qubits (int): Number of qubits in the circuit.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The transpiled quantum circuit optimized for ibm_brisbane.
    """
    # Create EfficientSU2 circuit with 1 repetition and circular entanglement
    circuit = EfficientSU2(num_qubits, reps=1, entanglement='circular')

    # Initialize the transpiler service for the 'ibm_brisbane' backend
    transpiler_service = TranspilerService()

    # Transpile the circuit with optimization level 3 and AI transpiler enabled
    transpiled_circuit = transpiler_service.transpile(
        circuit,
        backend='ibm_brisbane',
        optimization_level=3,
        ai_transpiler=True
    )

    return transpiled_circuit

def ai_transpiling(num_qubits):
    """
    Generate an EfficientSU2 circuit with the given number of qubits, 1 repetition,
    and circular entanglement. Then transpile the circuit using the Qiskit TranspilerService
    with AI transpiler enabled, targeting the ibm_brisbane backend and optimization level 3.

    Args:
        num_qubits (int): Number of qubits in the circuit.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The transpiled quantum circuit optimized for ibm_brisbane.
    """
    # Create EfficientSU2 circuit with 1 repetition and circular entanglement
    circuit = EfficientSU2(num_qubits, reps=1, entanglement='circular')

    # Initialize the transpiler service for the 'ibm_brisbane' backend
    transpiler_service = TranspilerService()

    # Transpile the circuit with optimization level 3 and AI transpiler enabled
    transpiled_circuit = transpiler_service.transpile(
        circuit,
        backend='ibm_brisbane',
        optimization_level=3,
        ai_transpiler=True
    )

    return transpiled_circuit

from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2
from qiskit_aer.noise import NoiseModel

def gen_noise_model():
    """ Generate a noise model from the Fake Cairo V2 backend. """
    # Create an instance of the FakeCairoV2 backend
    backend = FakeCairoV2()
    # Generate the noise model from the backend's noise characteristics
    noise_model = NoiseModel.from_backend(backend)
    # Return the generated noise model
    return noise_model

# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    noise = gen_noise_model()
    print(noise)  # Print the noise model summary to verify it was created successfully

   - Create a single Gate object that encapsulates the entire unitary transformation performed by the input circuit.
   - This gate should be usable as a subcomponent in larger circuits, acting identically to the original circuit on the corresponding qubits.
   
3. **Implementation steps:**
   1. **Import necessary function/module:**
      - Use `circuit_to_gate` from `qiskit.converters` to perform the conversion.
   
   2. **Conversion:**
      - Call `circuit_to_gate(circ)` to convert the input QuantumCircuit into an equivalent Gate.
      - This function internally compiles the circuit's unitary and wraps it as a Gate object.
   
   3. **Return value:**
      - Return the resulting Gate object from the function.
   
4. **Circuit requirements:**
   - The input circuit should be fully defined and unitary.
   - If the input circuit contains measurements or resets, these may prevent conversion to a gate (as gates must be unitary).
   - The circuit should not have classical control flow or mid-circuit measurements, as these are incompatible with gate conversion.
   
5. **Testing and Validation:**
   - Create a test QuantumCircuit with a known sequence of gates.
   - Convert it to a gate using `circ_to_gate`.
   - Insert this gate into a new circuit on the same number of qubits.
   - Verify that the unitary matrices of the original circuit and the new circuit containing the gate are identical (using `Operator` or `unitary_simulator`).
   
6. **Acceptance criteria:**
   - The function returns a valid Gate object.
   - The gate implements the exact same unitary operation as the input circuit.
   - The gate can be appended to other circuits and performs identically to the original circuit on corresponding qubits.
   - The function gracefully handles or documents limitations (e.g., no measurements or classical control in input circuit).

---

This plan provides a clear, high-level guideline for implementing the requested function using Qiskit's `circuit_to_gate` utility.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit import QuantumCircuit
from qiskit.converters import circuit_to_gate

def circ_to_gate(circ: QuantumCircuit):
    """
    Convert a QuantumCircuit into an equivalent Gate.

    Parameters:
        circ (QuantumCircuit): The input quantum circuit to convert.

    Returns:
        Gate: A gate that performs the same unitary operation as the input circuit.

    Note:
        The input circuit must be unitary and should not contain measurements,
        resets, or classical control flow.
    """
    # Use Qiskit's circuit_to_gate to convert the circuit into a gate
    gate = circuit_to_gate(circ)
    return gate


# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator
    from qiskit import Aer, transpile, execute

    # Create a test circuit
    test_circ = QuantumCircuit(2)
    test_circ.h(0)
    test_circ.cx(0, 1)
    test_circ.t(1)

    # Convert the circuit to a gate
    test_gate = circ_to_gate(test_circ)

    # Create a new circuit and append the gate
    new_circ = QuantumCircuit(2)
    new_circ.append(test_gate, [0, 1])

    # Verify that the unitary matrices are identical
    op_original = Operator(test_circ)
    op_new = Operator(new_circ)

    assert op_original.equiv(op_new), "The gate does not match the original circuit unitary."

    print("Conversion successful: The gate matches the original circuit unitary.")

    from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator

    # Create a test circuit
    test_circ = QuantumCircuit(2)
    test_circ.h(0)
    test_circ.cx(0, 1)
    test_circ.t(1)

    # Convert the circuit to a gate
    test_gate = circ_to_gate(test_circ)

    # Create a new circuit and append the gate
    new_circ = QuantumCircuit(2)
    new_circ.append(test_gate, [0, 1])

    # Verify that the unitary matrices are identical
    op_original = Operator(test_circ)
    op_new = Operator(new_circ)

    assert op_original.equiv(op_new), "The gate does not match the original circuit unitary."

    print("Conversion successful: The gate matches the original circuit unitary.")

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.library import HGate
from qiskit.quantum_info import Operator, process_fidelity
import numpy as np

def calculate_phase_difference_fidelity():
    """
    Create two quantum operators using Hadamard gate that differ only by a global phase.
    Calculate the process fidelity between these two operators and return the process fidelity value.
    """
    # Define the global phase angle phi
    phi = np.pi / 4  # 45 degrees
    
    # Create the Hadamard gate operator
    hadamard_gate = HGate()
    op1 = Operator(hadamard_gate)
    
    # Create the second operator by applying a global phase to the Hadamard operator
    global_phase = np.exp(1j * phi)
    op2 = global_phase * op1
    
    # Calculate the process fidelity between the two operators
    fidelity = process_fidelity(op1, op2)
    
    return fidelity

# Example usage and print the fidelity
if __name__ == "__main__":
    fidelity_value = calculate_phase_difference_fidelity()
    print(f"Process fidelity between Hadamard and globally phased Hadamard: {fidelity_value}")
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.circuit.random import random_circuit
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def random_circuit_depth():
    """Using qiskit's random_circuit function, generate a circuit with 4 qubits and a depth of 3
    that measures all qubits at the end. Use the seed value 17 and return the generated circuit.
    """
    # Generate a random circuit with 4 qubits, depth 3, and seed 17
    qc = random_circuit(num_qubits=4, depth=3, max_operands=2, seed=17)
    
    # Add classical bits for measurement
    qc.add_register(qc.cregs[0] if qc.cregs else QuantumCircuit(4).cregs[0])  # Ensure classical register exists
    if len(qc.clbits) < 4:
        # Add classical register of size 4 if not present
        qc.add_register(QuantumCircuit(4).cregs[0])
    
    # If no classical register, add one
    if qc.num_clbits < 4:
        qc.add_register(QuantumCircuit(4).cregs[0])
    
    # Create a classical register of 4 bits
    from qiskit import ClassicalRegister
    creg = ClassicalRegister(4)
    qc.add_register(creg)
    
    # Measure all qubits into the classical bits
    qc.measure(range(4), range(4))
    
    return qc

=== [129/151] qiskitHumanEval/128 :: conditional_quantum_circuit ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit, QuantumRegister, ClassicalRegister
from qiskit.circuit.classical import expr
def conditional_quantum_circuit():
    """ Create a quantum circuit with 4 qubits and 4 classical bits. Apply Hadamard gates to the first three qubits, measure them with three classical registers,
    and conditionally apply an X gate to the fourth qubit based on the XOR of the three classical bits. Finally, measure the fourth qubit into another classical register.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the described quantum circuit:

1. Initialize the circuit:
   - Create a QuantumCircuit with 4 qubits.
   - Create 4 classical bits for measurement: 3 classical bits for the first three qubits and 1 classical bit for the fourth qubit.

2. Apply Hadamard gates:
   - Apply the Hadamard (H) gate to qubits 0, 1, and 2.

3. Measurement of the first three qubits:
   - Measure qubits 0, 1, and 2 into the first three classical bits respectively.

4. Define the classical condition:
   - Compute the XOR of the three classical bits (from the first three measurements). This can be expressed as a classical expression: c0 XOR c1 XOR c2.

5. Conditional operation:
   - Conditionally apply an X gate to qubit 3 if the XOR of the three classical bits equals 1.

6. Measurement of the fourth qubit:
   - Measure qubit 3 into the fourth classical bit.

Expected outcome:
- The first three qubits are put into superposition and measured.
- Based on the parity (XOR) of these measurement results, the fourth qubit is flipped or not.
- The final measurement of the fourth qubit reflects this conditional operation.

Acceptance criteria:
- The circuit uses 4 qubits and 4 classical bits.
- Hadamard gates are applied to the first three qubits.
- Measurements are performed correctly on the first three qubits into separate classical bits.
- The X gate on qubit 3 is applied only when the XOR of the three classical bits is 1.
- The fourth qubit is measured into the last classical bit.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit, ClassicalRegister, QuantumRegister

def conditional_quantum_circuit():
    """
    Create a quantum circuit with 4 qubits and 4 classical bits.
    Apply Hadamard gates to the first three qubits, measure them into three classical bits,
    and conditionally apply an X gate to the fourth qubit based on the XOR of the three classical bits.
    Finally, measure the fourth qubit into another classical bit.
    """
    # Create quantum and classical registers
    qr = QuantumRegister(4, 'q')
    cr = ClassicalRegister(4, 'c')
    circuit = QuantumCircuit(qr, cr)

    # Apply Hadamard gates to the first three qubits to create superposition
    circuit.h(qr[0])
    circuit.h(qr[1])
    circuit.h(qr[2])

    # Measure the first three qubits into the first three classical bits
    circuit.measure(qr[0], cr[0])
    circuit.measure(qr[1], cr[1])
    circuit.measure(qr[2], cr[2])

    # Define the classical condition: XOR of the three classical bits equals 1
    # This is equivalent to the parity of the bits being 1
    # Qiskit classical condition expressions can be used for this
    from qiskit.circuit.classical import expr
    condition_expr = expr.Expr('c0 ^ c1 ^ c2', {'c0': cr[0], 'c1': cr[1], 'c2': cr[2]})

    # Conditionally apply X gate to the fourth qubit if XOR == 1
    circuit.x(qr[3]).c_if(condition_expr, 1)

    # Measure the fourth qubit into the fourth classical bit
    circuit.measure(qr[3], cr[3])

    return circuit
```

    Return this qubit pair along with the corresponding error rate as a tuple. If the backend doesn't support the RZ gate, return None.
    """

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the function `find_highest_rz_error_rate(backend_name)`:

   - Import QiskitRuntimeService or IBMQ to access backend properties.
   - No quantum circuit needed; this function queries backend calibration data.

2. Steps:
   1. Initialize QiskitRuntimeService or IBMQ and load the backend by name.
   2. Retrieve the backend properties object using `backend.properties()`.
   3. Iterate over the backend properties to find all gate calibration data:
      - Focus on gates named "rz" (single-qubit rotations around Z).
      - Note: Because RZ is a single-qubit gate, error rates are associated with individual qubits, not qubit pairs.
   4. For each "rz" gate entry:
      - Extract the qubit index and the gate error rate.
   5. Identify the qubit with the highest RZ error rate.
   6. Since the function is specified to return a qubit pair, but RZ gates are single-qubit operations:

      - Alternatively, if the user intends to find two-qubit pairs, and RZ gates are not present on pairs, return None.
   7. If no RZ gates are found, return None.

   - The function correctly retrieves backend properties.
   - It identifies and returns the qubit index or pair with the highest RZ gate error rate.
   - Returns None if no RZ gates are available.
   - Handles cases where RZ gates are only single-qubit gates (clarify qubit pair handling).

Summary:

- Initialize QiskitRuntimeService or IBMQ.
- Load backend by backend_name.
- Retrieve backend properties.
- Search for "rz" gates and their error rates per qubit.
- Find the qubit with max error rate.
- Return `(qubit_index, qubit_index), error_rate` tuple.
- Return None if no RZ gate found.

This approach clarifies the ambiguity about qubit pairs for single-qubit RZ gates and provides a consistent return format.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService

def find_highest_rz_error_rate(backend_name):
    """
    Given the name of a quantum backend, retrieve the properties of the specified backend and identify the qubit
    with the highest error rate among its RZ gates. Since RZ is a single-qubit gate, return a tuple with the qubit
    index repeated twice and the corresponding error rate as a tuple: ((qubit, qubit), error_rate).
    If the backend doesn't support the RZ gate, return None.
    """
    # Initialize the IBM Quantum Runtime service (assumes IBM Quantum account is already saved)
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Get the backend by name
    backend = service.get_backend(backend_name)

    # Retrieve backend properties
    properties = backend.properties()

    max_error_rate = None
    max_qubit = None

    # Iterate over gate properties to find RZ gates and their error rates
    for gate in properties.gates:
        if gate.gate == "rz":
            # RZ is a single-qubit gate, so gate.qubits has length 1
            qubit = gate.qubits[0]
            # Find the error parameter in gate.parameters
            for param in gate.parameters:
                if param.name == "gate_error":
                    error_rate = param.value
                    if error_rate is not None:
                        if (max_error_rate is None) or (error_rate > max_error_rate):
                            max_error_rate = error_rate
                            max_qubit = qubit

    if max_error_rate is None:
        # No RZ gate found
        return None

    # Return qubit pair as (qubit, qubit) and the error rate
    return (max_qubit, max_qubit), max_error_rate
```

from qiskit.circuit import QuantumCircuit
def inv_circuit(n):
    """ Create a quantum circuit with 'n' qubits. Apply Hadamard gates to the second and third qubits.

2. Apply Hadamard gates:
   - Apply Hadamard (H) gates to the second and third qubits.
   - Assuming 0-based indexing: qubit 1 and qubit 2.

3. Apply CNOT gates:
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 1 (second qubit) and target qubit 3 (fourth qubit).
   - Apply a CNOT gate with control qubit 2 (third qubit) and target qubit 4 (fifth qubit).

4. Invert the circuit:

5. Output:
   - Return the inverse circuit.

Acceptance criteria:

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def inv_circuit(n):
    """
    Create a quantum circuit with 'n' qubits. Apply Hadamard gates to the second and third qubits.
    Then apply CNOT gates between the second and fourth qubits, and between the third and fifth qubits.
    Finally return the inverse of the quantum circuit.

    Parameters:
        n (int): Number of qubits, must be at least 5.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The inverse of the constructed quantum circuit.
    """
    if n < 5:
        raise ValueError("Number of qubits must be at least 5.")

    # Initialize quantum circuit with n qubits
    qc = QuantumCircuit(n)

    # Apply Hadamard gates to qubits 1 and 2 (second and third qubits)
    qc.h(1)
    qc.h(2)

    # Apply CNOT gates: control qubit 1 -> target qubit 3, control qubit 2 -> target qubit 4
    qc.cx(1, 3)
    qc.cx(2, 4)

    # Return the inverse (adjoint) of the circuit
    return qc.inverse()
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2, FakeBackend

def backend_info(backend_name: str) -> dict:
    """
    Given the fake backend name, retrieve information about the backend's number of qubits,
    coupling map, and supported instructions using the Qiskit Runtime Fake Provider,
    and create a dictionary containing the info.

    Args:
        backend_name (str): The name of the fake backend.

    Returns:
        dict: Dictionary with keys 'num_qubits', 'coupling_map', and 'supported_instructions'.
    """
    # Map backend names to their corresponding fake backend classes
    fake_backends = {
        "FakeCairoV2": FakeCairoV2,
        # Add other fake backends here if needed
    }

    if backend_name not in fake_backends:
        raise ValueError(f"Unsupported backend name: {backend_name}")

    # Instantiate the fake backend
    backend: FakeBackend = fake_backends[backend_name]()

    # Retrieve number of qubits
    num_qubits = backend.configuration().n_qubits

    # Retrieve coupling map (list of directed edges)
    coupling_map = backend.configuration().coupling_map

    # Retrieve supported instructions (basis gates)
    supported_instructions = backend.configuration().basis_gates

    # Construct and return the dictionary
    return {
        "num_qubits": num_qubits,
        "coupling_map": coupling_map,
        "supported_instructions": supported_instructions,
    }
```

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeCairoV2

def backend_info(backend_name: str) -> dict:
    """
    Given the fake backend name, retrieve information about the backend's number of qubits,
    coupling map, and supported instructions using the Qiskit Runtime Fake Provider,
    and create a dictionary containing the info.

    Args:
        backend_name (str): The name of the fake backend.

    Returns:
        dict: Dictionary with keys 'num_qubits', 'coupling_map', and 'supported_instructions'.
    """
    # Map backend names to their corresponding fake backend classes
    fake_backends = {
        "FakeCairoV2": FakeCairoV2,
        # Add other fake backends here if needed
    }

    if backend_name not in fake_backends:
        raise ValueError(f"Unsupported backend name: {backend_name}")

    # Instantiate the fake backend
    backend = fake_backends[backend_name]()

    # Retrieve number of qubits
    num_qubits = backend.configuration().n_qubits

    # Retrieve coupling map (list of directed edges)
    coupling_map = backend.configuration().coupling_map

    # Retrieve supported instructions (basis gates)
    supported_instructions = backend.configuration().basis_gates

    # Construct and return the dictionary
    return {
        "num_qubits": num_qubits,
        "coupling_map": coupling_map,
        "supported_instructions": supported_instructions,
    }
```

def run_batched_random_circuits():

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.circuit.random import random_circuit
from qiskit.transpiler.preset_passmanagers import generate_preset_pass_manager
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import Batch, Sampler, QiskitRuntimeService
from qiskit_ibm_runtime.fake_provider import FakeManilaV2

def run_batched_random_circuits():
    """
    Generate 6 random quantum circuits (3 qubits, depth 2, with measurement),
    optimize them with a preset pass manager (optimization level 1, backend FakeManilaV2, seed 1),
    partition into 2 batches of 3 circuits each, and execute each batch using Qiskit Runtime's Batch mode
    with the Sampler primitive (seed 42). Return a list of measurement outcomes for each batch.
    """
    # Step 1: Generate 6 random circuits with 3 qubits, depth 2, measurement included, seed=17
    num_circuits = 6
    num_qubits = 3
    depth = 2
    seed_circuit = 17
    circuits = []
    for _ in range(num_circuits):
        qc = random_circuit(num_qubits, depth, measure=True, seed=seed_circuit)
        circuits.append(qc)

    # Step 2: Optimize circuits using preset pass manager with optimization level 1, backend FakeManilaV2, seed=1
    backend = FakeManilaV2()
    pass_manager = generate_preset_pass_manager(optimization_level=1, backend=backend, seed=1)
    optimized_circuits = [pass_manager.run(circ) for circ in circuits]

    # Step 3: Partition circuits into 2 batches of 3 circuits each
    batch_size = 3
    batches = [optimized_circuits[i:i + batch_size] for i in range(0, num_circuits, batch_size)]

    # Step 4: Execute each batch using Qiskit Runtime Sampler with seed=42
    # Initialize Qiskit Runtime service (uses default IBM Quantum account)
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    results_per_batch = []
    for batch_circuits in batches:
        # Create a Batch object with the circuits
        batch = Batch(batch_circuits)
        # Create a Sampler instance with the backend and seed=42
        sampler = Sampler(session=service, backend=backend, seed=42)
        # Run the batch circuits
        job = sampler.run(batch)
        # Wait for job to complete and get results
        result = job.result()
        # Append the result object for this batch
        results_per_batch.append(result)

    return results_per_batch
```

```python
from datetime import datetime, timedelta
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService

def find_recent_jobs():
    """
    Find and return jobs submitted in the last three months using QiskitRuntimeService.

    Returns:
        List of job objects submitted within the last three months.
        Returns an empty list if no jobs found.
    """
    # Initialize the QiskitRuntimeService (assumes IBM Quantum account is already saved)
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Calculate the datetime for three months ago (approximate as 90 days)
    three_months_ago = datetime.utcnow() - timedelta(days=90)

    # Query jobs submitted since three_months_ago
    # The jobs() method returns an iterable of job objects
    recent_jobs = list(service.jobs(start_datetime=three_months_ago))

    return recent_jobs
```

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService

def backend_info():
    """
    Using the QiskitRuntimeService, retrieve the backends that meet the following criteria:
    - They are real quantum devices (not simulators)
    - They are operational
    - They have a minimum of 20 qubits

    Returns:
        List[Dict]: A list of dictionaries, each containing:
            - 'name': backend name (str)
            - 'num_qubits': number of qubits (int)
            - 'instructions': list of supported instruction names (List[str])
        The list is sorted by backend name in ascending order.
    """
    # Initialize the IBM Quantum Runtime service
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Get all backends available to the user
    all_backends = service.backends()

    filtered_backends_info = []

    for backend in all_backends:
        # Filter for real devices (not simulators), operational, and with at least 20 qubits
        if (not backend.configuration().simulator and
            backend.status().operational and
            backend.configuration().n_qubits >= 20):

            # Retrieve backend details
            name = backend.name()
            num_qubits = backend.configuration().n_qubits
            instructions = backend.configuration().basis_gates  # List of supported instruction names

            backend_dict = {
                'name': name,
                'num_qubits': num_qubits,
                'instructions': instructions
            }
            filtered_backends_info.append(backend_dict)

    # Sort the list of dictionaries by backend name
    filtered_backends_info.sort(key=lambda x: x['name'])

    return filtered_backends_info
```

=== [136/151] qiskitHumanEval/135 :: noise_model_with_readouterror ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit_aer.noise import NoiseModel, ReadoutError
def noise_model_with_readouterror():
    """ Construct a noise model with specific readout error properties for different qubits. For qubit 0, a readout of 1 has a 20% probability 
    of being erroneously read as 0, and a readout of 0 has a 30% probability of being erroneously read as 1. For all other qubits, a readout of 1 has a 3% 
    probability of being erroneously read as 0, and a readout of 0 has a 2% probability of being erroneously read as 1.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Plan for implementing the noise model with specified readout errors in Qiskit:

1. **Create a NoiseModel object**
   - Initialize an empty noise model from Qiskit's Aer noise module.

2. **Define readout error matrices for different qubits**
   - For qubit 0:
     - Construct a 2x2 readout error probability matrix:
       - P(0|0) = 0.7 (70% correct read of 0)
       - P(1|0) = 0.3 (30% error: 0 read as 1)
       - P(0|1) = 0.2 (20% error: 1 read as 0)
       - P(1|1) = 0.8 (80% correct read of 1)
   - For all other qubits:
     - Construct a 2x2 readout error probability matrix:
       - P(0|0) = 0.98 (98% correct read of 0)
       - P(1|0) = 0.02 (2% error: 0 read as 1)
       - P(0|1) = 0.03 (3% error: 1 read as 0)
       - P(1|1) = 0.97 (97% correct read of 1)

3. **Create ReadoutError objects**
   - Use the probability matrices to instantiate ReadoutError objects for qubit 0 and for other qubits.

4. **Add readout errors to the noise model**
   - Add the readout error for qubit 0 specifically to qubit index 0.
   - Add the readout error for other qubits to all qubits except 0.
     - This assumes knowledge of the total number of qubits or adding it with a wildcard for all except 0.

5. **Return the constructed NoiseModel object**

**Acceptance Criteria:**
- The noise model includes a custom readout error for qubit 0 matching the specified probabilities.
- All other qubits have a uniform readout error matching the specified lower error probabilities.
- The noise model can be applied to a quantum circuit simulator in Qiskit Aer.
- The function returns a valid NoiseModel object with the described properties.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit_aer.noise import NoiseModel, ReadoutError
import numpy as np

def noise_model_with_readouterror():
    """
    Construct a noise model with specific readout error properties for different qubits.
    For qubit 0:
        - P(0|0) = 0.7, P(1|0) = 0.3
        - P(0|1) = 0.2, P(1|1) = 0.8
    For all other qubits:
        - P(0|0) = 0.98, P(1|0) = 0.02
        - P(0|1) = 0.03, P(1|1) = 0.97
    Returns:
        NoiseModel: Qiskit noise model with the specified readout errors.
    """
    noise_model = NoiseModel()

    # Readout error matrix for qubit 0
    readout_error_qubit0 = np.array([[0.7, 0.3],
                                     [0.2, 0.8]])
    ro_error_0 = ReadoutError(readout_error_qubit0)

    # Readout error matrix for other qubits
    readout_error_others = np.array([[0.98, 0.02],
                                    [0.03, 0.97]])
    ro_error_others = ReadoutError(readout_error_others)

    # Add readout error for qubit 0
    noise_model.add_readout_error(ro_error_0, [0])

    # Add readout error for all other qubits (using wildcard for all qubits except 0)
    # Qiskit noise model does not support direct exclusion, so we add the error to all qubits
    # and then override qubit 0 with its specific error.
    # To do this properly, we add the error to all qubits except 0 by specifying qubits explicitly.
    # Since we don't know total qubit count here, we add the error to all qubits except 0 by using a wildcard '*'
    # and then override qubit 0. But Qiskit noise model does not support exclusion, so we add to all qubits,
    # then override qubit 0. The last added error for a qubit takes precedence.

    # Add readout error for all qubits (wildcard)
    noise_model.add_readout_error(ro_error_others)

    # Override qubit 0 with its specific error again to ensure correct error on qubit 0
    noise_model.add_readout_error(ro_error_0, [0])

    return noise_model
```

I have added comments explaining the approach. Please test this code and let me know if you encounter any issues.

=== [137/151] qiskitHumanEval/136 :: pure_states ===
QiskitDeveloperProxy (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.quantum_info import entropy, random_density_matrix, DensityMatrix
def pure_states(ε):
    """ Return a list of ten density matrices which are pure up to a tolerance of ε.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement the function `pure_states(ε)` that returns a list of ten density matrices which are pure up to tolerance ε, follow this detailed plan:

---

### Goal
Generate 10 density matrices that are "close" to pure states, meaning their purity measure (Tr(ρ²)) is within ε of 1.

---

### Step-by-step Plan

1. **Number of density matrices:**
   - Generate exactly 10 density matrices.

2. **Dimension of density matrices:**
   - Decide on the dimension of the Hilbert space. 
   - For simplicity, use a single qubit system (2x2 density matrices), unless otherwise specified.

3. **Generating pure states:**
   - A pure state density matrix ρ satisfies Tr(ρ²) = 1.
   - Start by generating pure states:
     - Randomly generate 1-qubit pure states (e.g., via random state vectors).
     - Construct density matrices from these state vectors: ρ = |ψ⟩⟨ψ|.

4. **Perturbing pure states to create nearly pure states:**
   - To get density matrices that are close to pure but not perfectly pure (i.e., have purity ≥ 1 - ε), mix the pure state with a small amount of noise.
   - For example, create a mixed state:
     \[
     \rho = (1 - \delta) |\psi\rangle\langle\psi| + \delta \frac{I}{2}
     \]
     where \( I \) is the identity matrix and \( \delta \) is a small mixing parameter chosen so that purity is ≥ 1 - ε.
   - Adjust \(\delta\) accordingly to satisfy the purity tolerance ε.

5. **Check purity and tolerance:**
   - Purity is calculated as Tr(ρ²).
   - Ensure:
     \[
     1 - \text{Tr}(\rho^2) \leq \epsilon
     \]
   - Only include density matrices that meet this criterion.
   - If a randomly generated matrix does not meet the criterion, regenerate or adjust parameters.

6. **Return list:**
   - Return a Python list of 10 density matrices (Qiskit `DensityMatrix` objects or numpy arrays representing density matrices).

---

### Required Quantum Objects and Operations

- **Quantum states:** Random state vectors (e.g., uniform on the Bloch sphere)
- **Density matrices:** Constructed as outer products of state vectors
- **Depolarizing noise or mixing with identity:** To create nearly pure states
- **Purity calculation:** Using Tr(ρ²) to measure closeness to pure state

---

### Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements

- No quantum circuit required here because this is a density matrix generation and verification task.
- Only qubit dimension assumed: 1 qubit (2x2 matrices).

---

### Measurement Strategy

- No measurements are needed since this is a state preparation function (not a circuit execution).

---

### Expected Outcomes

- A list of 10 density matrices, each a 2x2 matrix.
- Each matrix is a valid density matrix (Hermitian, positive semidefinite, trace 1).
- Each matrix satisfies:
  \[
  1 - \text{Tr}(\rho^2) \leq \epsilon
  \]
- Matrices represent states that are "pure up to ε".

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- Output is a list of length 10.
- Each element is a valid density matrix (Hermitian, PSD, trace=1).
- Purity condition holds: for each matrix ρ,
  \[
  1 - \text{Tr}(\rho^2) \leq \epsilon
  \]
- The function handles edge cases where ε is 0 (returns pure states) or close to 1 (allows more mixed states).
- The function uses a consistent approach to generate states (e.g., uniform sampling on the Bloch sphere plus mixing).
- No external dependencies beyond Qiskit’s quantum_info module and standard Python libraries.

---

This plan can guide a Qiskit developer to implement `pure_states(ε)` properly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info import DensityMatrix
import numpy as np

def pure_states(epsilon):
    """
    Return a list of ten 1-qubit density matrices which are pure up to a tolerance of epsilon.
    Each density matrix ρ satisfies: 1 - Tr(ρ²) <= epsilon.
    
    Parameters:
        epsilon (float): Tolerance for purity deviation from 1.
        
    Returns:
        List[DensityMatrix]: List of 10 density matrices close to pure states.
    """
    def random_pure_state():
        # Generate a random pure state vector uniformly on the Bloch sphere
        # Using spherical coordinates: theta in [0, pi], phi in [0, 2pi]
        theta = np.arccos(1 - 2 * np.random.rand())  # cos(theta) uniform in [-1,1]
        phi = 2 * np.pi * np.random.rand()
        # State vector on Bloch sphere
        state = np.array([np.cos(theta / 2), np.exp(1j * phi) * np.sin(theta / 2)])
        return state

    def purity(rho):
        # Calculate purity Tr(rho^2)
        return np.real(np.trace(rho @ rho))

    def mix_with_identity(pure_rho, delta):
        # Mix pure state with maximally mixed state I/2
        dim = pure_rho.shape[0]
        identity = np.eye(dim) / dim
        mixed_rho = (1 - delta) * pure_rho + delta * identity
        return mixed_rho

    density_matrices = []
    dim = 2  # single qubit

    for _ in range(10):
        # Generate a random pure state density matrix
        psi = random_pure_state()
        pure_rho = np.outer(psi, np.conj(psi))  # |psi><psi|

        if epsilon == 0:
            # No mixing allowed, pure state only
            density_matrices.append(DensityMatrix(pure_rho))
            continue

        # Find maximum delta so that purity >= 1 - epsilon
        # Purity of mixed state: Tr(rho^2) = (1 - delta)^2 * 1 + delta^2 * Tr((I/2)^2) + 2*delta*(1-delta)*Tr(pure_rho * I/2)
        # Since Tr((I/2)^2) = Tr(I/4) = 2 * 1/4 = 0.5
        # and Tr(pure_rho * I/2) = Tr(pure_rho)/2 = 1/2
        # So purity = (1 - delta)^2 * 1 + delta^2 * 0.5 + 2 * delta * (1 - delta) * 0.5
        # Simplify:
        # purity = (1 - delta)^2 + 0.5 * delta^2 + delta * (1 - delta)
        # purity = (1 - 2delta + delta^2) + 0.5 delta^2 + delta - delta^2
        # purity = 1 - 2delta + delta^2 + 0.5 delta^2 + delta - delta^2
        # purity = 1 - 2delta + delta^2 + 0.5 delta^2 + delta - delta^2
        # purity = 1 - 2delta + 0.5 delta^2 + delta
        # purity = 1 - delta + 0.5 delta^2

        # We want purity >= 1 - epsilon
        # 1 - delta + 0.5 delta^2 >= 1 - epsilon
        # -delta + 0.5 delta^2 >= -epsilon
        # 0.5 delta^2 - delta + epsilon >= 0
        # Solve quadratic: 0.5 delta^2 - delta + epsilon = 0

        a = 0.5
        b = -1
        c = epsilon

        discriminant = b**2 - 4*a*c
        if discriminant < 0:
            # No real roots, set delta=0 (pure state)
            delta = 0.0
        else:
            root1 = (-b + np.sqrt(discriminant)) / (2 * a)
            root2 = (-b - np.sqrt(discriminant)) / (2 * a)
            # delta must be between 0 and 1, pick the smaller positive root
            candidates = [r for r in [root1, root2] if 0 <= r <= 1]
            if candidates:
                delta = min(candidates)
            else:
                delta = 0.0

        # Mix pure state with identity using delta
        mixed_rho = mix_with_identity(pure_rho, delta)
        density_matrices.append(DensityMatrix(mixed_rho))

    return density_matrices
```

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_density_matrix, entanglement_of_formation
import warnings

def entanglement_dataset(epsilon, target=0.5, dataset_size=100):
    """
    Generate a dataset of 2-qubit density matrices whose entanglement of formation (EoF)
    lies within the range [target - epsilon, target + epsilon].

    Parameters:
    - epsilon (float): Tolerance for EoF values.
    - target (float): Target EoF value (default 0.5).
    - dataset_size (int): Number of density matrices to collect (default 100).

    Returns:
    - dataset (list): List of 2-qubit density matrices (qiskit.quantum_info.DensityMatrix)
                      with EoF within the specified tolerance.
    """
    if epsilon < 0:
        raise ValueError("Epsilon must be a non-negative float.")
    if not (0 <= target <= 1):
        raise ValueError("Target entanglement of formation must be between 0 and 1.")
    if dataset_size <= 0:
        raise ValueError("Dataset size must be a positive integer.")

    dataset = []
    max_attempts = 10000  # To avoid infinite loops
    attempts = 0

    lower_bound = max(0, target - epsilon)
    upper_bound = min(1, target + epsilon)

    while len(dataset) < dataset_size and attempts < max_attempts:
        # Generate a random 2-qubit density matrix (dim=4)
        rho = random_density_matrix(4)
        # Compute entanglement of formation
        eof = entanglement_of_formation(rho)
        # Check if EoF is within tolerance
        if lower_bound <= eof <= upper_bound:
            dataset.append(rho)
        attempts += 1

    if len(dataset) < dataset_size:
        warnings.warn(
            f"Only found {len(dataset)} density matrices within tolerance after {max_attempts} attempts."
        )

    return dataset

# Example usage:
# dataset = entanglement_dataset(0.05)
# print(f"Collected {len(dataset)} density matrices with EoF near 0.5 ± 0.05")

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_density_matrix, entanglement_of_formation
import warnings

def entanglement_dataset(epsilon, target=0.5, dataset_size=100):
    """
    Generate a dataset of 2-qubit density matrices whose entanglement of formation (EoF)
    lies within the range [target - epsilon, target + epsilon].

    Parameters:
    - epsilon (float): Tolerance for EoF values.
    - target (float): Target EoF value (default 0.5).
    - dataset_size (int): Number of density matrices to collect (default 100).

    Returns:
    - dataset (list): List of 2-qubit density matrices (qiskit.quantum_info.DensityMatrix)
                      with EoF within the specified tolerance.
    """
    if epsilon < 0:
        raise ValueError("Epsilon must be a non-negative float.")
    if not (0 <= target <= 1):
        raise ValueError("Target entanglement of formation must be between 0 and 1.")
    if dataset_size <= 0:
        raise ValueError("Dataset size must be a positive integer.")

    dataset = []

    attempts = 0

    lower_bound = max(0, target - epsilon)
    upper_bound = min(1, target + epsilon)

        rho = random_density_matrix(4)

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info import mutual_information, random_density_matrix
from qiskit.quantum_info.states.densitymatrix import DensityMatrix

def mutual_information_dataset(epsilon, num_samples=100):
    """
    Generate a list of 2-qubit density matrices whose mutual information between qubit 0 and qubit 1
    is greater than the given threshold epsilon.

    Parameters:
        epsilon (float): The mutual information threshold.
        num_samples (int): Number of random density matrices to generate (default 100).

    Returns:
        List[DensityMatrix]: List of 2-qubit density matrices with mutual information > epsilon.
    """
    dim = 4  # Dimension for 2 qubits (2^2)
    filtered_states = []

    for _ in range(num_samples):
        # Generate a random 2-qubit density matrix
        rho = random_density_matrix(dim)

        # Compute mutual information between qubit 0 and qubit 1
        # Subsystems are defined by qubit indices [0] and [1]
        mi = mutual_information(rho, [0], [1])

        # Keep density matrices with mutual information greater than epsilon
        if mi > epsilon:
            filtered_states.append(rho)

    return filtered_states
```

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement a function `schmidt_test` that returns the Schmidt decomposition coefficients and subsystem vectors for a given density matrix and a specified subsystem partition in Qiskit, here is a detailed plan:

---

### Task Objective
Given a density matrix (quantum state) and a specification of subsystem qubits (qargs_B), compute the Schmidt decomposition of the state relative to the bipartition defined by qargs_B and its complement.

---

### Step-by-step Implementation Plan

1. **Input Parameters:**
   - `data`: A density matrix representing the quantum state. This can be a `DensityMatrix` object or a numpy array.
   - `qargs_B`: A list of qubit indices defining the subsystem B for the bipartition. The subsystem A is the complement of B within the full system.

2. **Determine the full system dimension and partition:**
   - Identify the total number of qubits `n` in the system from `data`.
   - Define subsystem A qubits as the complement of `qargs_B` in `[0, 1, ..., n-1]`.

3. **Rearrange the density matrix to group subsystem A and B:**
   - The density matrix is defined on the full system; for Schmidt decomposition, the state vector (for pure states) or purification (for mixed states) needs to be reshaped and bipartitioned.
   - If `data` is a density matrix of a pure state |ψ⟩, convert it to the state vector.
   - If the state is mixed, the Schmidt decomposition is not strictly defined; consider purification or restrict to pure states.

4. **Schmidt decomposition:**
   - Use `qiskit.quantum_info.schmidt_decomposition` which takes a state vector and a list of subsystem qubits for partition.
   - The function returns:
     - Schmidt coefficients (non-negative real numbers)
     - Vectors corresponding to subsystem A
     - Vectors corresponding to subsystem B
   - Note: `schmidt_decomposition` works for pure states (state vectors), so ensure the input is a state vector.

5. **Handling density matrices:**
   - If the input is a density matrix of a pure state, convert it to a state vector by extracting the eigenvector with eigenvalue 1.
   - If it is mixed, return an error or warning (Schmidt decomposition is not applicable).

6. **Return values:**
   - Return the Schmidt coefficients and the subsystem vectors as output.

---

### Circuit Structure and Qubit Requirements

- No quantum circuit is directly required for this function since it is an analysis tool on quantum states represented classically.
- The function operates on the quantum state's classical representation.

---

### Measurement Strategy

- Not applicable for this function as it performs state analysis, not circuit execution.

---

### Expected Outcomes

- For a valid pure state input:
  - Output a tuple containing Schmidt coefficients (list of floats)
  - Subsystem A vectors (list of state vectors)
  - Subsystem B vectors (list of state vectors)
- For invalid inputs (mixed states), raise an informative error.

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- The function correctly identifies the subsystem A and B based on `qargs_B`.
- For pure state density matrices, the function extracts the state vector and computes the Schmidt decomposition.
- The function returns the correct Schmidt coefficients and subsystem vectors.
- The function raises an error or handles gracefully if input is a mixed state.

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info import DensityMatrix, Statevector, schmidt_decomposition
import numpy as np

def schmidt_test(data, qargs_B):
    """
    Return the Schmidt decomposition coefficients and subsystem vectors for the given density matrix and partition.

    Parameters:
        data (DensityMatrix or np.ndarray): Density matrix representing the quantum state.
        qargs_B (list[int]): List of qubit indices defining subsystem B.

    Returns:
        tuple:
            - schmidt_coeffs (list[float]): Schmidt decomposition coefficients (non-negative real numbers).
            - subsystem_A_vectors (list[Statevector]): List of state vectors for subsystem A.
            - subsystem_B_vectors (list[Statevector]): List of state vectors for subsystem B.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If the input state is mixed (not a pure state).
    """
    # Convert input to DensityMatrix if it is a numpy array
    if isinstance(data, np.ndarray):
        dm = DensityMatrix(data)
    elif isinstance(data, DensityMatrix):
        dm = data
    else:
        raise TypeError("Input data must be a DensityMatrix or a numpy ndarray representing a density matrix.")

    # Check if the state is pure by verifying if Tr(rho^2) == 1 within numerical tolerance
    purity = np.real(np.trace(dm.data @ dm.data))
    if not np.isclose(purity, 1.0, atol=1e-10):
        raise ValueError("Input state is mixed; Schmidt decomposition is only defined for pure states.")

    # Extract the state vector from the density matrix (eigenvector with eigenvalue 1)
    eigvals, eigvecs = np.linalg.eigh(dm.data)
    # Find eigenvector corresponding to eigenvalue close to 1
    idx = np.argmax(eigvals)
    if not np.isclose(eigvals[idx], 1.0, atol=1e-10):
        raise ValueError("No eigenvalue equal to 1 found; input state is not pure.")
    state_vector = eigvecs[:, idx]

    # Convert to Statevector object
    sv = Statevector(state_vector)

    # Determine total number of qubits
    num_qubits = int(np.log2(len(state_vector)))

    # Define subsystem A as complement of qargs_B
    qargs_B_set = set(qargs_B)
    qargs_A = [i for i in range(num_qubits) if i not in qargs_B_set]

    # Perform Schmidt decomposition using Qiskit's function
    schmidt_coeffs, subsystem_A_vecs, subsystem_B_vecs = schmidt_decomposition(sv, qargs_B)

    return schmidt_coeffs, subsystem_A_vecs, subsystem_B_vecs
```

    """ Return a list of ten probability vectors each of length 16 whose shannon entropy is greater than a given value.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement the function `shannon_entropy_data(ε)` that returns a list of ten probability vectors each of length 16 whose Shannon entropy is greater than ε, here is a detailed plan:

---

### Implementation Plan

1. **Understanding the task:**

   - Input: A scalar threshold value ε (epsilon).
   - Output: A list containing 10 probability vectors (each of length 16).
   - Each probability vector represents a valid probability distribution (elements >= 0 and sum to 1).
   - Each vector must have Shannon entropy > ε.

2. **Required components and operations:**

   - Use or mimic Shannon entropy calculation (e.g., via `qiskit.quantum_info.shannon_entropy` or implement manually).
   - Generate random probability vectors of length 16.
   - Check if their Shannon entropy is greater than ε.
   - Collect 10 such vectors.

3. **Step-by-step plan:**

   1. **Initialize an empty list** to store the probability vectors.

   2. **Loop until the list contains 10 vectors:**

      - Generate a random vector `v` of length 16 with non-negative entries. For example, sample 16 random numbers from a uniform distribution.

      - Normalize the vector so that it sums to 1, making it a valid probability vector.

      - Compute the Shannon entropy of `v`.

      - If the entropy > ε, append `v` to the list.

   3. **Return the list** of 10 probability vectors.

4. **Additional notes:**

   - The Shannon entropy for a probability vector \( p = (p_1, ..., p_{16}) \) is defined as:

     \[
     H(p) = -\sum_{i=1}^{16} p_i \log_2(p_i)
     \]

     where terms with \( p_i=0 \) are treated as zero contribution.

   - The maximum entropy for a length-16 distribution is \(\log_2(16) = 4\).

   - ε should be between 0 and 4; if ε is too close to 4, generating such vectors may take longer.

5. **Acceptance criteria:**

   - The returned list has exactly 10 vectors.

   - Each vector is a list or numpy array of length 16.

   - Each vector sums to 1 with a small numerical tolerance (e.g., 1e-8).

   - Each element in the vector is ≥ 0.

   - Each vector's Shannon entropy is strictly greater than ε.

---

This plan allows a Qiskit developer to implement the function efficiently and correctly.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.quantum_info import shannon_entropy
import numpy as np

def shannon_entropy_data(epsilon):
    """
    Return a list of ten probability vectors each of length 16 whose Shannon entropy is greater than epsilon.

    Parameters:
        epsilon (float): The entropy threshold.

    Returns:
        List[np.ndarray]: List of 10 probability vectors with entropy > epsilon.
    """
    vectors = []
    dim = 16

    while len(vectors) < 10:
        # Generate a random probability vector of length 16
        vec = np.random.rand(dim)
        vec /= vec.sum()  # Normalize to sum to 1

        # Compute Shannon entropy using qiskit function
        entropy = shannon_entropy(vec, base=2)

        # Check if entropy is greater than epsilon
        if entropy > epsilon:
            vectors.append(vec)

    return vectors

# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    epsilon = 2.0
    prob_vectors = shannon_entropy_data(epsilon)
    for i, vec in enumerate(prob_vectors):
        print(f"Vector {i+1}:")
        print(vec)
        print(f"Sum: {vec.sum()}, Entropy: {shannon_entropy(vec, base=2)}\n")

from qiskit.quantum_info import shannon_entropy
import numpy as np

def shannon_entropy_data(epsilon):
    """
    Return a list of ten probability vectors each of length 16 whose Shannon entropy is greater than epsilon.

    Parameters:
        epsilon (float): The entropy threshold.

    Returns:
        List[np.ndarray]: List of 10 probability vectors with entropy > epsilon.
    """
    vectors = []
    dim = 16

    while len(vectors) < 10:
        # Generate a random probability vector of length 16
        vec = np.random.rand(dim)
        vec /= vec.sum()  # Normalize to sum to 1

        # Compute Shannon entropy using qiskit function
        entropy = shannon_entropy(vec, base=2)

        # Check if entropy is greater than epsilon
        if entropy > epsilon:
            vectors.append(vec)

    return vectors

# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    epsilon = 2.0
    prob_vectors = shannon_entropy_data(epsilon)
    for i, vec in enumerate(prob_vectors):
        print(f"Vector {i+1}:")
        print(vec)
        print(f"Sum: {vec.sum()}, Entropy: {shannon_entropy(vec, base=2)}\n")

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_pauli
import numpy as np
def anticommutators(pauli: SparsePauliOp):
    """ Return a list of ten anticommutators for the given pauli.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

Here is a detailed plan to implement the function `anticommutators` that returns a list of ten anticommutators for a given `SparsePauliOp` object:

---

### Implementation Plan for `anticommutators(pauli: SparsePauliOp)`

#### Objective:
Given a `SparsePauliOp` representing a Pauli operator, generate and return a list of 10 distinct anticommutators formed by `pauli` and other Pauli operators.

---

### 1. Inputs and Outputs

- **Input:** 
  - `pauli`: a `SparsePauliOp` instance representing a Pauli operator or sum of Paulis acting on `n` qubits.
  
- **Output:** 
  - A list of 10 `SparsePauliOp` objects, each corresponding to an anticommutator of `pauli` with another Pauli operator.
  
---

### 2. Key Concepts

- The **anticommutator** of two operators \( A \) and \( B \) is defined as:
  \[
  \{A, B\} = AB + BA
  \]
  
- The function must generate 10 anticommutators of the form:
  \[
  \{pauli, Q_i\}
  \]
  where each \( Q_i \) is another Pauli operator (single or multi-qubit).

- Since `pauli` is a `SparsePauliOp`, we can use Qiskit's operator arithmetic to compute products and sums.

---

### 3. Step-by-Step Implementation Plan

#### Step 1: Determine the number of qubits `n`
- Extract the number of qubits from the input `pauli` operator.
- This is necessary to generate compatible Pauli operators on the same number of qubits.

#### Step 2: Generate 10 distinct Pauli operators \( Q_i \)
- For each \( i \in \{1, \ldots, 10\} \):
  - Generate a random Pauli operator acting on \( n \) qubits.
  - Use Qiskit's `random_pauli(n)` to generate these operators.
  - Ensure that each \( Q_i \) is distinct from each other and from the input `pauli` to get meaningful anticommutators.

#### Step 3: Compute anticommutators \(\{pauli, Q_i\}\)
- For each \( Q_i \):
  - Compute the product \( pauli \times Q_i \) using `pauli.compose(Q_i)`
  - Compute the product \( Q_i \times pauli \) using `Q_i.compose(pauli)`
  - Sum these two operators to get the anticommutator:
    \[
    \{pauli, Q_i\} = pauli \times Q_i + Q_i \times pauli
    \]
    
- Store each anticommutator in a list.

#### Step 4: Return the list of anticommutators
- After generating all 10 anticommutators, return the list.

---

### 4. Additional Notes

- Each anticommutator will be a `SparsePauliOp` object.
- The function should not modify the input `pauli`.
- Optionally, ensure numerical stability or normalization if needed.
- The random generation ensures diversity in the anticommutators.
- No quantum circuit or measurement is required here since this is purely operator algebra.

---

### 5. Acceptance Criteria

- The function accepts a `SparsePauliOp` acting on \( n \) qubits.
- It returns a list of exactly 10 `SparsePauliOp` objects.
- Each element in the list is the anticommutator of the input `pauli` with a distinct randomly generated Pauli \( Q_i \).
- The anticommutators are correctly computed as \( pauli \times Q_i + Q_i \times pauli \).
- The function executes without errors and returns meaningful operator results.

---

This plan provides a clear roadmap for implementing the `anticommutators` function using Qiskit's `SparsePauliOp` capabilities and the `random_pauli` utility.

from qiskit.quantum_info import SparsePauliOp, random_pauli
from typing import List

def anticommutators(pauli: SparsePauliOp) -> List[SparsePauliOp]:
    """ Return a list of ten anticommutators for the given pauli.
    
    Each anticommutator is of the form {pauli, Q_i} = pauli*Q_i + Q_i*pauli,
    where Q_i is a distinct random Pauli operator on the same number of qubits.
    
    Args:
        pauli (SparsePauliOp): Input Pauli operator.
        
    Returns:
        List[SparsePauliOp]: List of 10 anticommutators as SparsePauliOp objects.
    """
    n = pauli.num_qubits  # Number of qubits
    
    anticommutators_list = []
    generated_paulis = set()
    
    # Convert input pauli to string to avoid duplicates
    pauli_str = pauli.to_labels()[0]
    generated_paulis.add(pauli_str)
    
    while len(anticommutators_list) < 10:
        # Generate a random Pauli operator on n qubits
        Q = random_pauli(n)
        Q_str = Q.to_labels()[0]
        
        # Ensure Q is distinct from previously generated Paulis
        if Q_str in generated_paulis:
            continue
        
        generated_paulis.add(Q_str)
        
        # Compute anticommutator: pauli*Q + Q*pauli
        pq = pauli.compose(Q)
        qp = Q.compose(pauli)
        anticom = pq + qp
        
        anticommutators_list.append(anticom)
    
    return anticommutators_list

# Test code to verify function runs without error
if __name__ == "__main__":
    from qiskit.quantum_info import Pauli
    # Example: single-qubit Pauli X operator
    pauli_op = SparsePauliOp(Pauli("X"))
    result = anticommutators(pauli_op)
    for i, op in enumerate(result, 1):
        print(f"Anticommutator {i}:")
        print(op)

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_density_matrix, purity, DensityMatrix

def purity_dataset():
    """ Return a list of 10 single qubit density matrices whose purity is greater than 0.5.
    """
    result = []
    while len(result) < 10:
        # Generate a random single-qubit density matrix
        rho = random_density_matrix(2)
        # Calculate purity
        p = purity(rho)
        # Check if purity is greater than 0.5
        if p > 0.5:
            # Convert to DensityMatrix object if not already
            dm = DensityMatrix(rho)
            result.append(dm)
    return result

# Example usage and test
if __name__ == "__main__":
    dataset = purity_dataset()
    print(f"Generated {len(dataset)} density matrices with purity > 0.5")
    for i, dm in enumerate(dataset):
        print(f"Density matrix {i+1} purity: {purity(dm):.4f}")

    """ Return a list of ten pairs of one qubit state vectors whose state fidelity is greater than 0.9.

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To implement the function `fidelity_dataset()` that returns a list of ten pairs of one-qubit state vectors with state fidelity greater than 0.9, here is a detailed plan:

---

### Task Breakdown and Implementation Plan

**Goal:**  
Generate 10 pairs of one-qubit state vectors where each pair has a state fidelity greater than 0.9.

---

### 1. Qubit Requirements

- Use a single qubit state vector for each element.  
- Each pair consists of two one-qubit state vectors.

---

### 2. State Vector Generation

- Generate random one-qubit state vectors using a random state vector generator (e.g., `random_statevector(2)` from Qiskit which generates a random state in a 2-dimensional Hilbert space).  
- To ensure fidelity > 0.9, the two states in each pair must be close to each other.

**Strategy to ensure fidelity > 0.9:**  
- Generate the first state vector randomly.  
- For the second state vector, generate a vector that is a small perturbation of the first state, so the fidelity remains high.  
- Alternatively, generate random states until a suitable pair with fidelity > 0.9 is found.

---

### 3. Fidelity Calculation

- Use `state_fidelity` function from Qiskit to calculate fidelity between two state vectors.  
- Fidelity threshold: > 0.9.

---

### 4. Loop and Collection

- Initialize an empty list to store pairs.  
- Loop until 10 pairs are found that satisfy fidelity > 0.9.  
- For each iteration:  
  - Generate or perturb states as above.  
  - Compute fidelity.  
  - If fidelity > 0.9, append the pair to the list.

---

### 5. Output Format

- Return a list of 10 tuples.  
- Each tuple contains two `Statevector` objects representing one-qubit states.  
- Example: `[(sv1a, sv1b), (sv2a, sv2b), ..., (sv10a, sv10b)]`

---

### Acceptance Criteria

- The function returns exactly 10 pairs.  
- Each pair consists of two one-qubit `Statevector` instances.  
- The fidelity between the two state vectors in each pair is strictly greater than 0.9.  
- The function does not run indefinitely (i.e., it should be efficient).

---

### Summary of Implementation Steps for Qiskit Developer

1. Import necessary modules: `random_statevector`, `state_fidelity`, `Statevector` from `qiskit.quantum_info`.  
2. Initialize an empty list `pairs`.  
3. While the length of `pairs` < 10:  
   a. Generate a random one-qubit state vector `sv1`.  
   b. Generate a second state vector `sv2` that is either a small perturbation of `sv1` or generate random state vectors until fidelity > 0.9.  
   c. Calculate fidelity between `sv1` and `sv2`.  
   d. If fidelity > 0.9, append `(sv1, sv2)` to `pairs`.  
4. Return the list `pairs`.

---

This plan can be straightforwardly translated into Qiskit code by a developer.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_statevector, state_fidelity, Statevector
import numpy as np

def fidelity_dataset():
    """ Return a list of ten pairs of one qubit state vectors whose state fidelity is greater than 0.9.
    """
    pairs = []
    while len(pairs) < 10:
        # Generate a random one-qubit state vector
        sv1 = random_statevector(2)
        
        # Create a small perturbation to sv1 to get sv2
        # We apply a small rotation around the Y axis to sv1 to get sv2
        # This ensures sv2 is close to sv1 and fidelity > 0.9
        theta = np.random.uniform(0, 0.3)  # small angle for rotation
        # Define the rotation matrix Ry(theta)
        ry = np.array([[np.cos(theta/2), -np.sin(theta/2)],
                       [np.sin(theta/2),  np.cos(theta/2)]])
        
        # Apply rotation to sv1's data vector
        sv2_data = ry @ sv1.data
        
        # Normalize sv2_data to ensure it's a valid statevector
        sv2_data /= np.linalg.norm(sv2_data)
        
        sv2 = Statevector(sv2_data)
        
        # Calculate fidelity
        fidelity = state_fidelity(sv1, sv2)
        
        if fidelity > 0.9:
            pairs.append((sv1, sv2))
    
    return pairs

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_statevector, state_fidelity, Statevector
import numpy as np

def fidelity_dataset():
    """ Return a list of ten pairs of one qubit state vectors whose state fidelity is greater than 0.9.
    """
    pairs = []
    while len(pairs) < 10:
        # Generate a random one-qubit state vector
        sv1 = random_statevector(2)
        
        # Create a small perturbation to sv1 to get sv2
        # We apply a small rotation around the Y axis to sv1 to get sv2
        # This ensures sv2 is close to sv1 and fidelity > 0.9
        theta = np.random.uniform(0, 0.3)  # small angle for rotation
        # Define the rotation matrix Ry(theta)
        ry = np.array([[np.cos(theta/2), -np.sin(theta/2)],
                       [np.sin(theta/2),  np.cos(theta/2)]])
        
        # Apply rotation to sv1's data vector
        sv2_data = ry @ sv1.data
        
        # Normalize sv2_data to ensure it's a valid statevector
        sv2_data /= np.linalg.norm(sv2_data)
        
        sv2 = Statevector(sv2_data)

            pairs.append((sv1, sv2))

from qiskit.quantum_info import random_density_matrix, concurrence, DensityMatrix
def concurrence_dataset():
    """ Return a list of 10 density matrices whose concurrence is 0.
    """

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: PlannerAgent

PlannerAgent (to chat_manager):

To design a function that returns a list of 10 density matrices each having a concurrence of 0, here is a detailed implementation plan:

1. **Qubit and System Setup:**
   - Each density matrix should represent a two-qubit system because concurrence is defined for two-qubit states.
   - Therefore, each density matrix will be a 4x4 complex matrix (dimension 2^2 = 4).

2. **Generating Density Matrices with Zero Concurrence:**
   - Concurrence measures the entanglement of a two-qubit state. Zero concurrence means the state is separable (not entangled).
   - To guarantee zero concurrence, generate density matrices corresponding to separable states.
   - Approaches:
     - Construct pure product states (|ψ⟩ = |a⟩ ⊗ |b⟩) and convert to density matrices.
     - Or generate mixed states that are convex combinations of product states.
   - Since the `random_density_matrix` function from Qiskit returns random density matrices (often entangled), avoid using it directly for the zero concurrence requirement.
   - Instead, create density matrices explicitly from tensor products of single-qubit states.

3. **Implementation Steps:**
   - For each of the 10 density matrices:
     - Generate two random single-qubit pure states (2x1 complex vectors normalized to 1).
     - Form the tensor product (Kronecker product) of these two states to get a two-qubit state vector.
     - Convert the two-qubit state vector to a density matrix by outer product: ρ = |ψ⟩⟨ψ|.
   - This ensures that each density matrix corresponds to a pure separable state with zero concurrence.

4. **Measurement Strategy:**
   - No measurement is needed within this function as it only returns density matrices.
   - Validation of zero concurrence can be done outside the function by calculating concurrence on each returned density matrix.

5. **Expected Outcomes:**
   - A list of 10 DensityMatrix objects representing two-qubit pure separable states.
   - Each density matrix should have concurrence exactly 0.

6. **Acceptance Criteria:**
   - The function returns a list of length 10.
   - Each element is a DensityMatrix object corresponding to a two-qubit system.
   - Concurrence computed for each density matrix is 0 (or numerically very close to 0 within floating-point tolerance).
   - The density matrices are valid (Hermitian, positive semi-definite, trace = 1).

Summary plan for Qiskit developer:

1. Initialize an empty list to store density matrices.
2. Loop 10 times:
   - Generate two random single-qubit pure states (e.g., random complex 2-vectors normalized).
   - Compute their tensor product to get a two-qubit pure state vector.
   - Create a DensityMatrix object from this vector.
   - Append the DensityMatrix to the list.
3. Return the list of 10 density matrices.

This approach guarantees zero concurrence by construction, fulfilling the function’s contract.

--------------------------------------------------------------------------------

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.quantum_info import DensityMatrix
import numpy as np

def concurrence_dataset():
    """ Return a list of 10 two-qubit density matrices whose concurrence is 0.
    
    Each density matrix corresponds to a pure separable state constructed as a tensor product
    of two random single-qubit pure states.
    """
    density_matrices = []
    for _ in range(10):
        # Generate random single-qubit pure state |a>
        a = np.random.randn(2) + 1j * np.random.randn(2)
        a /= np.linalg.norm(a)  # Normalize
        
        # Generate random single-qubit pure state |b>
        b = np.random.randn(2) + 1j * np.random.randn(2)
        b /= np.linalg.norm(b)  # Normalize
        
        # Tensor product |ψ> = |a> ⊗ |b>
        psi = np.kron(a, b)
        
        # Create density matrix ρ = |ψ><ψ|
        rho = DensityMatrix(psi)
        
        density_matrices.append(rho)
    
    return density_matrices
```

Plan to implement the function qft_inverse(n: int) that returns the inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit for n qubits:

1. Initialize a quantum circuit with n qubits.
   - The circuit should have n qubits because QFT and its inverse operate on n qubits.

2. Use the existing QFT circuit from Qiskit's circuit library.
   - Import the QFT circuit for n qubits.
   - The QFT circuit implements the forward quantum Fourier transform.

3. Invert the QFT circuit to obtain the inverse QFT.
   - Use the inverse() method of the QFT circuit object to get the inverse QFT.
   - This inversion reverses the order of gates and applies adjoints (daggers) to them.

4. Return the inverted QFT circuit.
   - The returned circuit will implement the inverse QFT on n qubits.

Additional considerations:
- The returned circuit should not include measurements because QFT and its inverse are unitary transformations.
- The function should properly handle edge cases such as n=0 or negative values by raising errors or returning empty circuits as appropriate.

Acceptance criteria:
- The function returns a QuantumCircuit object with n qubits.
- The returned circuit corresponds to the inverse QFT, i.e., it is the adjoint of the forward QFT.
- The circuit does not contain measurements.
- The function works correctly for typical values of n (e.g., n=1, 2, 3, ...).

This plan provides a clear and efficient way to implement qft_inverse leveraging Qiskit's existing QFT circuit.

from qiskit.circuit.library import QFT
from qiskit import QuantumCircuit

def qft_inverse(n: int) -> QuantumCircuit:
    """Return the inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (QFT) circuit for n qubits.

    Args:
        n (int): Number of qubits.

    Returns:
        QuantumCircuit: The inverse QFT circuit on n qubits.

    Raises:
        ValueError: If n is negative.
    """
    if n < 0:
        raise ValueError("Number of qubits must be non-negative")
    if n == 0:
        # Return an empty circuit with 0 qubits
        return QuantumCircuit(0)

    # Create the forward QFT circuit
    qft_circuit = QFT(num_qubits=n, do_swaps=False)

    # Invert the QFT circuit to get the inverse QFT
    inverse_qft_circuit = qft_circuit.inverse()

    return inverse_qft_circuit

# Example usage:
# qc = qft_inverse(3)
# print(qc.draw())

   - Add the `TrivialLayout()` pass to this PassManager; this pass maps virtual qubits to physical qubits in order.

4. **Create a StagedPassManager**
   - Instantiate a `StagedPassManager`.
   - Set the layout stage of this StagedPassManager to the PassManager created with `TrivialLayout()`.

5. **Return the StagedPassManager**

- **Circuit Structure:** No circuit involved; this is preparation of transpiler passes.

Next speaker: QiskitDeveloper

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler import PassManager, StagedPassManager
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService
from qiskit.transpiler.passes.layout.trivial_layout import TrivialLayout
from qiskit.providers.ibmq import least_busy

def trivial_layout() -> StagedPassManager:
    """
    Generate a StagedPassManager with a trivial layout pass set for the least busy IBM Quantum backend.

    Returns:
        StagedPassManager: A staged pass manager with the layout stage set to a PassManager
                           containing the TrivialLayout pass.
    """
    # Initialize IBM Quantum Runtime service (requires IBM Quantum account credentials configured)
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Filter backends that are operational and support at least 1 qubit
    backends = service.backends(filters=lambda b: b.configuration().n_qubits >= 1 and b.status().operational)

    # Select the least busy backend from the filtered list
    backend = least_busy(backends)

    # Create a PassManager and add the TrivialLayout pass
    pass_manager = PassManager()
    pass_manager.append(TrivialLayout())

    # Create a StagedPassManager and set the layout stage to the PassManager with TrivialLayout
    staged_pass_manager = StagedPassManager()
    staged_pass_manager.set_layout(pass_manager)

    return staged_pass_manager
```

QiskitDeveloper (to chat_manager):

```python
from qiskit.transpiler import PassManager, StagedPassManager
from qiskit_ibm_runtime import QiskitRuntimeService
from qiskit.transpiler.passes.layout.trivial_layout import TrivialLayout

def trivial_layout() -> StagedPassManager:
    """
    Generate a StagedPassManager with a trivial layout pass set for the least busy IBM Quantum backend.

    Returns:
        StagedPassManager: A staged pass manager with the layout stage set to a PassManager
                           containing the TrivialLayout pass.
    """
    # Initialize IBM Quantum Runtime service (requires IBM Quantum account credentials configured)
    service = QiskitRuntimeService()

    # Filter backends that are operational and support at least 1 qubit
    backends = service.backends(filters=lambda b: b.configuration().n_qubits >= 1 and b.status().operational)
```

```python
    # Manually find the least busy backend by comparing queue lengths
    least_busy_backend = None
    min_queue = float('inf')
    for backend in backends:
        try:
            queue_length = backend.status().pending_jobs
            if queue_length < min_queue:
                min_queue = queue_length
                least_busy_backend = backend
        except Exception:
            # If status or pending_jobs is not available, skip this backend
            continue

    if least_busy_backend is None:
        raise RuntimeError("No suitable backend found.")

    # Create a PassManager and add the TrivialLayout pass
    pass_manager = PassManager()
    pass_manager.append(TrivialLayout())

    # Create a StagedPassManager and set the layout stage to the PassManager with TrivialLayout
    staged_pass_manager = StagedPassManager()
    staged_pass_manager.set_layout(pass_manager)

    return staged_pass_manager
```

   - No measurement is required within this function; measurement can be added externally if needed.
